##### **General Introduction to the Handbook**

This handbook aims to offer a rigorous, scientifically grounded and richly exemplified
treatment of the principles, methodologies and applications of game design. The goal
is to provide students, researchers, aspiring designers and professionals in the sector
with a complete and in-depth tool to understand the complexity inherent in designing
interactive play experiences. This expanded edition integrates updated contributions
from different disciplines such as cognitive and behavioral psychology, computer
science and artificial intelligence, narratology and digital media studies, humanmachine interaction (HCI), cognitive science, behavioral economics and sociology of
virtual communities. Each chapter will address specific issues with an analytical
approach, supported by consolidated theoretical models, an increased number of
references to scientific and professional literature, and illustrated by concrete
examples taken from a wide range of video games, classic and contemporary, to
facilitate the understanding and practical application of the concepts exposed. The
aim is to create a text that is both theoretically sound and operationally useful,
promoting evidence-based, ethically conscious and constantly innovation-oriented
game design.

# **Chapter 1**
### **Epistemological** **Foundations** **and Game Design** **Methodologies:** **A systemic approach** **and Interdisciplinary**


_Video games are the most significant art form of the twenty-first century._

_**-Chris Crawford-**_


11


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


This inaugural chapter aims to establish the conceptual, historical and
methodological foundations for a scientific, critical and reflective approach to game
design. We will begin with an in-depth exploration of the evolution of the definitions
of "game" and "game design", analyzing the seminal contributions of classical
thinkers such as Huizinga and Caillois, and more contemporary figures such as Suits,
and then contextualize and enrich these perspectives in light of the digital age and
current debates in Game Studies. Subsequently, the game will be dissected as a
complex and interactive system, breaking it down into its fundamental structural
elements: players, rules, objectives, play space, mechanics, dynamics, aesthetics
and the socio-cultural context and illustrating each component with a rich range of
examples taken from iconic and innovative video games. The intrinsically hybrid and
interdisciplinary nature of game design will then be discussed in greater detail,
highlighting how it represents a dynamic synthesis between scientific rigor, artistic
vision, technical ingenuity and deep humanistic understanding. Advanced research
methodologies will be introduced: qualitative (such as virtual ethnography and
phenomenological analysis of the game experience), quantitative (including the use
of biometrics and advanced game analytics), mixed-method approaches and the
Design Based Research paradigm – as essential tools for informed and evidencebased design. A more detailed historical excursus will trace the evolution of game
design thought and practice, from its roots in traditional game forms to the impact of
technological revolutions (arcade, home console, PC, internet, mobile, VR/AR) and
the lessons learned from the pioneering designers and schools of thought that
shaped the medium. Finally, the chapter will address with a critical and in-depth
analysis the essential ethical issues and the growing social responsibility that every
scientifically aware game designer must face, reflecting on the complex psychological
implications (e.g. the "dark patterns" and the risk of problematic behaviors), cultural
(the representation of identities and values, the perpetuation or challenge of
stereotypes) and social (the formation of online communities, the impact of games
on public health and civil discourse) of the creation of pervasive and powerful
interactive experiences.


**1.1. Evolutionary Definitions of "Game" and "Game Design":**
**From Huizinga, Caillois and Suits to the Digital Age and**
**Contemporary Game Studies**

The search for an unambiguous and all-encompassing definition of "game" has
engaged philosophers, sociologists, psychologists and media theorists for centuries,
proving to be a task as fascinating as it is elusive. As famously observed by Ludwig
Wittgenstein (1953) in his "Philosophical Investigations", the concept of "play" serves
as a paradigmatic example of a category defined by "family similarities" rather than
by a fixed set of necessary and sufficient properties. Despite this intrinsic fluidity,
some fundamental definitions have profoundly influenced our understanding of the
gaming phenomenon and, consequently, the practice of game design.
Dutch historian Johan Huizinga's seminal work (1938), "Homo Ludens: A Study of
the Play Element in Culture," remains an inescapable starting point. Huizinga defines
play as "a free action, conscious of not being 'serious' and located outside ordinary
life, capable nevertheless of totally absorbing the player; an action devoid of any
material interest and from which no profit is derived; which is accomplished within a
definite space and time, according to given rules and in an orderly manner; and which


12


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


gives rise to social relations in life that willingly surround themselves with mystery or
accentuate, through disguise, their difference from the usual world" (Huizinga,
1938/1949, p. 13). Key elements of its definition include the freedom of the playful
act, its separation from everyday life through the concept of the "magic circle" – a
dedicated space-time with its own laws, exemplified by the arena of an esport such
as _Dota 2_ (Valve, 2013) or the narrative universe of _Red Dead Redemption 2_
(Rockstar Games, 2018) – the uncertainty of the outcome that generates tension, its
unproductive or autotelic nature (although this has been complicated by the advent
of esports and "play to earn" models), the presence of voluntarily accepted rules, and
the creation of an alternative fiction or world.
The French sociologist Roger Caillois (1958), in his influential "Les jeux et les
hommes (Le masque et le vertige)", takes up and refines Huizinga's ideas, agreeing
on the characteristics of freedom, separateness, uncertainty, unproductivity,
regulation and fiction. Caillois, however, proposes a more structured taxonomy of
forms of play, based on four fundamental categories of primary playful attitudes or
drives. The first is Agôn (Competition), which represents the tendency towards rivalry
and skill, typical of sports and many strategy or skill games such as _Street Fighter II_
(Capcom, 1991) or _StarCraft II_ (Blizzard Entertainment, 2010). This is followed by
Alea (Chance/Luck), which describes games dominated by fate, where the player's
will plays a minimal role, as in the "loot box" mechanics of _Genshin Impact_ (miHoYo,
2020). The third category is Mimicry (Simulacrum/Imitation/Role-Playing Game),
which refers to the pleasure of taking on a fictional role, such as in RPGs such as
_Cyberpunk 2077_ (CD Projekt Red, 2020) or life simulation games such as _Animal_
_Crossing: New Horizons_ (Nintendo, 2020). Finally, Ilinx (Vertigo/Trance)
encompasses games that try to alter ordinary perception, inducing states of vertigo
or euphoria, as in the high-speed experiences of _Burnout Paradise_ (Criterion Games,
2008) or in some VR games such as _Beat Saber_ (Beat Games, 2018). Caillois,
moreover, places these categories along an axis that goes from paidia (free and
improvised play) to ludus (structured and regulated game). Many complex video
games, such as _Minecraft_ (Mojang Studios, 2011), offer a balance between these
two poles, with Survival mode embodying _ludus_ and Creative mode allowing for a full
expression of _paidia_ .
Another crucial philosophical definition, particularly relevant to game design, is that
of Bernard Suits (1978), set out in his book "The Grasshopper: Games, Life and
Utopia". Suits defines play as "the voluntary endeavor of overcoming unnecessary
obstacles" (p. 55). This definition emphasizes the concept of "lusory attitude": the
voluntary acceptance by the player of a set of rules that make a task (the lusory goal)
more difficult than it would otherwise be, in order to make the play activity itself
possible (the lusory goal). In video games, this is evident in _QWOP_ (Bennett Foddy,
2008), where the simple act of running is made extremely difficult, turning a mundane
task into a challenge. Even in a platformer like _Super Mario Odyssey_ (Nintendo,
2017), the rules of jumping and movement create the "unnecessary obstacles" to
reach the end of a level.
In the specific field of videogame game design, Chris Crawford (1984), in his
influential "The Art of Computer Game Design", proposed that a game be
characterized by four factors: the representation as a closed and formal system; the
meaningful interaction between player and system; the conflict that hinders the
achievement of objectives; and security, i.e. consequences of failure less serious
than in real life. how to lose a life in _Pac Man_ (Namco, 1980).
Katie Salen Tekinbaş and Eric Zimmerman (2004), in their seminal text "Rules of
Play: Game Design Fundamentals", offer a modern and widely adopted synthesis,
defining play as "a system in which players engage in an artificial, rules-defined
conflict involving a quantifiable outcome" (p. 80). This definition emphasizes the
systemic nature of play, the importance of structured conflict, and the presence of a


13


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


measurable outcome.
In light of these perspectives, game design can be defined in a more comprehensive
and scientifically oriented way as the discipline and professional practice that deals
with the conception, formal specification, prototyping, iterative testing, and
supervision of game creation. These games are intended as complex interactive
systems, and the designer's goal is to orchestrate mechanics, rules, content, play
spaces and feedback systems to generate specific and desired cognitive, emotional,
social and aesthetic experiences in players, always taking into account the context
of use and the essential ethical implications. As Jesse Schell (2008, p. xxxii; 2019, p.
30) states in his work "The Art of Game Design: A Book of Lenses", the game
designer is basically a creator of experiences: "Experience is everything. Without
experience, the game is nothing." The designer does not directly create the
experience, which is subjective, but designs the playful artifact that has the potential
to evoke it. An example of game design focused on creating a specific experience is
_Shadow of the Colossus_ (Team Ico, 2005), where each element is orchestrated to
evoke feelings of loneliness, wonder, and moral ambiguity.
The scientific approach to game design, therefore, involves a deep understanding of
these definitions, the rigorous analysis of the components of the game and the
application of systematic methodologies to design, test and validate the player
experience, integrating creativity with an informed and evidence-based process.


**1.2. The Game as a Complex Interactive System: In-Depth Structural**
**Analysis (Players, Rules, Objectives, Playful Space, Mechanics,**
**Dynamics, Aesthetics, Context) with Detailed Videogame Examples**


Adopting a systemic perspective, as anticipated, is crucial for a scientific analysis and
design of games. A game is not simply a collection of isolated elements, but a
**complex interactive system**, in which each component influences and is influenced
by the others, generating a holistic experience. General **Systems Theory**, introduced
by **Ludwig von Bertalanffy (1968),** provides a language and conceptual framework
for understanding these interdependencies. Authors such as **Salen and Zimmerman**
**(2004)** have explicitly applied this thinking to game design, identifying the
fundamental structural elements of a game. This section will delve into these
elements, enriching them with the concept of "context" and illustrating them with
detailed videogame examples to highlight their interrelationships.


The first fundamental element is represented by the **Players** . They are the active,
sentient participants who engage with the game system, interpreting the information
provided, making strategic decisions, and taking actions that change the state of the
game. As **Jesper Juul (2005)** points out in "Half Real", the player is the one who
"actualizes" the rules and gives life to the game; without it, the game system remains
a potential, inert artifact. The characteristics of the players that a designer must
consider are multiple and complex: their **intrinsic and extrinsic motivations**
**(analyzed in Chapter 2 through theories such as that of Self-determination by**
Deci & Ryan, 2000 **), their** cognitive **skills (such as attention, memory, problem-**
**solving skills – see Chapter 2.3) and** motor skills (precision, reaction times), their
**prior knowledge** (familiarity with specific video game genres, interface conventions,
or even thematic knowledge related to the game world), their **game genre**
**preferences** (explored through taxonomies such as Bartle's, see Chapter 2.2), their
**cultural background** (which may influence the interpretation of symbols or
narratives), their **emotional state** at the time of the game, and their **personal**
**expectations** and **goals** that they bring to the gaming experience. Players are not


14


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


mere passive receptors of an imposed design, but active agents who interpret the
rules, pursue objectives through available mechanics and, with their actions – and
their interactions, in multiplayer games – dynamically influence the state of the game,
often generating emergent and unpredictable dynamics. Their subjective experience,
the "Aesthetics" in the MDA framework (see Chapter 3.2), is the final output desired
by the designer and the main yardstick for evaluating design success. For example,
in _The Witcher 3: Wild Hunt_ (CD Projekt Red, 2015), the player not only controls the
protagonist Geralt, but his moral choices in dialogue and quests (which represent
significant input from the player) have tangible and often long-term consequences on
the narrative, relationships with non-player characters (NPCs) and the state of the
world, profoundly influencing the emotional experience and perception of agency. In
a competitive multiplayer game like _Overwatch 2_ (Blizzard Entertainment, 2022),
players choose heroes with radically different skill sets (mechanics); success
depends not only on individual skill (aim, use of skills, positioning), but also on
understanding the synergies between one's team's skills and the weaknesses of the
opposing team, generating complex dynamics that emerge from the interaction
between one's team's decisions and performance. twelve players. Even in a
seemingly simple game like _Candy Crush Saga_ (King, 2012), the player is an active
puzzle solver who must operate within precise rules (match at least three candies of
the same type to eliminate them and drop new ones); his motivations can range from
overcoming the intrinsic challenge of the puzzle, to the pleasure of progressing
through hundreds of levels, up to social motivations such as competing with friends
on the leaderboards or receiving extra lives. A more recent example of how the player
co creates the experience is _Death Stranding_ (Kojima Productions, 2019), where the
player, as Sam Porter Bridges, interacts with a complex system of asynchronous
transport and construction; the actions of other players (such as building bridges,
roads, stairs, or placing warning signs) persistently affect everyone else's game
world, creating a unique sense of "indirect sociality" and collective effort.


The **Rules** constitute the second structural pillar. As defined by **Juul (2005),** they are
a distinctive and fundamental feature of games, which differentiates them from other
forms of free play ( Caillois' paidia). The rules are the set of instructions, constraints,
permissions and prohibitions that define the space of possible actions for the players,
the conditions of interaction between the elements of the game, the consequences
of the actions performed and the criteria for winning, defeating or continuing the
gaming experience. Their primary function is to structure the game, limiting the space
of possibilities (and thus creating the challenge and meaning of the choices), defining
the systemic consequences of the actions and providing a common and, ideally,
unambiguous framework of interaction for all participants. **Salen and Zimmerman**
**(2004)** usefully distinguish between **Operational Rules**, which are the "superficial"
and often explicit instructions on how to play (e.g. "press the X button to jump" in a
platformer, or "you can move the knight to L" in chess); **Constitutive Rules**, which
represent the underlying mathematical, algorithmic, and formal logic that defines the
internal and abstract workings of the game (e.g., the formula that calculates the
damage of a weapon in _Borderlands 3_ (Gearbox Software, 2019) based on the
enemy's stats, weapon level, elemental bonuses, and resistances; the pathfinding
algorithm used by NPCs in _The Last of Us Part II_ (Naughty Dog, 2020) to navigate
complex environments); and **Implicit or Social Rules** (sometimes referred to as
"ludic etiquette" or "meta game"), which are unwritten norms, conventions, or
expectations of behavior that emerge from the gaming culture or player community
(e.g., in many online multiplayer games, the rule of not "spawn camping," i.e., not
waiting near spawn points to repeatedly eliminate newly rejoined players, or not using
known "exploits" to gain unfair advantages). A classic example of operational and


15


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


constitutive rules is found in chess: the rules define the unique movement of each
piece (the bishop moves only diagonally), the capture conditions, the promotion of
the pawn, castling, and, finally, the victory condition of checkmate. In _Portal 2_ (Valve,
2011), the fundamental rules, masterfully taught through level design, concern the
operation of the "Portal Gun" to create a maximum of two interconnected portals, one
blue and one orange, only on flat and suitable surfaces and the laws of physics
simulated by the game, in particular the conservation of momentum when crossing a
portal, which becomes the key to solving many puzzles. In a narrative game like
_Detroit: Become Human_ (Quantic Dream, 2018), the rules that govern the evolution
of the story are often tied to dialogue choices and actions taken at specific key
moments, with a "flowchart" system that shows the player (often at the end of the
chapter) how their decisions have branched out the narrative.


The third element is the **Goals/Objectives**, which are the specific, measurable and
desirable goals that players try to achieve within the game. They provide direction,
act as a powerful motivational engine (both intrinsic and extrinsic), and offer a
criterion for evaluating success and progression. Locke **and Latham's (1990)** Goal
Setting theory, although born in organizational contexts, suggests that clear, specific,
and adequately challenging goals lead to better performance and greater satisfaction,
a principle widely applicable to game design. Achievements can be **explicit and**
**prescribed** by the game (e.g., "Defeat Ganon" in _The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of_
_Time_, Nintendo, 1998; "Complete all major missions" in _Grand Theft Auto V_ ); **implicit**
**or emergent**, suggested by design but not overtly stated (e.g. in _Hitman 3_, IO
Interactive, 2021, the implicit goal of completing a mission with the highest score of
"Silent Assassin, Suit Only"); or **self-imposed by the player**, especially in sandbox
or high-agency games (e.g. in _Minecraft_, build a detailed replica of the Taj Mahal; in
_Stardew Valley_, Eric "ConcernedApe" Barone, 2016, creating a farm perfectly
optimized for a certain type of product; or attempt a "no hit run" in _Cuphead_, Studio
MDHR, 2017). Games often feature a **hierarchy of objectives** : main long-term
objectives (defeating the final boss), medium-term secondary objectives (completing
an optional quest line to obtain special equipment) and short-term micro objectives
(defeating a single group of enemies, solving an environmental puzzle to open a
passage). This layering helps to keep the player constantly engaged and provide a
sense of continuous progress. In _Civilization VI_ (Firaxis Games, 2016), players
pursue global victory conditions (Domination, Science, Culture, Religion, Scoring)
that represent very long-term strategic objectives, supported by a myriad of medium
and short-term objectives such as researching a specific technology, building a
Wonder of the World, or converting an opposing city to its religion. In contrast, a game
like _Outer Wilds_ (Mobius Digital, 2019) features a much more elusive main goal –
uncovering the mystery of a 22-minute time loop and the disappearance of an ancient
civilization – which breaks down into a series of exploration and understanding goals
driven almost entirely by the player's curiosity and discoveries, with very few explicit
indications.


The **Play Space / Game World** is the environment – physical, virtual, conceptual or
even social – in which the game action takes place and in which the rules and
mechanics take shape and meaning. This can vary tremendously in terms of scale
(from a single screen to entire procedural universes), dimensionality (2D, 2.5D, 3D),
and abstraction. The play space is not a simple passive container, but an active
component of the system: it provides context for player actions, presents obstacles
and opportunities, influences available strategies and tactics, can convey narrative
information through "environmental storytelling" (see Chapter 6.5), and contributes
significantly to the immersion, atmosphere and emotional tone of the game. Its


16


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


notable features include **dimensionality** (a 2D platformer like _Hollow Knight_, Team
Cherry, 2017, offers different navigation and combat challenges than a 3D action
RPG like _Horizon Forbidden West_, Guerrilla Games, 2022); topology **and structure**
(a linear level design like that of _Uncharted 2: Among Thieves_, Naughty Dog, 2009,
guides the player in a more stringent way than the open world structure of _Red Dead_
_Redemption 2_ ); **navigability and readability** (how easy it is for the player to orient
and move, see Chapter 5.2); interactivity (the presence of manipulable objects,
deformable terrain, destructible elements, such as environmental physics in _Half Life_
_2_, Valve, 2004); **aesthetics and atmosphere** (the visual style, sound design, and
lighting that define the "tone" of the space, such as the oppressive and distressing
Sevastopol space station in _Alien: Isolation_, Creative Assembly, 2014); and the
**boundaries** of the game world, which can be physical, logical, or narrative. The
playful space of _Tetris_ (Alexey Pajitnov, 1984) is an abstract, vertical and well-defined
two-dimensional grid, whose boundaries are fundamental rules of the game. In
contrast, the world of Yharnam in _Bloodborne_ (FromSoftware, 2015) is a labyrinthine,
interconnected three-dimensional space, whose complex level design full of hidden
shortcuts is itself a form of storytelling and a source of challenge. The spaceship "The
Skeld" in _Among Us_ (Innersloth, 2018) is a limited 2D play space, whose knowledge
of the map, the location of the "tasks", the "vents" and the security systems is crucial
for the gameplay of social deduction.


Finally, **Feedback** represents any information that the game system provides to the
player in direct or indirect response to their actions, or as a notification of changes in
the state of the game. It is an essential communicative element that closes the playersystem interaction loop. As discussed more extensively in Chapter 4.4, feedback
allows the player to understand the consequences of their choices, to learn and
master the rules and mechanics, to evaluate their progress towards goals, to feel in
control (supporting the SDT's need for competence and autonomy) and to maintain
engagement. It can be **visual** (an enemy staggering after a hit in _God of War_
_Ragnarök_, Santa Monica Studio, 2022; the interface showing the remaining health),
**auditory** (the sound of a completed reload in an FPS; the music changing to signal
imminent danger), **tactile** (the vibration of the PlayStation 5's DualSense controller
that simulates the tension of an arc or the surface of a ground), or **systemic/logical**
(a puzzle that is solved after the correct manipulation of the elements). In _Super Mario_
_Bros._, the characteristic sound when Mario picks up a coin, or the visual and audible
feedback when he hits a "?" block, are classic examples of immediate, multimodal
feedback that reinforces the player's actions. In _Sekiro: Shadows Die Twice_, the highpitched metallic sound and orange visual sparks generated by a perfectly timed parry
(deflection) provide crucial and highly rewarding feedback, essential for mastering
the complex combat system.


These structural elements – Players, Rules, Objectives, Playspace and Feedback
together with the **Mechanics** (the "verbs" of the game, the specific actions that
players can perform, analyzed in Chapter 3), are not isolated entities. They form a
dynamic and interconnected system. It is from the complex interaction of these
elements over time that the **Game Dynamics** (behavior patterns, actual strategies,
unexpected social interactions) and, ultimately, the **desired Aesthetics**, i.e. the
player's overall emotional, cognitive and social experience, emerge. This is the core
of the MDA (Mechanics, Dynamics, Aesthetics) framework proposed by **Hunicke,**
**LeBlanc and Zubek (2004),** which will be discussed in Chapter 3.2. A game designer
who takes a scientific approach must analyze and design each of these elements
and, above all, their interactions, in an intentional, systematic and iterative way, using
research and testing to validate and refine their choices. For example, the design of


17


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


the stealth experience in _Metal Gear Solid V: The Phantom Pain_ (Kojima Productions,
2015) is the result of careful orchestration of playful spaces that offer multiple paths
and cover (space), flexible infiltration mechanics (mechanical), rules on enemy alert
and visibility (rules), clear mission objectives but with multiple approaches
(objectives), and a sophisticated feedback system on enemy attention (feedback), all
aimed at creating an experience of tension, strategy and agency for the player. Added
to these elements is the **Context** (socio-cultural, technological, situational) in which
the game is enjoyed, which can significantly modulate the player's experience and
which a conscious designer must consider.


**1.3. Game Design as a Hybrid and Reflective Practice: The Synergy**
**between Scientific Method, Artistic Vision, Engineering Solutions and**
**Humanistic Understanding**


Game design, as a discipline and professional practice, is distinguished by its intrinsic
hybrid and deeply interdisciplinary nature. It cannot be confined within the limits of a
single field of knowledge, but feeds on and synthesizes knowledge, methodologies
and sensibilities from a multiplicity of domains, creating a unique synergy.
Understanding this multifaceted nature is essential to appreciate the complexity of
the game designer's role and to cultivate a scientific approach that is at once rigorous,
flexible, and reflective. We can identify at least four fundamental macro areas that,
interacting, contribute to defining contemporary game design: science, art,
engineering and the humanities and social sciences.


The **scientific dimension** of game design is based on the application of the scientific
method to deeply understand players, to formulate and validate design hypotheses
through experimentation and to measure the impact of the game experiences
created. This approach draws heavily from **psychology**, particularly cognitive and
behavioral psychology. As explored in Chapter 2, fundamental theories such as **Deci**
**and Ryan's (2000)** Theory of Self-Determination inform the design of motivational
systems that support the needs for competence, autonomy and relationality. For
example, the progression system and optional challenges in _Hollow Knight_ (Team
Cherry, 2017) are excellent examples of how to support the player's sense of
competence and offer autonomy. Learning principles, such as those outlined by
**James Paul Gee (2003)** in "What Video Games Have to Teach Us About Learning
and Literacy", guide the creation of effective tutorials and gradual learning curves, as
seen in the design of the first levels of _Portal 2_ (Valve, 2011), which introduce the
mechanics of the portal in an intuitive and progressive way. The understanding of
attentional processes, working memory and cognitive load (cf. Sweller's Cognitive
Load Theory **, 1988** ) is crucial for designing interfaces and information flows that do
not overload the player, especially in complex games such as RTS (Real Time
Strategy) such as _Age of Empires IV_ (Relic Entertainment & World's Edge, 2021).
**Human-Computer Interaction (HCI),** as detailed in Chapter 7, provides a corpus of
heuristics (e.g., Nielsen's usability heuristics **, 1993** ) and methods (e.g., usability
testing) for designing interfaces that are not only functional but also pleasant to use.
**Statistics and data analysis**, through game analytics (see Chapter 8.3), have
become indispensable for interpreting playtest data, balancing complex game
systems (such as the economy of _EVE Online_, CCP Games, 2003) and
understanding the behavior of millions of players, allowing evidence-based design
iterations. Finally, emerging fields such as **cognitive neuroscience** and **affective**
**computing** (see Chapter 9.5) are beginning to offer tools to measure and understand
players' neural and emotional responses in a more direct way, opening new frontiers


18


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


for personalization and emotional design.


In parallel, game design is undeniably an **art form**, expressing itself through the
creation of aesthetically meaningful, emotionally resonant, and culturally relevant
experiences. This artistic dimension concerns the designer's ability to evoke
"feelings" and touch deep chords in the player's soul, as eloquently described by
**Jesse Schell (2008).** **Creative writing and narratology** (see Chapter 6) are key to
creating compelling stories, memorable characters, and incisive dialogue, as
evidenced by the acclaimed storytelling of games such as _The Last of Us Part II_
(Naughty Dog, 2020) or the thematic complexity of _Nier: Automata_
(PlatinumGames, 2017). **Visual arts** including concept art, illustration, 3D
modeling, animation, texturing, and lighting define the game's aesthetic identity and
contribute powerfully to immersion and atmosphere. The distinctive visual style of
_Cuphead_ (Studio MDHR, 2017), inspired by cartoons of the 30s, or the evocative
use of light and shadow in _Limbo_ (Playdead, 2010) are examples of how art
direction can define an experience. Sound **design and musical composition** are
equally crucial, capable of creating immersive sound worlds, conveying emotions
(the tension conveyed by the music of _Resident Evil Village_, Capcom, 2021),
providing crucial feedback to the player (the sound of a successful parry in _Sekiro:_
_Shadows Die Twice_, FromSoftware, 2019) and defining the identity of a game (the
iconic melodies of _The Legend of Zelda series_ ). Directing and cinematography,
especially in cutscenes or scripted sequences, use techniques borrowed from
cinema to guide attention, build tension and tell stories in a visually impactful way.
Finally, there **'s a "** game feel" (often also described as "juice"), which relates to the
tactile quality, responsiveness, and inherent pleasantness of the game mechanics
themselves – the feeling of a well-timed jump in _Super Mario Odyssey_ or the
satisfying feedback of a successful hit in _DOOM Eternal_ (id Software, 2020). The
artistic methodology is based on intuition, aesthetic experimentation, the search for
expressive originality, a deep understanding of the language of media and the
ability to evoke specific "aesthetics" or forms of entertainment, such as those
outlined in the MDA framework (sensation, fantasy, narration, challenge,
companionship, discovery, expression, submission/pastime).


The third soul of game design is engineering, which deals with the practical, logical
and functional construction of game systems, ensuring that they are robust,
balanced, performing and technically feasible. This component requires analytical
thinking and a solid understanding of the operating principles of complex systems.
**Software engineering and computer science** are the basis of the implementation
of every aspect of digital gaming, from the algorithms that govern the basic
mechanics, to the artificial intelligence of enemies and allies (such as the complex
team behaviors of NPCs in _Gears 5_, The Coalition, 2019), to network systems for
online play. **Systems design**, as explored by **Adams and Dormans (2012** ), is crucial
to define the rules, components, and interactions of complex game systems, such as
the intricate virtual economies of games such as _Path of Exile_ (Grinding Gear Games,
2013), combat systems with multiple variables (damage, resistances, status effects),
or skill trees that offer progression paths Diversified. **Mathematics and formal logic**
are indispensable tools for the numerical balancing of game parameters (e.g., the
probability of a "critical hit", the mana cost of a spell), for the creation of stringent logic
puzzles (such as those in _The Witness_, Thekla, Inc., 2016), and for the formalization
of rules in an unambiguous way. Information **architecture** guides the organization of
content and the design of user interfaces and menus that are navigable and
understandable. Finally, **Quality Assurance (QA) and technical testing** are critical
engineering processes for identifying and fixing bugs, glitches, performance issues,


19


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


and bottlenecks that could compromise the player experience. The engineering
methodology is based on rapid and iterative prototyping, systematic debugging,
resource optimization, mathematical modeling and system simulation, with a constant
focus on scalability, maintainability and robustness of the final product.


Finally, game design is deeply rooted in the **humanities and social sciences**, which
provide the tools to understand the cultural, social, historical and ethical context in
which games are created, enjoyed and interpreted, and to reflect on the impact they
have on individuals and society as a whole. Sociology **and anthropology** are
fundamental to study the dynamics of online gaming communities (e.g. the complex
social and political structures that emerge in _EVE Online_ ), the virtual cultures that
form around games (cf. **Taylor, 2006**, "Play Between Worlds"), emerging social
norms and the impact of games on daily life and interpersonal relationships. Cultural
**studies and media studies** allow us to analyze games as cultural texts, exploring
representations of gender, ethnicity, social class and sexual orientation (cf.
**Consalvo, 2006**, "Console Cowboys and Cyber Senoritas"; **Nakamura, 2002**,
"Cybertypes"), and to understand the role of games in public discourse and in the
formation of the collective imagination. The **story** not only provides thematic material
for games (such as in the _Assassin's Creed series_, Ubisoft), but also helps to
understand the evolution of the gaming medium itself and the cultural influences that
have shaped it. Philosophy, particularly ethics and aesthetics, offers tools for
reflecting on the moral dilemmas presented or induced by games (e.g., the difficult
choices in _This War of Mine_, 11 bit studios, 2014), the nature of fun and the ludic
aesthetic experience, and the growing ethical responsibilities of designers (see
Section 1.6). Economics, especially behavioral and political economics, is useful for
analyzing virtual economies (see Chapter 4.2), industry business models (from
premium to free to play), and consumption and production dynamics in virtual worlds.
The methodology of the humanities and social sciences applied to game design
includes critical analysis of text and discourse, ethnographic studies (including virtual
ones), comparative historical analysis, systematic ethical reflection and constant
attention to the social and cultural impact of design choices.


A scientifically complete game designer, therefore, is not an isolated expert in a single
discipline, but rather an **integrator and orchestrator of different knowledge**,
capable of navigating the complexity of this hybrid practice. Designing a meaningful
moral choice mechanic in an RPG like _Mass Effect 2_ (BioWare, 2010), for example,
requires an understanding of the psychology of decision and emotions to anticipate
the player's reaction (science); writing dialogue and scenarios that present the
dilemma in an artistically engaging and emotionally impactful way (art); and
technically implementing a system that tracks the player's choices and shows their
consequences short- and long-term in the game world and narrative progression
(engineering); and a reflection on the ethical implications of choice and its resonance
with universal moral themes (human sciences). This ability to reflexively synthesize,
to "think like a scientist, feel like an artist, and build like an engineer," all informed by
a deep understanding of the human factor and sociocultural context, is what defines
excellence in contemporary game design.


**1.4. Advanced Research Paradigms and Methodologies for**
**Game** **Design:** **Qualitative** **(Virtual** **Ethnography,**
**Phenomenological** **Analysis),** **Quantitative** **(Biometrics,**
**Advanced Game Analytics), Mixed Methods and Design Based**
**Research for Ludic Innovation**


20


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


Adopting a scientific approach to game design necessarily involves using systematic
and rigorous research methodologies to inform decision-making, validate design
hypotheses, and thoroughly understand the player experience. While Chapter 8 of
this handbook will discuss in detail playtesting practices and research methods with
common users, this section intends to introduce advanced research paradigms and
methodologies that are further enriching the field, allowing for deeper and more
nuanced investigations. These approaches include sophisticated qualitative
techniques such as virtual ethnography and phenomenological analysis, state-of-theart quantitative methods such as biometrics and game analytics based on machine
learning, the strategic integration of these approaches into mixed methods, and the
Design Based Research (DBR) paradigm, which holds particular promise for playful
innovation in application contexts.


Advanced **qualitative research** in game design goes beyond standard interviews
and focus groups to explore the "why" and "how" of the gaming experience with
greater depth and contextual sensitivity. Virtual **ethnography**, for example, adapts
the classical ethnographic methods of anthropology (cf. **Fetterman, 2010** ) to the
study of the communities and cultures that form within and around online gaming
worlds. Researchers such as **Bonnie Nardi (2010)** in "My Life as a Night Elf Priest"
or **T.L. Taylor (2006)** in "Play Between Worlds" have used prolonged participant
observation, interviews, and analysis of digital artifacts to understand the complex
social dynamics, norms, rituals, and identities that emerge in massively multiplayer
online (MMO) games such as _World of Warcraft_ or _EverQuest_ . These studies provide
valuable insights for designers who aim to create robust social systems and positive
online communities. Another powerful qualitative methodology is **phenomenological**
**analysis**, which seeks to grasp the essence of the lived experience (Husserl's
"Lebenswelt") by the players. Through detailed phenomenological interviews (cf.
**Giorgi, 2009** ) or the analysis of experiential accounts, researchers can explore the
subjective nature of concepts such as immersion, presence in VR, or the specific
emotional experience evoked by a particular game, such as the feeling of "agency"
and moral responsibility in a complex narrative game such as _The Walking Dead:_
_Season One_ (Telltale Games, 2012). Other advanced qualitative methods include
critical discourse analysis to examine representations and ideologies in games, or
autoethnography, where the researcher analyzes his or her own gaming experience
in a reflective way.


On the quantitative **front**, methodologies are evolving to capture increasingly rich
data and to analyze it with more sophisticated techniques. Biometrics and
physiological measurements (see also Chapters 7.4 and 9.5) represent an important
frontier. The use of **electroencephalogram (EEG)** to measure brain activity related
to attention, cognitive load, or emotional states during play (cf. **Vecchiato et al.,**
**2011** ); **galvanic skin response (GSR/EDA)** to track emotional arousal; **facial**
**electromyography (fEMG)** to detect micro expressions related to positive or
negative emotions; and advanced **eye tracking** to analyze not only where players
look, but also pupillary dilation as an indicator of cognitive effort or interest (cf.
**Duchowski, 2007** ), are providing researchers and designers with more direct
windows into the player's internal experience. For example, eye tracking could be
used to optimize the interface design of a complex RTS like _StarCraft II,_ ensuring that
critical information is placed in the areas of greatest visual attention during the game's
hectic phases. Advanced **game analytics** (see also Chapter 8.3) go beyond simple
descriptive statistics, employing **machine learning** (ML) and **data mining**
techniques to uncover complex patterns in the behavior of millions of players. This
includes predictive modeling (e.g., predicting which players are at risk of churn or


21


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


churn, as in many F2P mobile games), player segmentation based on behavioral play
styles (player profiling), anomaly detection (cheating, botting), and dynamic
optimization of game systems through large-scale A/B testing or reinforcement
learning algorithms. **Seif El Nasr, Drachen and Canossa (2013)** offer an overview
of these techniques.


**Mixed Methods Research**, as introduced in Chapter 1.3 and discussed more
extensively by **Creswell and Plano Clark (2017),** takes on even greater importance
when dealing with the complexities of game design. The strategic integration of deep
qualitative data and broad quantitative data allows for a more holistic understanding
and greater validity of the results. For example, in studying the effectiveness of a new
tutorial system for a game such as _Kerbal Space Program_ (Squad, 2015), known for
its complexity, researchers could combine: telemetry (quantitative) data on tutorial
completion rates and early successes/failures in the game; performance test results
on specific (quantitative) tasks; and in-depth (qualitative) interviews with new players
to understand their difficulties; their learning strategies and their subjective
perception of the clarity of the tutorial. This approach allows you to not only identify
_what_ is not working, but also _why_ it does not work and _how it_ could be improved.


Finally, **Design Based Research (DBR)** represents a methodological paradigm
particularly suitable for innovation in game design, especially in application contexts
such as educational games, "serious games" or health games. DBR is characterized
by a pragmatic and iterative approach that aims to simultaneously develop theories
of learning or behavioural change in specific contexts and practical interventions (the
games themselves) that are effective in those contexts (cf. **DBR Collective, 2003** ;
**Anderson & Shattuck, 2012** ; **Reeves, Herrington, & Oliver, 2005** ). Unlike
traditional experimental research that tests a "finished" intervention, DBR involves
researchers in the process of designing, implementing, and continuously refining the
playful intervention within its real-world context of use (e.g., a classroom, a
rehabilitation program). Each DBR cycle typically includes: practical problem analysis
and learning/behavior goal setting; development of a game prototype based on
theoretical principles; implementation of the prototype in the real context; collection
of qualitative and quantitative data on user experience and intervention effectiveness;
data analysis to inform theory review and game redesign. An example would be the
iterative development of a game to teach coding concepts to middle school students,
where each version of the game is tested in the classroom, and data on the
engagement, learning, and difficulties faced by students is used to improve both the
game and the theoretical understanding of how games can support learning to code.
**Barab and Squire (2004)** discussed the potential of DBR for game studies.


The mastery of these advanced research methodologies, and the ability to select and
integrate the most appropriate ones to answer specific design questions, are
fundamental skills for the scientifically oriented game designer. They allow us to go
beyond personal intuitions and preferences, basing design decisions on a solid basis
of empirical evidence and contributing to the growth of knowledge within the
discipline. Research in game design is not an activity separate from practice, but an
intrinsic and vital component of it, which fuels innovation, improves the quality of
gaming experiences and deepens our understanding of the complex and fascinating
phenomenon of gaming.


**1.5. Historical Evolution of Game Design Thought and Practice:**
**From the Origins of Games to the Manifestos of the Digital**


22


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


**Pioneers, up to the Contemporary Schools of Thought and the**
**Impact of Disruptive Technologies**


The discipline of game design, although it has only been academically formalized
relatively recently, has its roots in a long and rich history of ludic creation and
theoretical reflection that spans millennia and cultures. Understanding this evolution
is critical to contextualizing contemporary practices, appreciating the contributions of
pioneers, recognizing the transformative impact of technological innovations, and
identifying continuities and ruptures in the way of thinking and creating games. This
section will trace a historical path, highlighting key figures, seminal works, paradigm
shifts and the influence of technologies.


The origins of the game are lost in the mists of time, with archaeological finds that
testify to recreational activities already structured in the first civilizations. Board
games such as the **Egyptian Senet** (dated around 3100 BC), the Sumerian **Royal**
**Game of your** (about 2600 BC), or the Chinese Go (whose origins could date back
to over 2500 years ago and whose strategic depth is still being studied by AI today,
as demonstrated by DeepMind's AlphaGo, cf. **Silver et al., 2016** ) demonstrate a
sophisticated implicit understanding of mechanics, strategy, interaction, and even
elements of luck. While we can't talk about "game designers" in the modern sense,
the anonymous creators of these games laid the groundwork for many fundamental
game concepts. At the same time, philosophers of antiquity such as **Plato**, in the
"Laws", and **Aristotle**, in the "Nicomachean Ethics" and in the "Politics", already
reflected on the educational, social and cathartic value of play (paidia) and mimetic
representations, sensing the importance of play for individual development and social
cohesion.


During the Middle Ages and Renaissance in Europe, games continued to evolve, with
the spread of games such as **chess** (which came from India via Persia and the Arab
world around the tenth century), dice and the first card games. These games were
played in different social classes, from noble courts to the working classes. In this
period, the first specific treatises on certain games emerged, such as the famous
" **Libro de los juegos** " commissioned by Alfonso X of Castile in the thirteenth century,
an illuminated manuscript detailing the rules and strategies of chess, dice and tables
(an ancestor of backgammon). These texts, while being more descriptive than
prescriptive in terms of "design" as we understand it today, represent the first
attempts to formalize, analyze, and transmit ludic knowledge.


The Modern Age, particularly between the eighteenth and nineteenth centuries, saw,
with the advent of printing and mass production and a growing bourgeoisie eager for
entertainment and education, a new flowering of board games. Figures such as the
Englishman **John Wallis** (creator of geographical educational games) or the
Americans **Milton Bradley** and **George S. Parker** began to design and market
games that went beyond traditional forms, often with educational, moral or current
economic and social themes. The concept of "author" or "designer" of games began
to take shape more clearly, although the practice was still largely empirical and
artisanal. Games such as _The Game of Life_ (originally _The Checkered Game of Life_,
Milton Bradley, 1860), which simulated a life path with successes and failures, or
_Monopoly_ (patented by Charles Darrow in 1935, but based on _Elizabeth Magie_ 's The
Landlord's Game, 1903, a game critical of monopoly capitalism), became cultural
phenomena, demonstrating the power of games to reflect, comment on, and even
influence society.


23


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


The twentieth century was a turning point for both the theory and practice of the
game. As already mentioned in Section 1.1, the fundamental contributions of **Johan**
**Huizinga (1938)** with "Homo Ludens" and **Roger Caillois (1958)** with "Les jeux et
les hommes" provided the theoretical basis for the academic study of play as a
cultural and social phenomenon. At the same time, the genre of modern **wargames**
emerged and developed . Initially conceived as military training tools (think of the
Prussian _Kriegsspiel_ of the nineteenth century), wargames on the board became a
form of sophisticated entertainment for an audience of enthusiasts. Figures such as
**Charles S. Roberts**, founder of Avalon Hill in 1952, are considered pioneers of this
genre, introducing concepts such as Combat Results Tables (CRTs), the use of
hexagonal counters to represent units, and a focus on balancing and historical
simulation, albeit simplified. Games such as _Tactics II_ (Avalon Hill, 1958) or the more
complex _Gettysburg_ (Avalon Hill, 1958, reissued several times) set standards for the
genre and influenced generations of designers, including those who would later turn
to strategy video games.


The advent of the digital age, starting from the second half of the twentieth century,
represented a paradigmatic break and an explosion of new possibilities for playful
interaction. The first experiments with computers as gaming platforms, such as
_Tennis for Two_ (created by **William Higinbotham** in 1958 on an oscilloscope at
Brookhaven National Laboratory) or the famous _Spacewar!_ (developed by **Steve**
**Russell** and other students at MIT in 1962 on the PDP 1 mainframe), demonstrated
the unique potential of computers to create dynamic and immersive interactive
systems. These early "designers" were often engineers and scientists exploring the
capabilities of new media, often in academic or research settings.


The 70s saw the birth and explosion of the **arcade video game industry** and the
first **home consoles** . Iconic titles such as _Pong_ (Atari, 1972, designed by **Allan**
**Alcorn** under the guidance of Nolan Bushnell), _Space Invaders_ (Taito, 1978,
**Tomohiro Nishikado** ) and _Pac Man_ (Namco, 1980, **Toru Iwatani** ) defined the first
video game genres and introduced innovative core mechanics, highly immersive
gameplay loops (such as Pac Man's "avoid eat-flight" cycle) and a focus on crucial to
immediate and rewarding feedback. In parallel, early home consoles such as the
Magnavox Odyssey (designed by **Ralph Baer**, often considered the "father of home
video games", 1972) and the huge success of the Atari VCS/2600 (1977) brought
video games into the homes of millions. Designers such as **Carol Shaw** (author of
_River Raid_ for Activision, 1982, one of the first examples of scrolling shooters with
procedural terrain generation) or **Warren Robinett** (creator of _Adventure_ for Atari,
1979, considered one of the first action adventure games and famous for containing
the first widely known "Easter egg") were among the pioneers of this period, often
working as individuals or in very small teams, covering multiple roles (designer,
programmer, artist).


With the increasing complexity and popularity of video games in the 1980s and
1990s, a more structured and formalized reflection on the game design process
began to emerge. **Chris Crawford**, with his book "The Art of Computer Game
Design" (1984), was one of the first to attempt to outline the specific principles of
digital game design, emphasizing the importance of interactivity, clear objectives and
conflict as a driver of experience. Crawford himself was an influential designer, known
for complex strategy games such as _Eastern Front (1941)_ (Atari Program Exchange,
1981) and for his theoretical reflections on interactivity. The era of microcomputers
(such as the Commodore 64, ZX Spectrum, Apple II) and the spread of PCs saw the
birth of more complex genres, such as the text adventures of Infocom (Zork _, 1977-_


24


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


_79), the graphic adventures of Sierra On Line (_ Roberta Williams **' King's Quest**
series, starting in 1984) and LucasArts ( _Ron Gilbert_ 's Monkey Island series and
others, starting in 1990), who explored interactive narrative, puzzle-solving, and
world-building. In Japan, figures such as **Shigeru Miyamoto** (Nintendo), creator of
immortal franchises such as _Donkey Kong_ (1981), _Super Mario Bros._ (1985) and _The_
_Legend of Zelda_ (1986), revolutionized design with meticulous attention to "game
feel" (the responsiveness and pleasantness of the controls), to the intuitive level
design that teaches through experience ("learning by doing"), and to the gradual and
organic introduction of mechanics. Miyamoto's approach, often described through the
Japanese narrative structure "kishōtenketsu" (introduction, development, twist,
conclusion) applied to level design, has profoundly influenced generations of
designers. At the same time, designers such as **Will Wright**, with _SimCity_ (Maxis,
1989) and later the global phenomenon _The Sims_ (2000), popularized the genre of
"software toys" or open simulations ("god games"), where the player has a high
degree of agency and the objectives are often emerging or self-imposed. Wright's
work emphasizes the design of complex, interconnected systems and the enjoyment
of discovery and creativity within those systems. Other influential designers of this
period include **Sid Meier** (the _Civilization_ series, starting in 1991), master of "4X"
strategy games (eXplore, eXpand, eXploit, eXterminate), and **Peter Molyneux** (with
games such as _Populous_, Bullfrog, 1989, and later the _Fable series_ ), known for his
ambitious (and sometimes controversial) visions of the potential of AI and interaction.


The modern era, from the 2000s to the present, has been characterized by a
significant formalization of game design thinking, the proliferation of analytical
theories and models, and a growing scientific and evidence-based orientation.
Seminal texts such as "Rules of Play" by **Salen and Zimmerman (2004** ), "A Theory
of Fun for Game Design" **by Raph Koster (2005),** "The Art of Game Design: A Book
of Lenses" by **Jesse Schell (2008, with subsequent editions),** "Game Design
Workshop" by **Tracy Fullerton (2008, with subsequent editions),** and "Game
Mechanics: Advanced Game Design" by **Ernest Adams and Joris Dormans (2012)**
They have helped to create a shared body of knowledge, a common language for
designers and dedicated academic programmes. Technological impact has
continued to be a driver of transformation. The advent of the **Internet and massively**
**multiplayer online games (MMORPGs)** such as _Ultima Online_ (Origin Systems,
1997) and _EverQuest_ (Verant Interactive, 1999), and then _World of Warcraft_ (Blizzard
Entertainment, 2004), introduced complex challenges related to the design of social
dynamics, persistent virtual economies, content for thousands of players, and the
management of "games as a service" (GaaS) with updates and continuous content.
Games like _Fortnite_ (Epic Games, 2017) or _Genshin Impact_ (miHoYo, 2020)
exemplify this model. **Mobile gaming**, which exploded with the advent of
smartphones, has led to a focus on short gaming sessions, intuitive touch controls, a
wide variety of casual and hypercasual genres, and the predominance of free-to-play
(F2P) monetization models with in-app purchases (IAPs), also raising important
ethical questions (see Section 1.6). Immersive technologies such as **Virtual Reality**
**(VR)** and **Augmented Reality (AR)** (see Chapter 9.2) are opening up new frontiers
for immersion, presence, and interaction, but they also pose unique challenges in
terms of interface design, locomotion, and motion sickness prevention. **Artificial**
**Intelligence (AI)** (see Chapter 9.4) is having an ever-deeper impact, not only in the
creation of more believable NPCs and smarter opponents, but also in the dynamic
customization of the game experience, balancing, and even in the procedural
generation of content (PCG). The **democratization of development tools**, with
powerful and accessible game engines like Unity and Unreal Engine, and digital
distribution platforms like Steam, itch.io, and mobile app stores, has made game


25


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


development more accessible to smaller teams and individuals. This has led to an
explosion of **indie games that** often serve as a testing ground for innovative
mechanics, unique art styles and bold narratives, as seen in acclaimed titles such as
_Braid_ (Jonathan Blow, 2008), _Undertale_ (Toby Fox, 2015), _Papers, Please_ (Lucas
Pope, 2013), _Celeste_ (Matt Makes Games, 2018) or _Hades_ (Supergiant Games,
2020). In parallel, the growth of **Game Studies** as an interdisciplinary academic field
has provided an increasingly sophisticated critical and analytical framework for
studying games as cultural artifacts, formal systems, and human experiences,
informing and enriching design practice.


This long and complex historical evolution demonstrates how game design has
always been a dynamic dialogue between individual and collective creativity,
technological constraints and opportunities, cultural influences and a growing (though
not always explicit) understanding of the player and the mechanics of the game. The
lessons learned from the pioneers, whether in terms of successful innovations or
dead ends or failures, continue to be a valuable source of inspiration and warning.
The contemporary scientific approach to game design does not seek to erase or
replace intuition, accumulated experience and artistic vision, but rather to integrate
them with rigorous methods of analysis, experimentation and validation, in order to
address the increasingly complex challenges and immense opportunities of
designing meaningful and engaging gaming experiences in the digital age.
Understanding this historical trajectory allows today's designers to situate their work
in a broader context, to learn critically from the past and to contribute in a more
conscious and informed way to the future of this fascinating discipline.


**1.6. Game Design Ethics and Social Responsibility in the Digital**
**Age: Critical Analysis of the Psychological (e.g. "dark patterns",**
**addiction), Cultural (representation, stereotypes) and Social**
**(online communities, impact on society) Implications**


The application of a scientific approach to game design, with its growing ability to
understand and influence player psychology, behavioral dynamics and engagement,
requires a deep ethical reflection and an awareness of the social responsibility that
weighs on designers and the video game industry as a whole. Games are not neutral
artifacts; They convey values, shape perceptions, influence behaviour and have a
tangible impact on the lives of individuals and society. A scientifically conscious game
designer must therefore navigate a complex landscape of ethical considerations,
striving to create experiences that are not only engaging and effective, but also
respectful, fair, and potentially beneficial. This section will critically analyze some of
the main psychological, cultural and social implications of game design in the digital
age.


One of the areas of greatest ethical concern concerns the **psychological**
**implications** of design, particularly the risk of creating or exacerbating problematic
gambling behaviors or addiction. The scientific understanding of reinforcement
mechanisms (see Chapter 4.1), cognitive biases (Chapter 2.5) and motivational
dynamics can be used, intentionally or unintentionally, to design systems that
maximize engagement at the expense of player well-being. The so-called " **dark**
**patterns** " in design (cf. **Brignull, n.d.** ; **Zagal, Björk, & Lewis, 2013** ; **Gray et al.,**
**2018** ) are deliberately deceptive or manipulative interfaces or mechanics, designed
to trick users into taking actions that are not in their primary interest, such as making


26


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


unwanted purchases, spending more time than intended in the game, or giving away
personal data. Examples include artificially urgent bidding timers, interfaces that
make it difficult to cancel a subscription, or variable-ratio reward systems (such as
**loot boxes** ) that exploit psychological mechanisms similar to those of gambling. **King**
**& Delfabbro, 2018** ; **Zendle & Cairns, 2018** ). Games like _Star Wars Battlefront II_ (EA
DICE, 2017) sparked huge controversy at launch for their aggressive loot box
mechanics, which were perceived as "pay to win" and potentially harmful. Ethical
design requires radical transparency about such mechanics, the protection of
vulnerable players (especially minors), and prioritizing player welfare over short-term
profit maximization. **Schüll's (2012)** research in "Addiction by Design" offers a
disturbing ethnographic analysis of how slot machines are designed to induce a
compulsive "zone" state. Although video games are different, some lessons on
designing for compulsion are transferable and require vigilance. Designers have a
responsibility to consider the impact of their work on mental health, promoting
"responsible play" through self-imposed time limits, reminders for breaks, and design
that does not overly penalize players who choose to disconnect.


The **cultural implications** of game design are equally significant. Video games are
powerful vehicles of storytelling and representation, capable of shaping the
perception of the world, history, identities and values. The issue of representation of
gender, ethnicity, sexual orientation, disability and other identities is crucial. For
decades, the industry has been criticized for underrepresenting or stereotyping
marginalized groups (cf. **Consalvo, 2006** ; **Nakamura, 2002** ; **Kafai et al., 2008** ). For
example, the persistence of the "damsel in distress" stereotype or the
hypersexualization of female characters have been the subject of widespread
criticism. An ethical and scientifically informed approach to character and narrative
design (see Chapter 6) should aim for more diverse, authentic, nuanced, and
respectful representations that reflect the complexity of the real world and that offer
players a wider range of models and possibilities of identification. Games like _The_
_Last of Us Part II_, while controversial in other respects, have been praised for their
portrayal of strong, complex female characters and the inclusion of LGBTQ+ themes.
Conversely, games that perpetuate harmful stereotypes or trivialize violence against
specific groups can have a negative cultural impact. Research on the effects of the
media (cf. **Bryant & Vorderer, 2006** ) suggests that media representations can
influence attitudes and beliefs. Designers must be aware of the cultural power they
hold and strive to use it responsibly, promoting empathy and cross-cultural
understanding rather than prejudice. For example, _Never Alone (Kisima Ingitchuna)_
(Upper One Games, 2014), developed in collaboration with Alaska's Iñupiaq
community, is an example of how games can be used to preserve and share cultural
heritage in an authentic and respectful way.


Finally, the **social implications** of game design, especially in the age of online
games and pervasive virtual communities, require constant ethical attention. As
discussed in Chapter 9.3, multiplayer games can be powerful platforms for friendship
formation, social support, and collaboration (cf. **Kowert & Quandt, 2016** ). Games
like _EVE Online_ or the big guilds in _World of Warcraft_ have spawned complex
communities with their own economies, political systems, and enduring cultures.
However, these online spaces can also become breeding grounds for toxic
behaviour, cyberbullying, harassment, hate speech and the spread of misinformation.
**Blackburn & Kwak, 2014**, on toxicity in _League of Legends_ ; **Anti Defamation**
**League, 2021**, on reports on hate in online games). Designers have a responsibility
to create systems that mitigate these risks and promote positive interactions. This
includes designing communication systems that offer filtering and blocking options,


27


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


effective and transparent reporting mechanisms, moderation systems (human and/or
AI-assisted), and, ideally, game mechanics that incentivize cooperation, empathy,
and prosocial behavior. For example, the "endorsement" system in _Overwatch 2_,
which allows players to positively recognize the sporting or cooperative behavior of
others, is an attempt to foster a more positive gaming culture. The **privacy of player**
**data** is another crucial social concern, especially with the increasing collection of
telemetry and biometric data. Designers and companies must adhere to strict data
protection standards (such as GDPR in Europe), be transparent about data use, and
obtain informed consent (see Chapter 8.5). In addition, the impact of gaming on
**public health** (sedentary lifestyle, vision problems, sleep disorders) and the
**environment** (power consumption of consoles, PCs, and servers) are emerging
issues that require ethical consideration.


The scientific approach to game design, by providing tools to understand and
influence behavior, amplifies the scope of these ethical responsibilities. It is not
enough to create a game that is "effective" in achieving its engagement or
monetization goals; you need to constantly ask yourself, "What is the impact of this
design on my player's well-being? On the culture I am helping to create? On society
as a whole?". Authors such as **Mary Flanagan and Helen Nissenbaum (2014),** in
"Values at Play in Digital Games", propose a "Value Sensitive Design" approach,
which encourages designers to explicitly identify and incorporate human values into
the design process. Ethical reflection should not be an afterthought, but an integral
and ongoing component of the scientific game design process, guided by principles
of beneficence, non-maleficence, autonomy, justice, and respect for people. The
ethical training of game designers, open dialogue within the industry and academic
community, and the development of professional codes of conduct and guidelines
(such as those proposed by IGDA) are all essential elements to navigate these
complex challenges responsibly.


**1.7. Conclusions: Towards an Informed, Critical and Responsible Game Design**
**Practice**


This inaugural chapter laid the foundations for a scientific approach to game design,
outlining its epistemological nature, its systemic structure, its interdisciplinary
richness, its research methodologies and its historical evolution, culminating in a
reflection on the essential ethical and social responsibilities. It emerged that game
design is a complex and dynamic discipline, which requires a unique synthesis of
artistic creativity, analytical rigor, engineering skill and deep understanding of the
human factor and the socio-cultural context.


Understanding the evolutionary definitions of "game" and "game design", from
Huizinga and Caillois to the conceptualizations of the digital age, allows us to
appreciate the depth and pervasiveness of the playful phenomenon. The analysis of
the game as a complex system, with its players, rules, objectives, spaces, mechanics
and feedback, provides a language and structure to dissect and design interactive
experiences in an intentional way. Recognizing game design as an inherently hybrid
practice, drawing on science, art, engineering, and the humanities, pushes us to
value interdisciplinarity and cultivate an open and integrative mindset.


The introduction of qualitative, quantitative and mixed research methodologies,
including Design Based Research, emphasizes the importance of an evidence-based
approach, where the designer's insights are constantly tested, validated and refined


28


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


through interaction with players and rigorous data analysis. The historical excursus
showed us how the practice of design evolved in response to the technological
innovations and critical thinking of its pioneers, offering valuable lessons for
contemporary challenges.


Finally, the discussion on ethical implications and social responsibility highlights how
a scientific approach to game design cannot be separated from a deep awareness of
the impact that games have on individuals and society. The ability to create powerful
and engaging experiences comes with a duty to carefully consider the psychological,
cultural, and social consequences of one's design choices, promoting player wellbeing, inclusivity, fair representation, and building positive online communities.


In summary, this chapter wanted to establish that scientific game design is a practice
**informed** by theory and research, **critical** in its analyses and reflections, and
**responsible** in its applications. The following chapters of this manual will delve into
the specific details of each of the core areas of game design, building on this
foundation to provide concrete tools and in-depth knowledge. The ultimate goal is to
train game designers who are not only skilled artisans or visionary artists, but also
scientific thinkers and conscious citizens, capable of shaping the future of this
powerful medium in ways that are innovative, meaningful and deeply human.


29


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


30


GAME DESIGN MANUAL

# **Chapter 2**
### **Player Psychology:** **Motivation, Cognition,** **Emotion and Gaming** **Experience**


_Games are a number of interesting choices_

**-** _**Sid Meier-**_


31


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


At the heart of every gaming experience lies the player, a complex being with a rich
inner life, whose perceptions, motivations, cognitive processes, and emotions
ultimately determine the success or failure of a design. Understanding the psychology
of the player is not an optional, but a fundamental prerequisite for a scientifically
based game design capable of creating deeply engaging, meaningful and memorable
experiences. This chapter will delve into the depths of the player's mind, exploring
the key theoretical models that explain what drives people to play, how they learn
and interact with game systems, how they experience and regulate their emotions
while playing, and how cognitive biases can influence their decisions. The goal is to
provide designers with a robust arsenal of psychological knowledge to inform their
design choices, allowing them to anticipate player reactions, create appropriate
challenges, cultivate intrinsic motivation, and orchestrate resonant emotional
experiences. Through the analysis of established theories and exemplification with
concrete videogame cases, this chapter aims to promote empathetic, player-centered
design that is aware of the complexity of the human experience in the context of
gaming.


**2.1.** **Theories** **of** **Motivation** **Applied** **to** **Games:** **Self-**
**Determination** **(Deci** **&** **Ryan),** **Flow** **(Csikszentmihalyi),**
**Expectation-Value Theory (Eccles & Wigfield) and Needs Models**
**(Maslow, Murray)**


Motivation is the driving force that drives players to start a gaming experience, persist
in the face of difficulties, and return to a particular game over and over again.
Understanding the psychological roots of ludic motivation is therefore of primary
importance for game designers. Several psychological theories offer valuable
insights on this topic, and their conscious application can transform a game from a
simple pastime to a deeply rewarding experience. Among these, Self Determination
Theory (SDT) and Flow Theory are particularly influential in the context of game
design, but Expectation-Value Theory and hierarchical models of needs also provide
useful perspectives.


The **Theory of Self-Determination (SDT),** developed by **Edward L. Deci and**
**Richard M. Ryan (2000, 2008),** is a macro theory of human motivation that focuses
on the factors that promote intrinsic psychological growth, well-being, and optimal
motivation. SDT distinguishes between **intrinsic motivation**, i.e. acting for the
pleasure and satisfaction inherent in the activity itself (e.g. playing _The Legend of_
_Zelda: Tears of the Kingdom_, Nintendo, 2023, for the pure pleasure of exploration
and discovery), and **extrinsic motivation**, i.e. acting to obtain results that can be
separated from the activity, such as external rewards or the avoidance of
punishments (e.g. playing to unlock an achievement specific or to get a rare skin in
_Fortnite_ ). SDT posits that humans have three innate and universal psychological
needs, the satisfaction of which is essential for the flowering of intrinsic motivation
and the internalized integration of extrinsic motivation. These needs are:
**Competence**, the need to feel effective in one's interactions with the environment, to
master challenges and to experience a sense of growth and mastery (cf. **White, 1959**,
on the concept of "effectance motivation"). In games, this translates into beating
difficult levels in _Celeste_ (Matt Makes Games, 2018) after numerous attempts, or
mastering complex combos in _Street Fighter 6_ (Capcom, 2023). The second need is
**Autonomy**, or the need to perceive oneself as the origin of one's actions, to feel that
one's behaviors are volitional, self-approved and congruent with one's values and
interests, rather than controlled by external forces (cf. **deCharms, 1968** ). In games,


32


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


autonomy is supported by offering meaningful choices that impact narrative or
gameplay, such as moral decisions in _Mass Effect Legendary Edition_ (BioWare,
2021) that shape the fate of the galaxy, or the freedom to explore and build in
_Minecraft_ . The third need is **Relationality**, that is, the need to feel authentically
connected to others, to care for and be cared for, and to feel part of a community or
a group (cf. **Baumeister & Leary, 1995**, on the "need to belong"). In multiplayer
games, this need is met through cooperation in a _Final Fantasy XIV_ raid (Square
Enix, 2010), friendly competition, or simply socializing with other players in a hub like
the Tower of _Destiny 2_ (Bungie, 2017). Even single-player games can partially satisfy
this need through emotional connection with well-written characters or through
sharing experiences with an external fan community. **Ryan, Rigby and Przybylski**
**(2006)** have empirically demonstrated how video games are particularly effective in
motivating players precisely because they offer ample opportunities for the
satisfaction of these three needs. Scientifically informed game design, therefore,
should aim to create playful contexts that actively support competence (through
balanced challenges and clear feedback), autonomy (offering meaningful choices
and opportunities for expression), and relationality (facilitating positive social
interactions or emotional connections).


The **Flow Theory**, developed by **Mihaly Csikszentmihalyi (1990, 2008)** in his book
"Flow: The Psychology of Optimal Experience", describes a state of optimal
experience, a "feeling completely absorbed and focused in an activity, to the point of
losing track of time and of oneself" (Csikszentmihalyi, 1990, p. 4). Flow is inherently
rewarding and occurs when a person is engaged in an activity that presents a
dynamic and perceived balance between the level of challenge of the activity and the
individual's abilities. If the challenge is too low compared to the skills, you experience
boredom; If the challenge is too high, you experience anxiety or frustration. Flow sits
in a "channel" between these two states, where the challenge pushes slightly beyond
the current skill level, promoting learning and growth. Csikszentmihalyi identified
several characteristic components of the flow experience: clear goals, immediate
feedback, balance between challenge and skill, intense focus on the action, sense of
personal control, loss of self-awareness, altered perception of time, and the autotelic
experience (the activity is rewarding in itself). Video games are often cited as powerful
flow inducers, thanks to their ability to provide clear objectives (defeating a boss in
_Elden Ring_ ), constant feedback (the enemy's health bar dropping), and a level of
challenge that can, ideally, adapt or be chosen by the player. **Sweetser and Wyeth**
**(2005)** developed the "GameFlow" model, which specifically adapts the criteria of
flow to the context of video games, and **Chen (2007)** explored how challenge
balancing is crucial. To design for flow, game designers should set clear goals at
various levels, provide continuous and unambiguous feedback, design a difficulty
curve that increases progressively (see Chapter 5.3), minimize interruptions, and
offer the player a strong sense of agency and control. An example of a game that
excels at inducing flow is _Tetris_, where the increasing challenge (the speed at which
blocks fall) adapts (within certain limits) to the player's increasing ability to manipulate
and place tetrominos.


Expectancy **Value Theory**, developed mainly by **Jacquelynne Eccles and Allan**
**Wigfield (Eccles et al., 1983; Wigfield & Eccles, 2000),** postulates that choice,
persistence, and performance in an activity are influenced by two main factors: the
individual's **expectations of success** (his or her belief that he or she can succeed in
