that task) and the value that the individual places on that task (how important, useful,
or enjoyable he or she considers it). In games, a player will be more motivated to take
on a difficult quest if they believe they have a reasonable chance of success


33


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


(expectation) and if the reward (material or intrinsic) or the experience itself is
perceived as highly valuable (value). Designers can influence expectations of
success through good onboarding, clear tutorials, and a difficulty curve that allows
the player to build a sense of self-efficacy (cf. **Bandura, 1997** ). Value can be
increased through meaningful rewards, engaging storytelling, or connecting the game
activity to the player's personal goals. For example, a player might persevere in a
long, repetitive grind in an MMORPG if the expectation of obtaining a piece of
legendary gear is high enough and the value placed on that item (for its power or the
status it confers) is high.


Finally, **more general models of needs**, such as Abraham **Maslow's (1943)**
**classical hierarchy of needs or** Henry Murray's (1938) list **of psychogenic needs**,
may offer further insights. Although Maslow's hierarchy (physiological, security,
belonging, esteem, self-actualization) has been criticized for its rigidity, the idea that
higher-level needs (such as self-actualization or esteem) only become motivating
after the satisfaction of the most basic ones can have some resonance. For example,
a player frustrated by constant technical problems (lack of "security" of the system)
will hardly be able to appreciate the higher aspects of the design. Murray's needs,
such as the need for achievements (nAch), affiliation (nAff), and power (nPow), have
been directly linked to game preferences and can inform the design of experiences
that meet specific motivational profiles (see also player taxonomies, Chapter 2.2).


In conclusion, player motivation is a multidimensional construct. A scientific approach
to game design requires understanding these different motivational theories and the
ability to translate them into concrete design choices. It is not a matter of blindly
applying a single theory, but of using this rich body of psychological knowledge as a
"toolbox" to analyze, prototype and iterate on game systems that are capable of
generating and sustaining deep, meaningful and, ideally, intrinsically rewarding
engagement for a wide range of players. Empirical validation of the impact of design
choices on motivation, through methods such as standardized questionnaires (e.g.,
PENS, Flow Short Scale), qualitative interviews, or behavioral analyses (telemetry),
is a crucial step in this scientific process.


**2.2. Player Types and Playstyles: Beyond Bartle – Empirical**
**Models (e.g. Yee, Quantic Foundry), Customization and Design**
**Adaptability**


Recognizing that players are not a monolithic group, but have a wide range of
preferences, motivations, and interaction styles, is a crucial step for effective and
inclusive game design. Player **taxonomies** attempt to categorize this diversity,
offering designers heuristics, or simplified models, to think about their audiences and
to design experiences that can resonate with different types of users. While no
taxonomy is universally accepted or without limitations, they are a useful starting
point, especially when integrated with the latest empirical research and a flexible
approach to design.


One of the most well-known and historically influential taxonomies, especially in the
context of online multiplayer games, is the one proposed by **Richard Bartle (1996)**
in his article "Hearts, Clubs, Diamonds, Spades: Players Who Suit MUDs". Based on
his observations of Multi User Dungeons (MUD) players, Bartle identified four main
types of players, defined by two axes: the preference for **action** versus **interaction**,


34


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


and the preference for interaction with the **game world** versus interaction with other
**players** . The four types are the **Achievers**, motivated by the achievement of in-game
goals, the accumulation of status, points or virtual wealth, and therefore focused on
acting on the world; the **Explorers**, interested in discovering all aspects of the game
world, including secrets, hidden mechanics, lore and geography, and therefore
focused on interacting with the world; the **Socializers** (Socializers), primarily
interested in interacting with other players, forming relationships, communicating,
collaborating and being part of a community, therefore focused on interacting with
other players; and **Killers** (Assassins, or more broadly Competitive/Destroyers),
motivated by imposing their will on other players, often through direct competition,
conflict, overpowering or disturbing the game of others (griefing), therefore focused
on acting on other players. Bartle's model, although originally conceived for textbased MUDs, has been widely discussed and applied, often somewhat anecdotally,
to modern graphical games as well. For example, in an MMORPG like _Final Fantasy_
_XIV_, you can identify players who focus on completing all achievements (Achievers),
others who spend hours exploring every corner of Eorzea and reading every lore
fragment (Explorers), still others whose main fun comes from belonging to a Free
Company and participating in social events (Socializers), and finally players who
excel in PvP modes or who try to dominate the server economy (Killers/Competitors).
However, Bartle's model has received criticism for its potential rigidity and the fact
that real players often exhibit features of multiple types at once or change their
preferences over time or depending on the specific game context (cf. **Taylor, 2006**,
"Play Between Worlds"). The Bartle Test of Gamer Psychology, an online
questionnaire based on this taxonomy, has sought to provide an empirical
measurement, but its psychometric validity has been a matter of debate.


In an attempt to overcome some limitations of previous models and to base the
understanding of player motivations on a more solid empirical basis, **Nick Yee (2006,**
**2007),** through large-scale factor analysis of data collected from MMORPG players,
developed the **Gamer Motivation Profile** model, which later evolved into the work
of **Quantic Foundry** (co-founded by Yee and Nic Ducheneaut). Instead of discrete
categories, this approach identifies different **motivational components** that can
combine in different ways to form unique player profiles. Yee's early research
identified three main components: **Achievement** (advancement, mechanics,
competition), **Social** (socialization, relationship, teamwork) and **Immersion**
(discovery, role-playing, customization, escapism). The most recent model from
Quantic Foundry (cf. **Yee, Ducheneaut, & Nelson, 2012** ; and the Quantic Foundry
website) articulates these motivations into a more granular framework, often
represented by six main clusters, each with two subcomponents: **Action**
(Destruction, Excitement), **Social** (Competition, Community), **Mastery** (Challenge,
Strategy), **Achievement** (Completion, Power), **Creativity** (Design, Discovery) and
**Immersion** (Fantasy, History). This data-driven approach allows for the creation of
more nuanced and personalized motivational profiles, recognizing that a player may
be highly motivated, for example, by both competition (Social) and strategy (Mastery),
but little interested in the story (Immersion). Designers can use these profiles to better
understand their target audience or to design games that offer different "ways to fun"
for different types of motivations. For example, a game like _Minecraft_ manages to
attract players with very different motivational profiles: some are driven by Creativity
(building complex structures), others by Discovery (exploring the infinite world),
others by Challenge (surviving in hardcore mode) or Community (playing on
multiplayer servers).


Another useful perspective is offered by **Amy Jo Kim (2000)** in her book "Community


35


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


Building on the Web: Secret Strategies for Successful Online Communities".
Although not a taxonomy of intrinsic game types, Kim proposes an evolutionary
model of the player's life cycle within an online community or game as a service,
identifying four main stages or roles that players can go through: **Visitors**, who are
newcomers, curious, exploring the environment without a strong initial commitment
and need clear and welcoming onboarding; **Novices** (Novices), who have begun to
learn the basics of the game and participate more actively, but who still require
guidance, support, and a sense of progress in order not to give up; **Regulars**, who
are stable, competent, and community-integrated participants, and who form the core
of the active player base, often seeking new challenges and content; and **Leaders**
(or Elders/Veterans), who are experienced, deeply engaged, and often highly
influential players, who can take on roles of leadership, mentoring, user-generated
content creation (UGC), or community management. This model is particularly
relevant for designers of persistent online games, F2P mobile games, or any game
that aims to build a lasting community, as it emphasizes the need to design systems
and features that support players at every stage of their "journey" within the game,
from first impression through to long-term engagement and potential contribution to
the life of the community. For example, a good guild system in an MMORPG should
benefit both Novices (support and learning) as well as Regulars (group activities) and
Leaders (management and recognition tools).


Finally, Nicole **Lazzaro's (2004)** Four Keys to Fun model, based on qualitative
research that observed players' facial expressions and emotional reactions, offers a
complementary perspective, focusing on the different emotional "textures" of fun
rather than on player types. These keys are: **Easy Fun** (or Fiero Fun, fun resulting
from curiosity, exploration, discovery, novelty, often related to immersion and roleplaying – e.g. exploring a new biome in _Subnautica_, Unknown Worlds Entertainment,
2018); **Hard Fun** (or Proud Fun, fun that arises from overcoming significant
challenges, mastering skills, strategy and achieving difficult goals, generating
feelings of triumph and accomplishment – e.g. defeating a particularly difficult boss
in a game in the _Souls series_ ); **People Fun** (fun that comes from interacting with
other people, cooperation, friendly competition, friendship, and shared fun – e.g.
playing a chaotic game of _Overcooked! 2_, Ghost Town Games, 2018, with friends);
and **Serious Fun** (or Altered States, fun related to change and impact, such as
relaxing, feeling intense excitement, or using the game for a greater purpose such as
learning, reflection or social change – e.g. the cathartic experience of _Gris_, Nomada
Studio, 2018, or the impact of a "serious game" such as _This War of Mine_ ). This
model encourages designers to consider a wider range of emotional experiences and
design games that can offer different types of fun, potentially appealing to different
player preferences or different moments within the same gaming experience.


In approaching these taxonomies and models, it is essential for a scientifically
oriented designer to avoid their dogmatic application. The reality of player psychology
is complex and dynamic. Preferences can vary depending on the context, the specific
game, the mood of the moment or the stage of the player's life. In addition, the
empirical validity and generalizability of some of the older taxonomies have been the
subject of scientific debate (cf. **Hamari & Tuunanen, 2014**, for a critical review).
Therefore, these models should be used primarily as **heuristics** or "lenses" (in
Schell's sense) to stimulate reflection and idea generation, rather than as rigid and
immutable categories. A more robust approach involves integrating these insights
with the deeper motivational models discussed in the previous section (such as SDT),
conducting **specific research on your target audience** (through surveys,
interviews, game data analysis – see Chapter 8) to understand their unique


36


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


motivations and preferences, and, where possible, **designing for flexibility and**
**personalization** . This could mean offering players multiple paths to success,
different supported playstyles, varying difficulty options, or even game systems that
dynamically adapt to the player's preferences or behavior (see Chapter 9 on adaptive
game design). Ultimately, the goal is not to pigeonhole players, but to understand the
diversity of their gaming aspirations to create richer, more inclusive and personally
meaningful experiences.


**2.3. Fundamental Cognitive Processes in Gameplay: Implicit and**
**Explicit Learning (Reber, Squire), Selective and Sustained**
**Attention (Posner), Working and Long-Term Memory (Baddeley),**
**Cognitive Load (Sweller) and Strategic Problem Solving (Newell**
**& Simon)**


Video games, in their essence, are deeply cognitive experiences. They require and
at the same time stimulate a wide range of mental processes, from learning new rules
and skills, to managing attention in dynamic environments, to using memory to
remember crucial information, to solving complex problems. A scientifically informed
game designer must possess a solid understanding of these cognitive processes that
are fundamental to creating experiences that are not only stimulating and engaging,
but also accessible, understandable and capable of promoting a sense of mastery
and intellectual satisfaction in the player.


Learning is intrinsic to the gaming experience. Each time a player approaches a new
game, they must learn its rules, mechanics, affordances of its world, and strategies
in order to succeed. Cognitive psychology distinguishes between **explicit learning**,
which is conscious, deliberate and often verbalizable (e.g. reading a manual,
following a tutorial that explains a mechanic), and **implicit learning**, which occurs in
a non-conscious way, through direct experience and the extraction of patterns and
regularities from the environment, without the subject necessarily being able to
verbalize what he has learned (cf. **Reber, 1967, 1993** ; **Squire, 2004**, on the
distinction between declarative and explicit memory systems). Many games excel at
facilitating implicit learning. For example, the level design of the first world of _Super_
_Mario Bros._ (Nintendo, 1985) is a masterpiece of implicit teaching: the player learns
to jump, hit blocks and defeat enemies through direct experimentation in a carefully
structured environment, without the need for explicit text instructions. **James Paul**
**Gee (2003),** in his influential "What Video Games Have to Teach Us About Learning
and Literacy," identified a number of effective "learning principles" that are often
incorporated into the design of successful video games. These include the principle
of " **active and critical identity** " (the player takes an active role and learns through
that lens), the principle of " **just in time** " and " **on demand** " (information and skills are
presented when they are immediately needed or when the player requests them,
making them more meaningful), and the concept of " **expertise** cycles"" (Games
structure learning through repeated cycles of practice, feedback, and new
challenges, allowing players to gradually develop mastery.) An example of the latter
can be found in games such as FromSoftware's Souls series, where the player learns
the attack patterns of enemies and bosses through repeated failures (feedback) and
subsequent attempts, gradually honing their strategy and skills. Designers can apply
these principles by designing tutorials integrated into the gameplay ("stealth
tutorials"), gradual learning curves (see Chapter 5.3), clear and timely feedback
systems, and offering opportunities for experimentation in safe environments
("sandboxes").


37


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


Attention is a limited and selective cognitive resource, crucial for information
processing and effective interaction with the game world (cf. **Kahneman, 1973**,
"Attention and Effort"; **Posner & Petersen, 1990**, on attentional systems). Games,
with their wealth of visual, auditory and interactive stimuli, constantly compete for the
player's attention. Effective design must be able to **capture initial attention** through
salient stimuli (e.g., an unexpected event, a captivating visual design, a mystery to
be solved), **maintain sustained attention** over time by varying stimuli, introducing
new goals and providing a constant stream of interesting feedback, and **guide**
**selective** attention of the player to the most relevant elements of the interface or
game environment at any given time. This can be done through the use of visual cues
(highlighting, arrows, color contrast, directional lighting, such as the "guide light" in
_Dead Space_, E.A. Redwood Shores, 2008, which indicates the path to the target),
auditory cues (sound cues that indicate a danger or an important object nearby), or
the composition of the scene itself. It is equally important **to manage the** player's
cognitive load, i.e. the total amount of mental effort spent in working memory to
process information and perform the required actions (cf. Sweller's Cognitive Load
Theory **, 1988, 1994** ). Excessive cognitive load, caused by too much information
presented simultaneously, complex interfaces, or mechanics that are difficult to
understand, can lead to frustration, errors, and ultimately abandonment of the game.
Designers must therefore carefully balance the complexity of the information, the
clarity of the interface (see Chapter 7), and the number of elements the player needs
to pay attention to at once, especially in high-intensity games such as RTS or flight
simulators.


Memory plays a crucial role in games, both short-term (working memory) and longterm. **Working Memory**, as described by the model of **Baddeley and Hitch (1974)**
and subsequent elaborations (cf. **Baddeley, 2000** ), is a limited-capacity system
responsible for the temporary maintenance and manipulation of information
necessary for the performance of complex cognitive tasks. In games, working
memory is constantly engaged in understanding rules, planning next moves,
remembering the location of objects or enemies, or keeping track of short-term goals.
Its limited capacity (often summed up in **Miller's famous remark, 1956**, about the
"magic number seven, plus or minus two", although today we tend to consider the
capacity in terms of information "chunks" rather than individual items) means that
designers must avoid requiring players to keep in mind too much information not
supported by the interface. The user interface (HUD, inventory, map, quest diary) can
and should act as an "external memory", displaying relevant information and reducing
the load on the player's working memory. For example, a well-organized inventory in
an RPG like _Divinity: Original Sin II_ (Larian Studios, 2017) helps the player manage
a large amount of items without having to memorize them all. **Long term memory** is
the vast and relatively permanent repository of knowledge, skills, experiences and
autobiographical episodes. Games promote the consolidation of information in longterm memory through mechanisms such as meaningful repetition (the practice of a
mechanic), the deep processing of information (linking new knowledge to pre-existing
ones, cf. **Craik & Lockhart, 1972**, on the theory of processing levels), the emotional
experience associated with learning (emotionally charged events are often better
remembered, cf. **Cahill & McGaugh, 1995** ), and the creation of "patterns" or mental
models of the game. Character skill progression, detailed map knowledge of a vast
game world like Grand _Theft Auto V_, remembering crucial narrative events, or
mastering complex strategies in a collectible card game like _Magic: The Gathering_
_Arena_ (Wizards of the Coast, 2019) are all examples of information and abilities
stored in long-term memory.


38


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


Finally, many games, in their essence, are **problem-solving** systems. As defined by
**Newell and Simon (1972)** in their theory of problem solving as searching in a state
space, players must identify an initial state (the problem), a desired end state (the
solution or goal), and a set of operators (the game mechanics) to transform the initial
state into the final state, often navigating through a space of possible intermediate
states. Problems in games can vary enormously in terms of structure and complexity:
from **well-defined problems** (with initial, final and clear operators, such as a Sudoku
puzzle or most environmental puzzles in _The Talos Principle_, Croteam, 2014) to
**poorly defined problems** (where one or more components of the problem are not
clearly specified, requiring the player to define the problem itself before it can be
solved, such as "how to survive and thrive?" in a sandbox game like _Don't Starve_,
Klei Entertainment, 2013). Some problems require insight, i.e. a sudden restructuring
of the representation of the problem that leads to a solution that seems obvious only
in retrospect (cf. **Wertheimer, 1959** ; **Ohlsson, 1992** ); many classic puzzle
adventures, such as those in the _Myst_ series (Cyan Worlds, 1993), are based on
moments of insight. To effectively support problem solving, game designers should:
present problems with an appropriate, progressive level of difficulty (see Vygotsky's
Proximal Development Zone **, 1978** ); provide the necessary tools, information and
feedback (or allow the player to discover them through exploration and
experimentation); allowing constructive failure, where error is seen as a learning
opportunity rather than as a punishment for its own sake; and encourage lateral
thinking, creativity, and flexible application of learned knowledge and skills. Games
like _Baba Is You_ (Hempuli Oy, 2019) take problem solving to a meta level, where the
very rules of the game can be manipulated by the player to come up with innovative
solutions.


An in-depth understanding of these cognitive processes – learning, attention,
memory and problem solving – and the psychological theories that explain them,
allows designers to create playful experiences that are not only fun and engaging,
but that respect and exploit human cognitive abilities effectively. This leads to games
that are easier to learn (but not necessarily easy to master), that maintain attention
without generating overload, that support the player's memory through intelligent
interface and information flow design, and that offer challenging and deeply
rewarding problem-solving challenges. Ongoing research in cognitive psychology
and HCI offers an inexhaustible reservoir of principles and guidelines (e.g., Nielsen's
usability heuristics **, 1993; Mayer'** s principles of multimedia design **, 2009** ) that can be
scientifically applied to optimize cognitive interaction with gaming systems and to
elevate the quality of the player experience.


**2.4. Emotions in Game Design: Theoretical Models (Lazarus and**
**Scherer Appraisals, Russell Dimensional Models), Emotional**
**Design** **(Norman),** **Affectivity** **Measurement** **(Self** **report,**
**Physiological, Behavioral) and Creation of Emotional Arcs**


Emotions are the connective tissue of the human experience and, in the context of
video games, they represent a central and very powerful component that can
determine the involvement, memorability and overall impact of a play work. Games,
through their mechanics, narratives, aesthetics and social interactions, are able to
evoke a wide and complex range of affective states, from the exultant joy of victory
to the scorching frustration of failure, from paralyzing fear to the euphoric wonder of
discovery, from deep sadness for the loss of a beloved character to pride in an
arduous task accomplished. Consciously designing for emotional impact, or


39


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


"emotional design," is therefore crucial to creating experiences that resonate deeply
with players and go beyond mere superficial entertainment. A scientific approach to
this aspect of design requires an understanding of the psychological patterns of
emotions, techniques for measuring affectivity, and strategies for orchestrating
meaningful emotional arcs.


**Donald Norman (2004),** in his influential book "Emotional Design: Why We Love (or
Hate) Everyday Things", although not focused exclusively on games, provides a
useful framework for understanding how people respond emotionally to products,
distinguishing three levels of cognitive processing that interact with each other. The
**visceral level** concerns the immediate, preconscious and automatic reactions to the
aesthetic and sensory aspect of a product. In games, stunning graphics like those in
_Ghost of Tsushima_ (Sucker Punch Productions, 2020), impactful sound effects, and
pleasant haptic feedback (like that offered by the PlayStation 5's DualSense
controller) can generate a positive visceral response, an almost instinctive attraction.
The **behavioral level** refers to the pleasure and effectiveness of use, usability,
functionality, comprehensibility, and the feeling of control that the player experiences
when interacting with the mechanics and interface. A game with responsive and
precise controls like _Hollow Knight_, a clear user interface, and well-functioning
mechanics that allow the player to feel competent and in control, generates
satisfaction at this level. The flow experience (see Section 2.1) is strongly linked to
good behavioral design. Finally, the **reflective level** concerns the conscious
interpretation, meaning, personal and cultural value we attribute to a product or
experience. It's about self-image, memories, the satisfaction of overcoming a
significant challenge, the message that play conveys, and how it relates to our life
experiences. In games, this level is triggered by complex and poignant narratives
such as that of _The Last of Us Part II,_ by deep themes that stimulate reflection, by
the possibility of personal expression through meaningful choices, by the sense of
belonging to a community or by the meaning that the player attributes to their
successes, failures and the relationships formed in the game world. Truly effective
game design considers and orchestrates all three of these levels, creating an
experience that is both aesthetically satisfying, functionally excellent, and personally
meaningful.


To understand how emotions are aroused, psychology offers several theoretical
models. **Theories of cognitive appraisal**, such as those proposed by **Richard**
**Lazarus (1991)** or **Klaus Scherer (Scherer, Schorr, & Johnstone, 2001),** argue
that emotions are not simple reactions to stimuli, but emerge from an appraisal
process that the individual performs regarding an event or situation in relation to his
or her goals, values, beliefs, and resources to cope with it. In the gaming context, a
player will continuously evaluate the events of the game (e.g., encountering a
powerful enemy, finding a rare item, failing a critical mission, receiving a compliment
from another player) based on how they affect their game goals and perceived wellbeing. These evaluations (e.g., "is this event a threat or an opportunity?", "is it
relevant to my goals?", "do I have the resources to deal with it?", "is it congruent with
my values?") determine the nature and intensity of the emotional response. For
example, the appearance of a boss in _Elden Ring_ will be rated as highly relevant and
potentially threatening to the progression goal, likely generating anxiety or excitement
depending on the player's perception of self-efficacy. A game designer can influence
this appraisal process by structuring game events, providing information (or
strategically retaining it), and modulating difficulty to elicit specific emotional
responses.


40


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


Dimensional **models of emotions**, such as James **Russell's (1980)** circumplex
model, describe emotions along two or more continuous dimensions, typically
**valence** (pleasure/displeasure) and **arousal** (physiological activation/deactivation).
According to this model, different emotions can be mapped into a two-dimensional
space: for example, arousal is high arousal and high valence, anger is high arousal
and low valence, sadness is low arousal and low valence, and relaxation is low
arousal and high valence. Game designers can use this model to think about how to
vary the intensity and emotional quality of the gaming experience, for example by
creating high-arousal sequences (fast-paced combat, chases) alternating with
moments of calm and reflection (exploration of a peaceful environment, introspective
dialogues). Games like _Journey_ (Thatgamecompany, 2012) are masters at
modulating arousal and emotional valence through level design, music, and
minimalist interactions.


Game designers have a wide range of tools at their disposal to orchestrate emotional
impact: the **game mechanics** themselves (the intrinsic challenge, risk, reward, loss,
competition, cooperation are all emotionally charged); the **narrative and characters**
(engaging stories, identifying, or charismatic antagonists can generate a strong
emotional investment, as discussed by **Oatley, 1999**, on the role of emotions in the
fruition of fiction); the **audiovisual aesthetics** (music, sound effects, color palettes,
character design and the composition of the environments contribute powerfully to
the emotional atmosphere, as demonstrated by the use of sound and darkness to
generate fear in _Amnesia: The Dark Descent_ ); stimulation **and pacing** of experience
(the alternation of moments of high tension and periods of calm, the management of
the narrative and game rhythm, influence the arousal and the overall emotional
experience, as in the skilful pacing of a Hitchcock film or a game like _Uncharted 4_ );
feedback (the way the game communicates successes and failures can modulate
the intensity and valence of the emotions felt, see Chapter 4.4); and **social**
**interaction** (interpersonal dynamics in multiplayer games are a primary source of
complex and intense emotional experiences, from the joy of team victory to the
frustration of an uncooperative teammate).


Measuring **affectivity** and emotions in the context of play is a complex
methodological challenge, but crucial for evidence-based design. Multimodal
approaches can be used:


  - **Self report:** Standardized questionnaires such as the Game Experience
Questionnaire (GEQ) ( **IJsselsteijn, de Kort, & Poels, 2013** ), which
includes scales for positive and negative affect, or dimensional scales such
as the Self Assessment Manikin (SAM) ( **Bradley & Lang, 1994** ) to measure
valence, arousal, and dominance. Qualitative interviews can explore the
emotional experience in greater depth.

  - **Physiological Measures (Biometrics):** As discussed in Section 1.4 and
Chapter 9.5, heart rate (ECG/PPG), heart rate variability (HRV), galvanic
skin response (GSR/EDA), facial electromyography (fEMG), and
electroencephalogram (EEG) can provide objective indicators of arousal,
stress, or specific emotional responses, although their interpretation
requires caution and contextualization (cf. **Nacke & Grimshaw, 2011** ).

  - **Behavioral Measures:** The analysis of facial expressions (manual or
automated by facial recognition software, cf. **Ekman & Friesen, 1978** ), body
language, tone of voice, or specific play patterns (e.g., increased error or
avoidance behavior may indicate frustration or fear).


41


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


Finally, game designers can aim to create **emotional arcs** within the game
experience, which are structured sequences of events and interactions designed to
guide the player through a progression of emotional states. This is especially relevant
in narrative games, where the protagonist's emotional arc can mirror or influence the
player's. Screenwriting techniques such as the three-act structure, the introduction of
turning points, moments of crisis, and resolution can be adapted to the interactive
context to create richer and more memorable emotional experiences. Games like
_What Remains of Edith Finch_ (Giant Sparrow, 2017) use a series of playable
vignettes, each with a distinctive emotional tone, to build a complex and poignant
emotional arc over the entire Finch family.


Designing for emotions requires deep empathy for the player, a solid understanding
of human psychology, a mastery of design tools (mechanical, narrative, aesthetic),
and a commitment to iterative testing to assess and refine the emotional impact of
the experience. It is an area where the science of game design blends intimately with
the art of creating meaningful human experiences.


**2.5. Cognitive Biases, Heuristics (Kahneman & Tversky) and**
**Irrational Player Behavior: Design Implications (e.g., reward**
**systems, perceived difficulty) and Ethical Considerations**
**(manipulation prevention)**


Contrary to an idealized model of a perfectly rational decision-maker, humans,
including gamers, are subject to a series of **cognitive biases** (systematic errors of
thought) and **heuristics** (mental shortcuts) that influence their judgments, decisions,
and behaviors. The pioneering work of psychologists such as **Daniel Kahneman and**
**Amos Tversky (Kahneman, Slovic, & Tversky, 1982; Kahneman, 2011)** has
revolutionized our understanding of bounded rationality and identified numerous
patterns of "irrational" but predictable thinking. Kahneman, in "Thinking, Fast and
Slow" (2011), popularized the distinction between two systems of thought: **System**
**1**, which operates quickly, automatically, intuitively, emotionally, and often
unconsciously, based on heuristics; and **System 2**, which is slower, deliberative,
analytical, logical, and requires cognitive effort. Many of the decisions players make
during gameplay, especially those that are quick or under pressure, are dominated
by System 1 and its shortcuts, which can lead to specific biases.


Understanding these cognitive biases is of paramount importance for game
designers, not only to avoid exploiting them in an ethically questionable way, but also
to design more intuitive, engaging experiences and, in some cases, to use the
knowledge of these biases in a positive way, for example to improve the perception
of fairness or to make learning more effective, always with careful consideration of
the ethical implications.


Numerous cognitive and heuristic biases have direct relevance to game design.
**Confirmation Bias**, for example, describes the tendency to seek, interpret, favor and
recall information in order to confirm or support one's pre-existing beliefs or
hypotheses, ignoring or devaluing contrary evidence (cf. **Nickerson, 1998** ). In
games, a player who believes that a certain strategy is the best may selectively
interpret the results of his actions to reinforce this belief, even in the face of evidence
suggesting more effective alternatives. A designer might, for example, notice that
players tend to prefer a certain type of weapon in a shooter, even though objective


42


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


stats show others that perform better in certain situations, due to the confirmation
bias fueled by some initial successes with that weapon.


The **Anchoring and Adjustment Heuristic** describes how people tend to overrely
on the first piece of information offered (the "anchor") when making decisions or
making judgments, and then adjust insufficiently from that starting point (cf. **Tversky**
**& Kahneman, 1974** ). In virtual economy design, the initial price shown for an item in
an in-game store can act as a powerful anchor, influencing the perception of that
item's value and the prices of subsequent items. For example, showing a very
expensive object (even if few will buy it) can make other objects, presented later,
seem more convenient in contrast.


The **Availability Heuristic** refers to the tendency to overestimate the probability of
events that are more easily recalled, often because they are recent, vivid or
emotionally charged (cf. **Tversky & Kahneman, 1973** ). If a player has just suffered
a series of particularly frustrating defeats against a certain type of enemy in _Dark_
_Souls_, they may overestimate the inherent difficulty of that enemy or the frequency
with which it appears, affecting their future strategic decisions or emotional state.
Designers can exploit (or should be aware of) this bias in how they present
information or structure game events.


Gambler **'s Fallacy** is the mistaken belief that if a particular random event occurs
more frequently than normal during a period, then it will occur less frequently in the
future (or vice versa), even when the events are statistically independent. This is
especially relevant for probability-based reward systems, such as "loot drops" or "loot
boxes". A player who has just received several low-rarity items may mistakenly
believe that the next opening of a loot box has a higher probability of containing a
rare item, even if the odds remain constant. This bias can fuel repetitive gaming
behaviors and, in monetization contexts, overspending.


The **Dunning-Kruger Effect (Dunning & Kruger, 1999)** is a cognitive bias whereby
people with poor skill or knowledge in a given task tend to overestimate their
competence, while, conversely, highly competent individuals may underestimate
their relative ability. In games, beginner players may believe that they are more skilled
than they actually are, leading them to underestimate challenges or feel frustration
when their results do not match their self-perceived expectations. A good tutorial
design and a progressive difficulty curve can help to better calibrate the player's selfevaluation.


**Loss Aversion**, a central concept of **Kahneman and Tversky's (1979)** Prospect
Theory, describes the psychological tendency whereby people perceive the
emotional impact of losses more intensely (about twice as much) than gains of
equivalent magnitude. This has profound implications for the design of risk and
reward systems. Losing resources, progress, or hard-earned items in a game can be
a much more negative and demotivating experience than the equivalent gain would
have been positive. Games like _EVE Online_, with its permanent ship destruction
mechanics, intensively exploit loss aversion to create tension and meaning.
Designers must carefully balance the loss mechanics to avoid excessive frustration,
unless such frustration is an intentional design goal (as in some "masocore" games).


The **Endowment Effect**, closely related to loss aversion, is the tendency to
overestimate the value of an object simply because one owns it (cf. **Thaler, 1980** ).


43


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


Players may be reluctant to trade or sell items they have obtained and become "fond
of", even if the offer is objectively advantageous from the point of view of the in-game
market value. This can affect the dynamics of virtual economies and crafting or
upgrade systems.


The **Sunk Cost Fallacy** describes the tendency to continue a behavior, investment,
or effort as a result of previous investments (time, money, emotional energy), even
though current perspectives suggest that abandoning would be the most rational
choice. **Arkes & Blumer, 1985** ). In games, this can manifest itself with players
continuing to play a title they no longer find fun, or investing in a character or strategy
that has proven ineffective, simply because they have already put in too much time
or resources to "throw everything away". This bias can contribute to prolonged
engagement but qualitatively poor or even harmful to the player's well-being. F2P
business models, with their long progression cycles and buying opportunities to
accelerate, can sometimes leverage this bias.


**Hindsight bias** is the tendency to perceive past events as more predictable than
they actually were before they happened ("I always knew that!"). After overcoming a
difficult challenge or figuring out the solution to a complex puzzle, players may
retrospectively underestimate the initial difficulty or their own confusion, which can
affect their feedback when questioned about the experience.


The implications of these (and many other) cognitive biases for game design are
pervasive. When designing tutorials **and learning systems**, it is important to present
information in a way that counteracts confirmation bias, encouraging the exploration
of different strategies and questioning one's own assumptions. In **reward and**
**monetization systems**, designers need to be extremely aware of the impact of loss
aversion, the endowment effect, and the gambler's fallacy, especially when
implementing mechanics that are probability-based or encourage real money
spending. Transparency about probabilities (as required by some loot box
regulations) is a key ethical step. When designing **feedback and difficulty**, it is
helpful to provide players with information that helps them accurately calibrate their
skills, counteracting the Dunning Kruger effect, and understand the nature of
randomness, if any. The **user interface** can be designed to make the desired options
more "available" and to guide decisions intuitively, but always avoiding deceptive
patterns. In **storytelling and choices**, the anchoring effect can influence how players
perceive early information about a character or situation, and designers can use this
awareness to structure the revelation of information.


A scientifically informed and ethically responsible game designer is not only aware of
these biases, but actively reflects on how their design choices might interact with
them. The primary goal should not be to manipulate the player to maximize metrics
such as playing time or spending, but rather to create experiences that are fair,
transparent, respectful of the player's psychology, and that, ideally, can even help
players become more aware of their own thought processes. Research in the field of
behavioral economics and the psychology of judgment and decision-making, such as
that of **Dan Ariely (2008)** in "Predictably Irrational", offers a rich body of knowledge
that can inform more conscious game design. Testing with users, by carefully
observing their decision-making processes, their verbalizations (think aloud protocol)
and collecting targeted qualitative feedback, can help identify where cognitive biases
could negatively or unexpectedly affect the gaming experience, allowing designers
to iterate towards more ethical and effective solutions.


44


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


**2.6.** **Conclusions:** **Towards** **a** **Holistic** **and** **Dynamic**
**Understanding of the Player**


This chapter explored the complex and multifaceted psychology of the player, a
domain of knowledge indispensable for anyone aspiring to design gaming
experiences with a scientific and user-centered approach. We've looked at how
fundamental theories of motivation, such as Self-Determination Theory and Flow
Theory, can provide a framework for understanding what drives players to action and
what makes an experience inherently rewarding. The satisfaction of the needs of
competence, autonomy and relationality, together with the search for an optimal
balance between challenge and ability, emerge as pillars for a design that cultivates
deep engagement and well-being.


We then looked at different player taxonomies, from Bartle's classic taxonomies to
more recent empirical models such as those of Yee and Quantic Foundry,
recognizing their usefulness as heuristics for thinking about audience diversity, but
also emphasizing the need to move beyond rigid categorizations in favor of a more
nuanced and dynamic understanding of individual preferences and playstyles.
Designing for flexibility and personalization, informed by specific research on one's
target, appears to be an increasingly promising direction.


The analysis of fundamental cognitive processes: learning, attention, memory and
problem solving has shown how games are intrinsically linked to the abilities and
limits of the human mind. Understanding how players learn new rules and skills (both
implicitly and explicitly), how they manage their attention in stimulating environments,
how they use working and long-term memory, and how they approach solving
complex problems, allows designers to create more intuitive interactions, effectively
manage cognitive load, and structure challenges that are both challenging and
surmountable.


The emotional dimension of the gaming experience was addressed through the
examination of psychological models of emotions, such as appraisal theories and
dimensional models, and through Norman's concept of emotional design. We have
seen how mechanics, narrative, audiovisual aesthetics, pacing and social interaction
are all powerful tools in the designer's hands to orchestrate meaningful emotional
arcs and to evoke a wide range of affective states. The measurement of affectivity,
through a multimodal approach that integrates self-report, physiological data and
behavioral observations, is crucial for evidence-based emotional design.


Finally, the discussion on cognitive biases and heuristics has highlighted how the
player's thinking, far from always being perfectly rational, is often guided by mental
shortcuts and subject to systematic errors. Awareness of these "predictably irrational"
patterns of thinking is critical for designers, both to avoid implementing manipulative
or ethically questionable mechanics, and to design experiences that take into account
these human tendencies in a constructive and transparent way.


In summary, this chapter wanted to emphasize that a holistic and dynamic
understanding of the player that integrates his motivations, his cognitive processes,
his emotional responses and his decision-making peculiarities is the cornerstone of
a scientific game design. It is not a question of reducing the player to a set of variables
to be optimized, but rather of cultivating a deep empathy and a scientific curiosity


45


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


towards the complexity of the human experience in the context of the game. Armed
with this psychological knowledge, the game designer is better equipped to create
interactive worlds that not only entertain, but can also enrich, stimulate, connect, and
ultimately deeply respect those for whom they were created: the players.


46


GAME DESIGN MANUAL

# **Chapter 3**

### **Game Mechanics: Design,** **Analysis and Systematic** **Innovation**


_Game design is the act of deciding how something should be; So, try to make it_

_happen and see what happens_


_**-Raph Koster-**_


47


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


Game **mechanics** are the beating heart of any interactive gaming experience. They
constitute the set of actions that players can perform, the rules that govern these
actions and the systems that determine their consequences within the game world.
They are, in essence, the "verbs" of the ludic language, the tools through which the
players interact with the game system and pursue their goals. The design, analysis
and systematic innovation of mechanics are therefore central and fundamental
activities for the game designer. This chapter will delve into the formal analysis of
mechanics, exploring rigorous operational definitions, their fundamental properties
such as interactivity and impact on the state of the game, and several advanced
taxonomies proposed by figures such as Sicart, Cook, and Björk & Holopainen.
Conceptual models such as the MDA (Mechanics, Dynamics, Aesthetics) framework
and other analytical tools such as Schell's Elemental Tetrad will be examined in
depth, to understand how mechanics translate into game dynamics and aesthetic
experiences for the player. Next, the scientific and practical principles for designing
core mechanics that are robust, clear, deep, elegant, and capable of generating
immediate feedback and emerging potential will be discussed. We will explore how
these core mechanics integrate into larger, interconnected systems, forming game
loops that structure the player experience. We will then address the complex
challenges of balancing game systems, illustrating quantitative (such as
mathematical modeling and simulation) and qualitative (such as expert playtesting)
methods to ensure fairness and an appropriate level of challenge. Finally, we will
explore the fascinating concept of emergent complexity, analyzing how relatively
simple sets of mechanics can generate unpredictable global behavior, player
creativity, and deep replayability, drawing inspiration from examples such as
Conway's "Game of Life". The overall goal of this chapter is to provide the reader with
a scientific, methodological and analytical approach to the design and innovation of
mechanics, recognizing them as the fundamental language through which game
design takes shape and expresses itself.


**3.1. Formalization of Game Mechanics: Operational Definitions,**
**Fundamental Properties (Interactivity, Ludic Verb, Impact on**
**State) and Advanced Taxonomies (Sicart, Cook, Björk &**
**Holopainen)**


Defining in a univocal and rigorous way what constitutes a "game mechanic" is a task
that has long engaged theorists and designers, given the diversity of interpretations
and the continuous evolution of language in the field of game design and game
studies. However, a formal and operational understanding of this concept is essential
for a scientific approach to their design, analysis and communication.


**Miguel Sicart (2008),** in his influential article "Defining Game Mechanics", proposes
a definition that has stimulated a wide debate and emphasizes action and interaction:
"Game mechanics are methods invoked by agents, designed for interaction with the
state of the game" (p. 1). This definition emphasizes several key aspects. First,
mechanics are **methods invoked by agents** : they are actions, procedures, or
algorithms that players (or, in some cases, AI-controlled agents) can actively perform.
They are not passive elements of the system, but dynamic tools of interaction.
Second, they are **designed for interaction** : the mechanics are intentionally created
by the designer to allow, structure and constrain the player's interaction with the game
system. Third, they operate by modifying the **game state**, which is the set of all the
variables, data and information that define the current situation of the game at a given
time (e.g. the position of the characters, their life points, the contents of the inventory,


48


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


the state of a quest). For example, the "shooting" mechanic in _DOOM Eternal_ (id
Software, 2020) is a method invoked by the player (pressing a button) that is
designed to interact with the state of the game (reducing a demon's life points,
consuming ammo, potentially altering the environment).


Other authors have offered complementary definitions. **Salen and Zimmerman**
**(2004),** in "Rules of Play", define mechanics as "the various processes, actions, and
methods of control that the game offers to the player" (p. 149), emphasizing their role
in shaping the gaming experience and allowing the player to act within the rules.
**Jesse Schell (2008),** in "The Art of Game Design", describes them more broadly as
"the actions that players can perform, the rules that govern those actions, and the
implications of those actions" (p. 129), thus also including some of the rules in the
concept of mechanics. Regardless of the specific wording, a consensus emerges that
mechanics are the **"verbs" of the game** : jump, run, shoot, collect, build, talk,
negotiate, move a piece, roll a dice, draw a card. They are the operating procedures
and rules that govern the player's input and the output of the game system, mediating
interaction.


It is important, although definitions may overlap, to maintain a conceptual distinction
between **rules** and **mechanics** . As discussed in Chapter 1.2, the rules define the
boundaries, constraints, and general conditions of the game (e.g., "you can't move a
pawn backwards in chess", "to win a game of _League of Legends_ you must destroy
the enemy Nexus"). Mechanics, on the other hand, are the specific processes and
concrete actions through which players operate within those rules to achieve
objectives (e.g. "the pawn movement mechanic allows you to advance a square, or
two from the starting position, and capture diagonally"; "a champion's 'ultimate' ability
mechanic in _League of Legends_ deals X damage in a Y area with a Z cooldown").
**Lundgren and Björk (2003),** and later **Björk and Holopainen (2005)** in their work
on game design patterns, distinguish between rules that define the object of the game
(objectives, victory conditions) and rules that define possible actions. The mechanics
are more closely linked to the latter, representing the operational implementation of
the possibilities of action.


To analyze and design mechanics in a systematic way, it is useful to consider some
of their **fundamental properties** :


1. **Interactivity:** Mechanics are inherently interactive; they require input from
the player (or another agent) and produce an output or change in the state
of the game.
2. **Ludic Verb:** Each mechanic can be expressed as a verb that describes the
action that the player can perform (jump, speak, build). **Daniel Cook (2006),**
in "The Chemistry of Game Design", emphasizes the importance of
identifying these fundamental verbs.
3. **Impact on the State of the Game:** Each activation of a mechanic should,
in principle, have an observable or inferable effect on the state of the game,
providing feedback to the player.
4. **Clarity/Understandability:** How easily the player can understand how the
mechanic works, what its inputs are, and what its likely consequences are.
Unclear mechanics, such as a crafting system with obscure recipes in an
RPG, can generate frustration and hinder engagement (see Nielsen's
principles of usability **, 1993** ).


49


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


5. **Depth:** The amount of meaningful, interesting, and non-trivial strategies that
a mechanic (or system of interacting mechanics) can support over time.
Deep mechanics offer possibilities for long-term learning and mastery, often
emerging from simple ground rules but with complex interactions (cf.
**Costikyan, 2013**, on the difference between surface complexity and
strategic depth). Street _Fighter 6's combat system_, with its basic mechanics
(punches, kicks, parries) and more complex systems (special, super, Drive
System), offers considerable depth.
6. **Elegance:** The ability of a mechanic or a small set of mechanics to produce
a wide variety of complex dynamics or interesting experiences with a
relatively simple and concise set of rules and components. The design of
_Go_ is often cited as an example of extreme elegance.
7. **Immediate and Readable Feedback:** How the mechanic communicates
the outcome of their actions to the player. Clear, timely and multimodal
feedback is essential for learning and satisfaction (see Chapter 4.4). The
"game feel" is closely linked to the quality of the feedback of the movement
and action mechanics.
8. **Agency:** The extent to which a mechanic allows the player to perform
meaningful actions that have a perceptible and intentional impact on the
game world and its state (cf. **Murray, 1997** ). Mechanics that offer choices
with real consequences increase the sense of agency.
9. **Balance:** The relationship between different mechanics or between the
options offered by a single mechanic, in terms of relative power, cost, utility,
and risk/reward. Balancing is crucial for perceived fairness, strategic variety,
and the prevention of unintentional dominant strategies (see Section 3.5).


Given the sheer and diverse mechanics possible, several attempts at **classification**
**or taxonomy** have been proposed, although none are universally exhaustive. These
taxonomies help designers understand the space of possibilities, communicate more
precisely, and identify recurring patterns. **Staffan Björk and Jussi Holopainen**
**(2005),** in "Patterns in Game Design", proposed an approach based on **game design**
**patterns** . A pattern is a recurrent and well-defined solution to a common design
problem within a specific context. They identified hundreds of patterns at different
levels of abstraction, often describing combinations of mechanics and rules.
Examples of patterns include "Area Control" (as in _Risk_ or many strategic game
modes), "Resource Management" (found in countless games, from _Settlers of Catan_
to _StarCraft_ ), "Set Collection" (common in card and board games), "Player
Elimination", "Press Your Luck" (as in the _dice game Can't Stop_ or in the risk
mechanics in some RPGs), or "Role-Playing". These patterns provide a higher-level
language for discussing and analyzing game design, and can be seen as aggregates
of more atomic mechanics.


**Daniel Cook (2006),** in his essay "The Chemistry of Game Design", proposes to think
of mechanics as "atoms" that can be combined to form more complex gameplay
"molecules". He distinguishes between **primary mechanics** (the fundamental and
repeated actions, such as moving and shooting in a shooter) and **secondary**
**mechanics** (less frequent or support actions, such as reloading or using a health kit).
The interaction between these mechanics creates the **game loops** (see Section 3.4).


Other taxonomies may be based on **player action** (movement, combat, object
interaction, social interaction, creation/construction, puzzle solving), impact **on game**
**state** (spatial modification, resource modification, information modification,
relationship modification mechanics), or application **domain** (physical, economic,


50


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


social, narrative mechanics). For example, in _The Sims 4_ (Maxis, 2014), we find
social interaction mechanics (talking, flirting, arguing), needs management
mechanics (hunger, hygiene, energy), construction and furniture mechanics, and
career progression mechanics.


The formalization and taxonomic understanding of game mechanics are fundamental
steps for their systematic and innovative design. By using precise language, drawing
from existing taxonomies, and considering the fundamental properties of each
mechanic, designers can analyze, communicate, prototype, and iterate on their game
systems more rigorously, scientifically, and effectively. Future research in this field
continues to explore new ways to formalize and classify mechanics, for example
through the use of formal ontologies (such as the "Game Ontology Project" discussed
by **Zagal et al., 2005** ), computational analysis of game rule corpora, or the study of
players' perceptions of the mechanics themselves.


**3.2. The In-depth MDA (Mechanics, Dynamics, Aesthetics)**
**Framework and Other Analytical Models for Systemic Design**
**(e.g. Schell's Elemental Tetrad, Flanagan's Lens of Critical Play)**


To analyze and design games systemically, designers need conceptual models that
help them break down the complexity of the gaming experience and understand the
relationships between its different components. Among these, the **Mechanics**
**Dynamics Aesthetics (MDA) framework**, proposed by **Robin Hunicke, Marc**
**LeBlanc and Robert Zubek (2004)** in an influential paper of the Game Developers
Conference, has established itself as a widely adopted and discussed analytical and
design tool. It offers a lens to understand how design choices at the level of
fundamental components of the game translate into the experience lived by the
player.


The MDA framework breaks down games into three distinct but intrinsically
interconnected layers: **Mechanics**, **Dynamics,** and **Aesthetics** . **Mechanics**
describe the particular components of the game at the level of data, algorithms and
formal rules. These are the elements that the designer creates and implements
directly: the basic actions that the player can perform (jumping, shooting, talking), the
systems that govern the game world (physics, enemy AI, scoring rules), objects and
their properties. For example, in the _Monopoly_ board game, mechanics include
rolling dice, moving on the board, buying property, collecting rent, paying taxes. In a
video game like _Super Mario Bros._, mechanics include Mario's jump, his ability to hit
blocks, the rule that enemies are defeated by jumping on them, and the power-up
system. The mechanics are what the designer _explicitly builds and defines._


Dynamics, on the other hand, describe the behavior of the game system in action
when mechanics are activated and interact with player inputs and each other over
time. The dynamics are not directly designed by the designer in the same way as the
mechanics, but rather _emerge_ from the actual gameplay. They include the strategies
that players develop, the behavior patterns of the system, the pace of the game, the
unexpected social interactions in a multiplayer game, or the feeling of urgency
created by a timer. Taking the example of _Monopoly_, dynamics could include the
formation of monopolies on certain colors, aggressive negotiations between players
to exchange properties, or the spiraling of debt that leads a player to bankruptcy. In
_Super Mario Bros._, dynamics include the precise timing required to overcome certain


51


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


obstacles, strategies to maximize points, or to find secret passages. Dynamics are
what the player _experiences_ at the level of systemic and behavioral interaction.


Finally, the Aesthetics describe the desirable emotional, cognitive, and sensory
responses that the game evokes in the player when interacting with the system.
Aesthetics represent the experiential objectives of design; are the different "forms of
entertainment" or affective qualities that one aims to arouse. For example, a game
might aim to evoke a sense of challenge, discovery, self-expression, fantasy,
storytelling, companionship (social interaction), sensation (motor sense pleasure), or
submission/pastime (relaxation). These eight categories were proposed by Hunicke,
LeBlanc and Zubek (2004) as a vocabulary to describe aesthetic qualities. Aesthetics
are what the player _feels_ and remembers about the gaming experience. In _Monopoly_,
aesthetics might be related to competition, strategy, and, for some, the frustration or
euphoria of luck. In _Super Mario Bros._, the dominant aesthetics could be challenge,
the feeling of mastery and the pleasure of movement (game feel). In a game like
_Journey_, the aesthetic sought is that of wonder, emotional connection and a kind of
spiritual transcendence.


The main value of the MDA framework lies in highlighting that designers and players
perceive and interact with the game from opposite but complementary perspectives.
The **designer** typically works from Mechanics to **Aesthetics** : he designs the
mechanics (e.g. a combat system with light attacks, heavy attacks and parries),
hoping that these generate certain **Dynamics** (e.g. players develop "hit and run"
strategies, learn to read enemy attack patterns, experiment with different combos),
which, in turn, they should produce the desired **Aesthetics** (e.g., a sense of
rewarding challenge, mastery, and adrenaline). The **player**, on the contrary,
experiences the game from Aesthetics to **Mechanics** : he experiences certain
sensations and emotions (Aesthetics), which derive from the game dynamics he
observes and engages in (Dynamics), which are in turn the result of the underlying
mechanics implemented by the designer. This model encourages designers to think
more explicitly and systematically about experiential goals (the Aesthetics they want
to evoke) and how choices at the level of Mechanics can influence those experiences
through emergent Dynamics. It becomes a tool for formulating design hypotheses ("If
I implement this stealth mechanic, I expect players to adopt avoidance and
observation strategies, generating an aesthetic of tension and cunning") that can then
be tested and validated through playtesting.


While the MDA framework has been extremely influential and is widely used as an
analytical and communication tool, it is not without its criticisms and limitations. Some
scholars, such as **Sicart (2011),** have pointed out that the distinction between
mechanics and dynamics can sometimes be blurred and that the model may not fully
capture the complexity of the player experience, especially in relation to ethical,
political values or more reflective and less traditional "fun" oriented forms of play.
Others have noted that the taxonomy of the eight aesthetics may be incomplete or
culturally specific.


To overcome some of these limitations or to offer complementary perspectives, other
analytical models have been proposed. Jesse Schell **'s** Elemental Tetrad **(2008),** for
example, suggests analyzing a game through four fundamental elements that must
be in harmony with each other: **Mechanics**, **Story**, **Aesthetics** (understood here
more as sensory presentation) and **Technology** . Schell emphasizes how each
choice in one of these elements affects the others and how the player experience


52


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


emerges from their synergistic interaction. For example, the technology available (a
powerful graphics engine or the constraints of a mobile platform) will influence the
aesthetic possibilities and mechanics that can be implemented, which in turn will have
to support or integrate with the story you want to tell. A game like _Cyberpunk 2077_
relies on advanced graphics technology to support a detailed and immersive visual
aesthetic, which in turn serves to convey a complex story and gameplay mechanics
that reflect the transhumanist theme.


Another significant approach is that of the **"Lens of Critical Play"** proposed by **Mary**
**Flanagan (2009)** in "Critical Play: Radical Game Design". This model encourages
designers and gamers to look at games not just as forms of entertainment, but as
systems that can convey critical messages, challenge social conventions, and
promote reflection. The "critical play lens" invites us to consider how mechanics,
rules, and narratives can be used to comment on political, social, or cultural issues.
Games such as _Papers, Please_ (Lucas Pope, 2013), which puts the player in the
shoes of a border inspector in a totalitarian state, or _This War of Mine_ (11 bit studios,
2014), which simulates the experience of civilians during a siege, are examples of
"critical play" that use mechanics to generate empathy and reflection on difficult
issues.


The use of these (and other) analytical models is not an end in itself, but serves to
provide designers with a richer and more structured language to think about their
games, to communicate their intentions, to identify potential problems and to
formulate testable hypotheses. A scientific approach to game design involves the
ability to critically select and apply these frameworks, adapting them to the specific
context of your project and integrating them with data from user research and
playtesting. They are tools for systems thinking, which help navigate the inherent
complexity of creating meaningful interactive experiences.


**3.3. Design of Robust and Engaging Core Mechanics: Principles**
**of Clarity, Depth, Elegance, Immediate Feedback and Emerging**
**Potential**


At the heart of every meaningful gaming experience are one or more **core**
**mechanics** (or "core gameplay loops"), i.e. the fundamental, repeated and central
activities that the player performs during most of the game time and that define the
very essence of the game interaction. These mechanics are not simply isolated
actions, but the foundation on which all other game systems, challenges, and
opportunities for player expression are built. Designing and refining them are among
the most critical and challenging tasks for a game designer. **Daniel Cook (2006),** in
his influential essay "The Chemistry of Game Design", compares core mechanics to
the "atoms" of gameplay, emphasizing how their intrinsic quality and the way they
combine determine the nature of the entire game "molecule". A scientific approach to
core mechanic design involves applying principles derived from HCI, cognitive
psychology, and established practical experience, focusing on aspects such as
clarity, depth, elegance, feedback quality, and potential for emergence.


53


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


The first fundamental principle in the design of a core transport is **clarity** and
**comprehensibility** . The player must be able to quickly and intuitively understand
what they can do (the action or "verb" of the mechanic), how they can do it (the
required input), and what the immediate and predictable consequences of that action
are (the output of the system). This clarity is closely related to the concepts of
**affordances** and **signifiers** discussed in Chapter 7.2. A jumping mechanic in a 2D
platformer like _Super Mario World_ (Nintendo, 1990) is immediately clear: pressing a
button makes the character jump, and the height and length of the jump are
modulated by the duration of the press and the horizontal speed. Visual feedback
(the jump animation) and sound feedback (the sound effect) reinforce this
understanding. On the contrary, an obscure core mechanic or with counterintuitive
controls, as sometimes happens in very complex simulation games without adequate
onboarding (e.g. the first hours of _Dwarf Fortress_, Bay 12 Games, 2006, for a
newbie), can generate a significant barrier to entry and frustration. Clarity does not
necessarily imply simplicity; Complex mechanics can be clear if introduced and
explained gradually.


A second crucial principle is the **depth** of the mechanics. A deep core mechanic, as
defined by **Greg Costikyan (2013),** is one that, while potentially simple to learn in its
basics, offers a rich decision-making space and supports a wide range of meaningful
and interesting strategies that the player can discover and master over time. Depth
is not synonymous with superficial complexity (a large number of rules or options for
their own sake), but rather with strategic potential and possibilities for the expression
of skill. Street _Fighter 6_ 's combat system is an example of depth: the basic mechanics
(light, medium, heavy attacks, parries, grabs) are relatively easy to understand, but
their interaction with the more advanced systems (special moves, Super Arts, the
Drive System with its multiple mechanics of Drive Impact, Drive Parry, Drive Rush,
etc.) It creates an immense strategic space that takes months or years to master at
high levels. Even a seemingly simple mechanic like the placement of tetrominos in
_Tetris_ reveals a remarkable strategic depth when considering techniques such as the
T-Spin or the management of the "preview" of future pieces. Depth is what ensures
long-term replayability and engagement, allowing players to feel a continuous sense
of learning and improvement.


Elegance is a more elusive but highly desirable quality in a core mechanic. An elegant
mechanic is one that manages to produce a wide variety of complex dynamics or
interesting experiences with a relatively simple, concise, and internally coherent set
of rules and components. Elegance is often associated with a design that feels "right"
and inevitable once understood. The portal system in _Portal_ (Valve, 2007) is a
masterful example of elegance: the single mechanic of creating two interconnected
portals, combined with coherent physics, generates an extraordinary variety of
puzzles and creative solutions with a minimalist ruleset. The "social links" system in
_Persona 5_ (Atlus, 2016), where time spent cultivating relationships with NPCs
improves combat skills in the Metaverse, is also an example of elegant integration
between narrative and gameplay mechanics.


Immediate **and meaningful feedback** is intrinsically linked to the quality of a core
transport. As discussed in Chapter 4.4, each player action that triggers a core
mechanic should produce clear, timely, multimodal feedback (visual, auditory, tactile)
that informs the player of the outcome of the action and its consequences. This
feedback is not only informative, but also contributes to the "game feel" or "juice" of
the mechanics, i.e. its sensory pleasantness and feeling responsive. The feedback
of a successful hit in _DOOM Eternal_, with its visceral impact, powerful sound effects,


54


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


and enemy reaction animation, makes for extremely satisfying combat mechanics.
Conversely, mechanics with vague, delayed, or absent feedback can be confusing,
frustrating, and unrewarding. Research in HCI and cognitive psychology (cf. **Shute,**
**2008** ) emphasizes the importance of formative feedback for learning and motivation.


Finally, a well-designed core mechanic should possess some **emerging potential** .
Emergence (see Section 3.6) refers to the ability of a system to generate complex
and often unpredictable behaviors and patterns from the interaction of simpler
components. Core mechanics that have a high degree of interaction with other
mechanics, the game environment, or the actions of other players (in multiplayer
games) can lead to a rich range of emergent dynamics, increasing replayability,
player creativity, and a sense of discovery. The "building" mechanics in _Minecraft_,
combined with the game's physics and variety of materials, allows for the creation of
structures and machinery that go far beyond what the original designers may have
anticipated. Elemental interactions in _Genshin Impact_, where different elemental
abilities can combine to create unique effects (e.g. Electro + Hydro = Electro Charge),
are also an example of a designed emergent potential.


Designing robust and engaging core mechanics is a highly iterative process that
relies on rapid prototyping (see Chapter 10.2) and continuous playtesting cycles (see
Chapter 8). Designers must constantly ask themselves, "Is this mechanic clear? Is it
deep enough to sustain interest? Is it elegant in its implementation? Do you provide
satisfactory feedback? Does it allow interesting dynamics to emerge?". The goal is
to create an intrinsically fun and manipulable "toy", as suggested by **Raph Koster**
**(2005),** even before building a larger play structure around it. The solidity of the core
mechanics is the foundation on which the entire gaming experience rests. A scientific
approach to this crucial phase of design involves not only creativity and intuition, but
also rigorous analysis of the mechanic's properties, the formulation of hypotheses
about its experiential impact, and empirical validation through the observation and
measurement of player reactions.


**3.4. Systems of Interacting Mechanics and Game Loops:**
**Complex Game Architectures, Synergies, Counterpoints and the**
**Creation of "Fun Machines" (Cook)**


Rarely does a video game, especially those of a certain complexity, rely on a single
isolated core mechanic. More frequently, the gaming experience emerges from the
**dynamic interaction of multiple mechanics**, which combine to form larger, more
interconnected gaming systems. These systems, in turn, structure the player's
activity through **game loops** (or core loops) at different time scales, creating a flow
of actions, feedback and objectives that maintain engagement. Understanding how
to design and orchestrate these complex game architectures, fostering positive
synergies and avoiding negative counterpoints, is essential to create what **Daniel**
**Cook (2006)** defines as efficient and rewarding "fun machines".


A **game system** can be defined as a set of mechanics, rules, and content that interact
to produce a particular set of dynamics and outcomes related to a specific aspect of
gameplay. Common examples of game systems include the combat system in an
RPG, the economic system in a strategy game, the character progression system,
the crafting system, the artificial intelligence system that governs enemies, or the
narrative system that manages quests and story events. Each of these systems is


55


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


made up of smaller mechanics (Cook's "atoms") that interact. For example, the
combat system of _God of War_ (Santa Monica Studio, 2018) integrates mechanics of
light and heavy attack, parry, dodge, runic abilities, the use of the Leviathan axe (with
its throwing and recall properties) and the abilities of the companion Atreus. The
effectiveness and depth of the combat system derive not only from the quality of each
individual mechanic, but above all from the **synergies** and **interactions** possible
between them, which allow the player to develop a personal fighting style and face a
variety of enemies with different tactics.


Designing these interconnected systems requires **holistic systems thinking** . As
**Adams and Dormans (2012)** pointed out in "Game Mechanics: Advanced Game
Design," designers must carefully consider how changes to a mechanic or parameter
within a system can have cascading, often unexpected, effects on other parts of the
game. For example, increasing the damage of a specific weapon could unbalance
the entire combat system, render other weapons useless, or trivialize encounters with
previously challenging enemies. To manage this complexity, designers use tools
such as flowcharts, concept maps, spreadsheets for parameter balancing, and, most
importantly, iterative prototyping of specific subsystems to test their interactions.


The interaction between mechanics and systems structures the player experience
through **game loops** . A game loop describes a cyclical, repeating sequence of
activities or actions that the player performs, typically consisting of an
**action/challenge** phase, a **result/feedback** phase, and a **reaction/redecision**
**phase** that leads back to the beginning of the cycle or a new cycle. Loops at different
time scales can be identified, often nested inside each other, as described by authors
such as **Keith Burgun (2012)** in "Clockwork Game Design". The **core action loop**
**(or micro-loop)** represents the shortest, most fundamental and frequently repeated
sequence of actions linked to the core mechanics of the game. For example, in a
first-person shooter like _Call of Duty: Modern Warfare III_ (Sledgehammer Games,
2023), the micro-loop could be: spotting an enemy (perception/challenge) -> aiming
(action) -> shooting (action) -> observing the enemy's impact and reaction
(result/feedback) -> deciding whether to shoot again, change targets, or seek cover
(new decision) -> repeat. These micro-loops must be inherently satisfying and
responsive (the "game feel") because they form the basis of moment-to-moment
interaction. Short **- and medium-term goal loops (or meso-loops)** involve longer
sequences of actions, aimed at completing an immediate goal or a small unit of
gameplay. Examples include: defeating an entire group of enemies to clear an area;
solve an environmental puzzle to open a passage in _Tomb Raider_ (Crystal Dynamics,
2013); complete a single quest or "contract" in _The Witcher 3_ ; or play a single hand
