in a card game like _Slay the Spire_ (MegaCrit, 2019), which in turn contains microloops of card play and resource management. These meso-loops provide a sense of
progress and accomplishment at regular intervals. **Long-term goal loops (or**
**macro-loops)** structure the game experience on larger time scales, driving the
player's overall progression through the game. Examples include: completing an
entire level or a "dungeon"; defeat a main boss that concludes a story arc; level up
your character and unlock significant new skills; or achieve one of the victory
conditions in a strategy game like _Civilization VI._ These macro-loops are often tied to
the game's progression and reward systems (see Chapter 4).


Effective design often nests these loops into each other harmoniously, creating a
continuous flow of activities, goals, and feedback that keeps the player engaged and
motivated at different time scales. Completing satisfying micro-loops contributes to
progress in meso-loops, and success in meso-loops moves the player forward in


56


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


macro-loops. **Daniel Cook** talks about creating "machines" (systems of mechanics)
that produce "resources" (rewards, progress, satisfaction) for the player, and how
these machines can be interconnected to create a larger game economy. For
example, in many RPGs, the combat loop (micro/meso) produces experience and
loot (resources), which feed into the character progression loop (macro), which in
turn unlocks new abilities or access to new areas, which offer new opportunities for
the combat loop.


Scientific design of game loops involves clearly defining the goals of each loop, the
mechanics involved, the type and timing of the feedback provided, and the rewards
(intrinsic or extrinsic) that motivate the player to continue the cycle. It is crucial to
analyze how the different loops interact: do they support each other ( **positive**
**synergies** )? Or do they create conflicts or redundancies ( **negative counterpoints** )?
For example, if a game requires excessive grinding (repeating an uninteresting loop)
to progress into the main macro-loop, this can lead to frustration. Playtesting is crucial
to identify issues in game loops, to ensure that they are balanced, rewarding, and
don't become overly repetitive or boring over time. Analyzing telemetry data can
reveal where players get stuck or leave certain loops, indicating potential design
issues.


The understanding of complex game architectures, based on the interaction of
systems of mechanics and the structuring of game loops at different scales, is a
fundamental skill for the game designer. It allows you to move from designing
individual actions to creating holistic, long-lasting experiences, where each part
contributes significantly to the whole.


**3.5. Balancing of Game Systems: Quantitative (Mathematical**
**Modeling, Simulation, Simplified Game Theory) and Qualitative**
**(Expert Playtesting, Heuristic Analysis) Methods for Fairness**
**and Challenge**


Game **balance** is a critical and iterative process in game design that consists of finetuning and harmonizing the mechanics, rules, numerical parameters and content of
a game. The primary goal of balancing is to ensure that the gaming experience is
**fair**, offers an **appropriate** and engaging level of challenge for the target audience,
maintains **the player's interest** over time, and is free of **unintentional dominant**
**strategies** (which render other options useless), **clearly useless or underpowered**
**elements**, or **insurmountable points of frustration** (unless these are specific
design goals, as in some "masocore games"). An unbalanced game can quickly lead
to boredom (if too easy or with few meaningful choices), frustration (if unfairly difficult
or with clearly inferior options), or a quick loss of interest from players once a
dominant strategy is discovered. Balancing is a complex task that is rarely solved
with a single solution, but requires a combination of design intuition, rigorous
analysis, extensive testing, and, increasingly, the use of analytical and quantitative
tools. Authors such as **Ian Schreiber**, in his lectures on game balance, and **Adams**
**and Dormans (2012),** in "Game Mechanics: Advanced Game Design", have
provided in-depth discussions of this topic.


Balance can affect multiple aspects of a game. A key distinction is between
**symmetrical balancing** and **asymmetric balancing** . In symmetrical balancing
games, all players or factions start with the same resources, skills and options


57


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


(classic examples are chess, Go, or many sports games such as _FIFA 23_, EA Sports,
where, net of the statistical differences between real teams, the basic rules are
identical for both players). In these cases, balancing focuses on ensuring that no
opening position or opening move is inherently and unfairly superior, and that play is
primarily determined by skill. In asymmetrical balancing games, however, players,
character classes, factions, or units have inherently different skills, resources, or
starting rules. Examples include strategy games like _StarCraft II,_ where the three
races (Terran, Zerg, Protoss) have unique units and mechanics; RPGs with distinct
character classes (warrior, mage, thief) like in _Baldur's Gate 3_ ; or fighting games with
a roster of fighters each with a specific moveset like _Street Fighter 6_ . Asymmetrical
balancing is considerably more complex and aims to ensure that, despite inherent
differences, each option is competitively viable and fun to play, often through a
system of interdependencies, mutual strengths and weaknesses (sometimes
described as a complex "rock-paper-scissors" system). For example, one faction
might be strong in the early stages but weak in the late game, while another has the
opposite pattern.


Other crucial aspects of balancing include **difficulty balancing**, i.e. ensuring that the
challenge presented to the player is appropriate to their perceived skill level and
progression in the game (see Chapter 5.3 on difficulty curve design and Chapter 9.4
on Dynamic Difficulty Adjustment); economic **balancing** in games with virtual
economies (see Chapter 4.2), which concerns the control of inflation, the scarcity of
resources and the usefulness of objects; balancing **the player's options**, ensuring
that the different strategic choices, skills, weapons or units available to the player are
all valid and interesting in certain contexts, preventing some options from becoming
"overpowered" (OP) or "underpowered" (UP, too weak) in such a way as to reduce
strategic variety; and risk **/reward** balancing, where riskier or more skillful actions
should generally lead to more significant rewards, such as facing a particularly
difficult optional boss in _Elden Ring_ to obtain unique equipment.


To address these challenges, designers use a mix of **qualitative and quantitative**
**methods** . **Iterative playtesting** (see Chapter 8) remains the most fundamental and
irreplaceable quality method. Observing real players (both newbies and experts,
depending on the objectives of the test) interact with the game, collect their verbal
feedback (through think-aloud protocols or post-session interviews) and analyze their
expressions and behaviors is essential to identify perceived balance problems,
moments of frustration, unexpected strategies or unclear elements. **Heuristic**
**analysis** by experienced designers, who use their knowledge of design principles
and similar games to assess balance, can also provide valuable insights, especially
in the early stages.


On the **quantitative** front, the use **of spreadsheets is** standard practice for game
designers, especially for games with many numerical parameters (e.g. unit stats in
an RTS, weapon damage and costs in an RPG, item drop probability). Spreadsheets
allow you to easily plot and modify values, calculate averages, standard deviations,
cost/effectiveness ratios, and simulate simple combat or economic scenarios. For
example, you can create formulas to calculate the "damage per second" (DPS) of
different weapons or the "time to kill" (TTK) against a standard enemy, to compare
their effectiveness. The **statistical analysis of game data (game analytics and**
**telemetry),** collected from large-scale playtest sessions or, for live games, directly
from players, has become a very powerful tool. This data can reveal which elements
of the game are over- or underutilized, which skills or characters have significantly
higher or lower win rates, where players tend to freeze or quit, or how the virtual


58


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


economy is evolving. For example, when analyzing MOBA data, you might find that
a certain hero has a win rate that is too high at all skill levels, indicating the need for
a "nerf."


For game systems that are particularly complex or have a high degree of
interdependence between variables, designers can resort to **mathematical**
**modeling** and **simulation** . This may include the use of:


  - **Agent-Based Models (ABM):** Create software agents that simulate the
behavior of players or game entities (NPCs, economic units) according to
certain rules or heuristics, and observe how the system evolves over time.
This can help predict emerging dynamics, bottlenecks, or points of instability
in a virtual economy or large-scale combat system.

  - **Game theory (simplified):** Although the formal application of game theory
(cf. **Osborne & Rubinstein, 1994** ) to complex games is difficult, its basic
concepts (such as dominant strategies, Nash equilibria, prisoner's dilemma)
can provide a useful framework for thinking about the balance of strategic
choices, especially in competitive games or with meaningful social
interactions. For example, you can analyze whether a certain choice always
offers a better payoff than others, regardless of what your opponents do.

  - **Monte Carlo Simulations:** Use repeated random sampling to estimate the
outcome of systems with probability elements (e.g., the damage distribution
of a weapon with a certain critical hit probability, or the result of thousands
of simulated fights across different unit compositions).

  - **Petri nets or State Flow Charts:** To model and analyze systems with
discrete states and transitions, such as crafting systems or quest
progressions.


**Artificial Intelligence (AI),** as discussed in Chapter 9.4, is also playing an increasing
role in balancing. AI agents based on reinforcement learning can be trained to play
the game and discover optimal or unexpected strategies, highlighting potential
balance issues before they are discovered by human players. AI can also be used to
support **Dynamic Difficulty Adjustment (DDA),** which are systems that
automatically change the difficulty of the game in real time based on the player's
performance, with the aim of maintaining an optimal level of challenge and favoring
the flow state (cf. **Hunicke, 2005** ). However, designing effective DDAs is complex
and requires careful calibration to prevent the player from perceiving the system as
manipulative or unfair.


It is crucial to recognize that balancing is not a static goal to be achieved once and
for all, but a **dynamic and continuous process**, especially for "live" games or
"games as a service" (GaaS) that receive regular updates of content and mechanics.
The introduction of new characters, items, or abilities in a game like _League of_
_Legends_ or _Destiny 2_ constantly requires a re-balancing of the entire game
ecosystem to maintain competitive integrity and strategic variety. Community
feedback, monitored through forums, social media and direct channels, becomes a
valuable source of information for this ongoing balancing process, although it should
be interpreted critically, taking into account possible biases and different player
perspectives.


In conclusion, balancing game systems is a subtle art that requires a deep


59


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


understanding of mechanics, player psychology, and emerging dynamics, supported
by a clever use of qualitative and quantitative methods. A scientific approach to
balancing does not seek absolute "perfection" (which may be unattainable or even
undesirable, as some "interesting asymmetry" can be a source of depth), but rather
a functional balance that supports the experiential goals of the game and ensures a
fair, engaging, and rewarding experience for the target audience. It is a process of
constant fine-tuning, listening and adaptation, where rigorous data analysis and
expert judgment from the designer must work in synergy.


**3.6. Emergence and Complexity in Games: Designing for**
**Controlled** **Unpredictability,** **Player** **Creativity** **and** **Deep**
**Replayability (Holland, Conway's Game of Life as Metapora)**


The concept of **emergence** refers to the phenomenon whereby systems composed
of a set of individually simple rules and components can generate, through their local
interactions, complex, unpredictable and often surprising global behaviors, which
were not explicitly programmed or which are not easily deduced from the analysis of
the individual parts alone. **John H. Holland (1998),** one of the pioneers in the study
of Complex Adaptive Systems (CAS) at the Santa Fe Institute, has explored how the
interaction between autonomous agents following local rules can lead to selforganized macroscopic patterns and novel systemic properties. The famous " _Game_
_of Life_ " by **John Horton Conway (1970),** a two-dimensional cellular automaton with
a few very simple rules of cell birth, survival and death, is a paradigmatic example of
how an extraordinary variety of complex and dynamic patterns (gliders, oscillators,
still lifes) can arise from basic simplicity, vividly illustrating the power of emergence.


In game design, designing for emergencies means creating a set of basic mechanics,
interaction rules, and environmental systems that, combined with players' actions and
choices, produce a wide range of interesting game dynamics, strategies, and
situations that are not entirely predictable by the designer. This approach is often
opposed to "scripted" or strictly linear design, in which most events, problem solutions
and narrative trajectories are predetermined and rigidly controlled by the author. The
emergency, when well managed, can lead to greater **replayability** (as each game
can play out differently), a higher degree of **player creativity** (discovering new ways
to use systems), a strong **sense of discovery**, and moments of **surprise** and
wonder.


The **sources of emergency** in video games are multiple and often interconnected.
A primary source is the **interaction between simple but versatile mechanics** . A
limited number of well-defined core mechanics, if they can be combined by the player
in many creative ways, can lead to strategic depth and emergent solutions. For
example, in _Into the Breach_ (Subset Games, 2018), each mech's unique abilities,
different Vek enemy capabilities, and environmental effects interact in ways that
generate ever-new tactical puzzles and often require creative and non-obvious
solutions to minimize collateral damage. Another classic example is the board game
_Go_, whose very simple rules of placing and capturing stones generate a strategic
complexity that has challenged human and artificial intelligence for centuries.


Player **freedom (agency)** and the presence of open systems ("sandbox") are another
powerful emergency engine. When players have a lot of freedom to choose how to
use the tools and mechanics at their disposal and how to interact with a responsive


60


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


game world, their actions can lead to completely unexpected outcomes and stories.
_Minecraft_ (Mojang Studios, 2011) is the epitome of this approach: the simple
mechanics of gathering resources and placing blocks, combined with systems such
as redstone (which allows you to create logic circuits), have allowed players to build
complex machines, entire cities and even computers that work within the game, far
beyond the initial intentions of the designers. More recently, _The Legend of Zelda:_
_Tears of the Kingdom_ (Nintendo, 2023), with its "Ultrahand" (to attach objects to each
other) and "Fuse" (to combine weapons with objects) mechanics, has unleashed an
extraordinary wave of creativity from players, who have built ingenious and often
hilarious vehicles, weapons and contraptions to solve puzzles and overcome
obstacles, demonstrating a very high emerging potential.


Simulated **physical systems** (gravity, collisions, fluid dynamics, chemical reactions)
and **reactive environments** can create unpredictable and often fun emergent
situations. The "immersive sim" genre, inaugurated by games such as _Ultima_
_Underworld_ (Blue Sky Productions, 1992) and perfected by titles such as _Deus Ex_
(Ion Storm, 2000) and the _Dishonored_ series (Arkane Studios), is based precisely on
the creation of systems (physics, AI, player skills) that interact in complex ways,
allowing multiple and often unforeseen solutions to problems. In _Noita_ (Nolla Games,
2020), a roguelike where every pixel is physically and chemically simulated,
interactions between different elements (water, fire, oil, acid, gunpowder, electricity)
can lead to spectacular and often lethal chain reactions emerging.


The **Artificial Intelligence (AI) of Non-Player Characters (NPCs),** especially when
they possess autonomous behaviors, their own goals, and the ability to react
dynamically to the environment and actions of the player (or other NPCs), can
contribute significantly to emerging dynamics. The famous **Nemesis system** in
_Middle-earth: Shadow of Mordor_ (Monolith Productions, 2014) and its sequel _Shadow_
_of War_ (2017) is an example of how AI can generate custom emergent stories: Orc
captains who survive encounters with the player remember past clashes, acquire new
skills, develop rivalries and can even set up ambushes, creating unique and recurring
antagonists with whom the player develops a dynamic "relationship". Even complex
factional AIs in strategic sandbox games like _Mount & Blade II: Bannerlord_
(TaleWorlds Entertainment, 2022) or _Stellaris_ (Paradox Development Studio, 2016)
can lead to large-scale wars, alliances, and political changes that are not pre-scripted
but emerge from systemic interactions.


In **multiplayer games**, social, economic, and political interactions between large
numbers of players are an inexhaustible source of large-scale emerging phenomena.
The entirely player-driven economies, massive space battles coordinated by
alliances of thousands of users, political intrigue and espionage wars that
characterize the persistent universe of _EVE Online_ (CCP Games, 2003) are classic
examples of how social complexity can generate narratives and dynamics that no
designer could predict or script individually. Social norms, specific languages, and
cultures that develop within online gaming communities are also forms of emergency.


Finally, **Procedural Content Generation (PCG),** although a tool in the hands of the
designer (see Chapter 5.4), can also contribute to the emergence when generated
content (levels, items, quests, enemies) interact in unexpected ways with the player's
actions or with other game systems. In roguelike games such as _Spelunky 2_
(Mossmouth, 2020) or _The Binding of Isaac_ (Edmund McMillen & Florian Himsl,
2011), the combination of procedurally generated levels, a wide range of objects with


61


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


synergistic or contrasting effects, and a rich physics system can lead to unique runs,
full of moments of surprise, danger and unexpected opportunities.


Designing for the emergency is highly desirable because it can lead to greater
replayability (every playthrough is different), a deeper sense of agency and creativity
for the player (who feels like a co-author of their own experience), and moments of
genuine discovery and surprise. However, it also presents **significant challenges**
for the designer. The main one is the **loss of a certain degree of authorial control** :
it is more difficult to predict and control all the possible dynamics that may emerge,
which can lead to exploits, unbalanced strategies, negative feedback loops or
frustrating situations for the player. An excess of unguided emergency can result in
**chaos** and make the game incomprehensible or directionless. In addition, **testing** a
system with high emerging potential is extremely complex, as it is impossible to
predict or replicate all possible combinations and outcomes. Finally, balancing the
emergency with a structured progression or coherent narrative can be challenging;
Too much freedom can cause you to lose sight of the main objectives or dilute the
impact of the story.


Despite these challenges, designers can adopt a few **principles for designing for**
**a "controlled" or "desirable" emergency** :


1. **Robust and Versatile Core Mechanics:** The foundation of the system
must be solid. Clear mechanics, with well-defined rules of interaction but
with multiple possible uses, are more likely to generate interesting and
meaningful interactions.
2. **Clear Feedback on Underlying Systems:** While global dynamics are
emerging, players should be able to understand (at least intuitively) the local
rules and mechanics that generate them, in order to be able to formulate,
experiment, and learn.
3. **Significant Constraints and Goals:** The emergency thrives within a
system of constraints and goals that give meaning and direction to the
player's actions. The complete absence of structure rarely leads to an
interesting game; rather, it is the tension between the player's freedom and
the constraints of the system that often generates the richest dynamics.
4. **Provide Player Tools to Manage or Exploit Complexity:** Provide players
with ways to influence, understand, or react to emerging phenomena, such
as through crafting systems, the ability to build defenses, or analysis and
information tools.
5. **Continuous Iteration and Careful Observation (the metaphor of**
**"Gardening"):** Playtesting becomes even more crucial. The designer takes
on a role similar to that of a "gardener" (as often described by **Will Wright** ):
he cultivates a system of rules and mechanics, carefully observes what
behaviors and dynamics emerge from player interactions, "pruning"
unwanted outcomes (e.g., exploits, boring dominant strategies), and
"nurturing" or reinforcing interesting and positive ones through iterative
design adjustments.
6. **System Modularity and Squareness:** Designing systems whose
components (mechanics, objects, abilities) can be combined in many
different ways without necessarily causing catastrophic failures or
completely unbalanced interactions. Squareness refers to mechanics that
affect different aspects of the game, and can therefore be combined in more
predictable but still interesting ways.


62


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


Games like the aforementioned _Dwarf Fortress_, with its incredibly detailed simulation
and unique stories that emerge from each fortress, _The Sims_ (Maxis), with its
emerging narratives based on Sims' simple behavioral rules and player choices,
immersive sims like _Deus Ex_ or _Prey_ (Arkane Studios, 2017), which offer multiple
solutions to problems arising from the interaction between player skills, AI and
environmental systems, and colony management games such as _RimWorld_ (Ludeon
Studios, 2018), where "AI storytellers" orchestrate emerging events and challenges,
are all excellent examples of designs that embrace and harness the power of
emerging complexity.


A scientific approach to designing for emergence involves the deliberate creation of
systems with specific properties (such as local interactions, feedback loops,
nonlinearities), the use of simulations and models (such as agent-based models) to
explore the "space of possibilities" generated by such systems, and a constant
analytical focus to understand and guide emergence towards outcomes that enrich
the player experience rather than leaving it entirely to chance or chaos. The theory
of complex systems, cybernetics and artificial life (Artificial Life, cfr. **Langton, 1989** )
offer conceptual frameworks and valuable tools for thinking about these fascinating
design problems.


**3.7. Conclusions: Mechanics as a Fundamental Language of**
**Game Design**


The game mechanics, as explored in this chapter, truly constitute the fundamental
language through which the game designer communicates with the player and
through which the gaming experience takes shape. From their formal definition and
understanding of their intrinsic properties, to the use of analytical frameworks such
as MDA to connect them to desired dynamics and aesthetics, mechanic design is a
central activity that requires both analytical rigor and creative sensitivity.


We've seen how designing strong, clear, deep, and elegant core mechanics with
immediate feedback and emerging potential is crucial to creating an inherently
immersive "toy." These core mechanics rarely exist in isolation, but are woven into
complex systems and game loops at different time scales, structuring the flow of the
player experience and creating rich, interconnected game architectures. The ability
to orchestrate these interactions, fostering synergies and managing counterpoints, is
a hallmark of mature design.


Balancing these complex systems emerges as an ongoing and iterative challenge,
requiring a scientific approach that integrates qualitative methods, such as expert
playtesting and heuristic analysis, with quantitative methods, such as mathematical
modeling, simulation, game data analysis, and, increasingly, the assistance of
artificial intelligence. The goal is not static perfection, but a dynamic balance that
ensures fairness, appropriate challenge, and strategic variety.


Finally, designing for emerging complexity represents one of the most fascinating
frontiers of game design, where the creation of simple rules can lead to an explosion
of controlled unpredictability, stimulating the player's creativity and ensuring deep
replayability. The designer, in this context, becomes less an omnipotent narrator and
more an "architect of possibilities" or a "gardener of systems".


63


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


Ultimately, a scientific approach to game mechanic design involves a deep
understanding of their components, their interactions, and their impact on the player
experience. It requires an iterative process of ideation, prototyping, rigorous testing,
and critical analysis, informed by psychological theories, HCI principles, and systems
models. Mechanics aren't just rules or actions; They are the vehicle through which
the challenge, learning, expression and, ultimately, fun and meaning that players
derive from gaming experiences are manifested. Mastering the language of
mechanics is therefore essential for any game designer who aspires to create games
that are not only functional, but truly memorable and impactful.


64


GAME DESIGN MANUAL

# **Chapter 4**

### **Playful Economies,** **Progression and Effective** **Feedback Systems**


_A game is a problem that you create for yourself._


_-_ **Jane McGonigal-**


65


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


After exploring the fundamentals of game design, player psychology, and mechanic
design, this chapter focuses on three interconnected pillars that are crucial for
shaping the player's experience over time, guiding their behavior, and sustaining their
long-term engagement: **reward systems**, **progression structures,** and **feedback**
systems. A scientific understanding of how these elements work and interact, drawing
from theories of behavioral and cognitive psychology, principles of economics, and
information design methodologies, is critical to creating games that are not only
immediately rewarding, but also offer a sense of development, mastery, and clear
communication. We will examine how reinforcement psychology can inform the
design of effective reward schemes, while going beyond a purely Skinnerian
approach to consider the impact on intrinsic motivation. We will then delve into the
complexity of designing balanced and functional virtual economies, analyzing the
management of resources, currencies, sources and wells. Next, we'll explore
different player progression structures, from leveling systems to skill trees, to
designing end-game content that keeps you interested. We will then analyze
feedback as a scientific communication tool, examining its types, channels and
principles to ensure clarity and effectiveness in learning and motivation. Finally, the
chapter will offer a critical analysis of gamification and persuasive design, discussing
their effectiveness, ethical risks, and strategies for designing gamified interventions
that are meaningful and responsible. The overall goal is to provide designers with the
knowledge to expertly orchestrate incentives, player development, and systemic
communication, in order to cultivate lasting engagement and a deeply satisfying
gaming experience.


**4.1. Psychology of Reinforcement and Design of Reward Schemes: Beyond**
**Skinner – Intrinsic Motivation vs. Intrinsic Motivation Extrinsic and Impact of**
**Reward Systems**


Reward systems are an almost ubiquitous component in video games, used to
incentivize certain behaviors, signal success, drive progression, and increase player
engagement. Their design often draws, implicitly or explicitly, from the principles of
**reinforcement psychology**, in particular from the work on **operant conditioning** by
**B.F. Skinner (1938, 1953).** However, a mature scientific approach to reward system
design must go beyond a purely behaviorist view, carefully considering the interaction
between extrinsic rewards and intrinsic motivation, and the ethical implications of
particularly powerful or potentially manipulative reward schemes.


According to operant conditioning, a behavior followed by a desirable consequence
(a **positive reinforcement** ) is more likely to be repeated, while a behavior followed
by an aversive consequence (a **punishment** ) or the removal of a desirable stimulus
(a **negative punishment** or cost of response) is less likely to be repeated. Negative
**reinforcement**, on the other hand, increases the likelihood of behavior by removing
a pre-existing aversive stimulus. In video games, positive reinforcement is the most
common form: gaining experience points (XP) after defeating an enemy in _Diablo IV_
(Blizzard Entertainment, 2023), unlocking new gear after completing a difficult quest
in _Monster Hunter Rise_ (Capcom, 2021), receiving virtual currency for selling items,
or even a simple rewarding sound effect ("jingle") and a celebratory animation after
solving a puzzle in _Professor Layton_ (Level-5). Examples of negative punishment
include the loss of souls (currency and experience) upon death in _Dark Souls_, or the
degradation of equipment with use in many RPGs. Negative reinforcement could be
exemplified by the disappearance of a negative status (e.g., poison) after using an
antidote.


66


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


The **timing and frequency** with which reinforcements are delivered, known as
**schedules of reinforcement**, are crucial to their effectiveness in shaping and
maintaining behavior. As discussed by **Ferster and Skinner (1957),** **continuous**
**reinforcement (CRF),** where each instance of the desired behavior is rewarded, is
very effective for the rapid learning of new behaviors (e.g., the first enemies in a game
always dropping a small reward), but it also leads to rapid extinction if the
reinforcement ceases. **Intermittent (or partial) reinforcement schemes**, where
reinforcement is given only a few times, are generally more resistant to extinction and
may maintain behavior for longer periods. These include **Fixed Ratio (FR) schemes**
(reward after a fixed number of responses, e.g. one bonus for every 10 enemies
defeated) and **Fixed Interval (FI) schemes** (reward for the first response after a fixed
time interval, e.g. a daily login bonus in many mobile games such as _Clash of Clans_,
Supercell, 2012) produce predictable response patterns. The most powerful patterns
in generating high response rates and strong resistance to extinction are the **variable**
ones. A **Variable Ratio (VR) pattern** provides a reward after an average number of
responses, but the exact number varies unpredictably (e.g., slot machines, the drop
of legendary items with low odds in games like _Borderlands 3_, or how loot boxes
work). A **Variable Interval (VI) pattern** provides a reward for the first response after
an average time interval, but the exact interval varies (e.g. the appearance of rare
resources that respawn in a game world at unpredictable intervals). VR schemes, in
particular, are known for their ability to induce persistent and, sometimes, compulsive
behavior, due to unpredictability and the hope of the next "big win." **Charles Duhigg**
**(2012),** in "The Power of Habit", discusses the power of variable habit-reward loops.


Although the principles of operant conditioning offer powerful tools for designers (cf.
**Hopson, 2001**, on the application of these principles in _EverQuest_ ), an over-reliance
on extrinsic rewards and powerful reinforcement schemes can have deleterious
effects on **intrinsic motivation** . The phenomenon of the **"overjustification effect",**
demonstrated by **Lepper, Greene and Nisbett (1973),** suggests that providing
extrinsic rewards for an activity that is already inherently interesting can reduce the
intrinsic interest in the activity itself. If a player plays an exploration game primarily
for the sake of discovery (intrinsic motivation), and the game begins to reward each
small discovery with trivial points or achievements, the player's focus may shift from
intrinsic discovery to the accumulation of extrinsic rewards; If these rewards are then
removed or become less significant, the overall interest in exploration may diminish.


The **Theory of Self-Determination (SDT)** by **Deci and Ryan (2000)** (see Chapter
2.1) offers a more nuanced picture for understanding the interaction between rewards
and motivation. According to SDT, the impact of an extrinsic reward on intrinsic
motivation depends on how it is perceived by the player in relation to his needs for
competence, autonomy and relationality.


  - Rewards perceived as **informative** (e.g., feedback that recognizes skill or
progress, such as a trophy for overcoming a difficult challenge creatively)
can increase the sense of competence and thus support or even increase
intrinsic motivation.

  - Rewards perceived as **controlling** (e.g., "do X to get Y", where the reward
becomes the sole purpose of the action, or rewards that seem to manipulate
the player into performing actions they would not freely choose) can
undermine the sense of autonomy and thus reduce intrinsic motivation.


67


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


  - Unexpected or non-performance-contingent rewards generally have less
(or no impact) on intrinsic motivation than expected and directly task-related
rewards.


Therefore, a scientifically informed and ethically conscious reward system design
should aim to:


1. **Prioritizing Intrinsic Motivation:** Designing mechanics and activities that
are inherently interesting, challenging, and that support the needs for
competence, autonomy, and relationality. Extrinsic rewards should be
secondary or supportive of these foundational experiences. Games like
_Outer Wilds_, which relies almost entirely on curiosity and intrinsic discovery,
with very few traditional extrinsic rewards, are an example of this.
2. **Use Extrinsic Rewards in a Strategic and Informative Manner:** Rewards
can be useful for guiding new players, signaling significant progress,
recognizing mastery, or adding an extra layer of satisfaction. However, they
should be perceived as a recognition of competence or as an expansion of
the possibilities of action (autonomy), rather than as the ultimate goal of the
game. For example, unlocking a new skill in _Hades_ after a successful run is
not just a reward, but a tool that increases the player's proficiency and
autonomy in subsequent runs.
3. **Offer Variety in Rewards:** Not all rewards need to be material or numerical.
Narrative rewards (new story fragments), aesthetic (new costumes,
customizations), social rewards (recognition by other players) or access to
new areas or challenges can be highly motivating.
4. **Consider the long-term impact:** Reward schemes that are initially very
effective (e.g., frequent and abundant rewards at first) may need to evolve
to maintain interest in the long run, avoiding "reward saturation" or the
feeling that rewards become trivial.
5. **Transparency and Perception of Fairness:** Especially for loot-based
rewards, transparency on drop rates and the perception that the system is
fair are key to avoiding frustration and mistrust. The controversy over loot
boxes has highlighted the importance of this aspect.
6. **Avoiding Exploitation of Cognitive Biases:** As discussed in Chapter 2.5,
designers need to be aware of how schemes like VR can interact with biases
like Gambler's Fallacy, and design responsibly so as not to encourage
excessive gaming behavior or problematic spending.


Research on gamification (see Section 4.5) has further explored the application of
ludic reward elements in non-playful contexts, often with conflicting results that further
underline the complexity of the interaction between extrinsic rewards and intrinsic
motivation (cf. **Deci, Koestner, & Ryan, 1999**, for a classic meta-analysis of the
effects of rewards on intrinsic motivation). Ultimately, reward systems are a powerful
tool, but like any tool, their effectiveness and ethical impact depend on how they are
designed and integrated into the overall gaming experience, with a deep
understanding of the player's psychology.


**4.2. Complex Virtual Economies: Scientific Design of Resources,**
**Currencies, Faucets, Sinks, Player Trading, and Prevention of**
**Economic Exploits (Castronova, Lehdonvirta)**


68


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


Many video games, especially those featuring persistent worlds, massively
multiplayer elements (MMORPGs), long-term strategy games, or titles with complex
crafting and trading systems, incorporate **virtual economies** within them. These
economies, which govern the creation (generation), distribution, exchange,
consumption, and destruction of virtual assets – which can include game-specific
currencies (gold, credits, gems), items of equipment, raw materials, virtual real
estate, and even services offered by other players – are complex systems that require
careful and scientifically informed design. The economist **Edward Castronova**
**(2005),** in his seminal book "Synthetic Worlds: The Business and Culture of Online
Games", was one of the first to systematically study these digital economies,
highlighting how they can develop characteristics strikingly similar to those of realworld economies, including phenomena such as inflation, deflation, scarcity, labor
specialization and even black markets for the exchange of virtual goods with Real
Money Trading (RMT). A flawed cheap design can lead to serious balance issues,
player frustration, and ultimately the failure of the game itself.


To design a functional and sustainable virtual economy, it is essential to carefully
understand and manage its core components. The first component is represented by
**the sources (faucets or sources),** i.e. all the mechanisms through which new
resources or currency enter into circulation in the game's economy. Common
examples of sources include rewards earned from completing quests or activities
(such as "gils" earned by completing quests in _Final Fantasy VII Rebirth_, Square Enix,
2024), "dropping" items or currency from defeated enemies (loots from monsters in
_Diablo IV_ ), crafting mechanics (such as mining minerals in _EVE Online_ or gathering
herbs in _World of Warcraft_ ), the creation of currency "out of thin air" by the system
(e.g., periodic salaries for certain activities, interest on virtual bank deposits, or the
sale of basic items by game-controlled merchants, who effectively "print" money).
Source design must carefully consider the rate of introduction of new resources into
the economy to avoid excessive inflation, which would devalue the currency and
players' efforts.


The second crucial component is **sinks**, which are all the mechanisms by which
resources or currency are permanently removed from the game's economy. Wells
are essential for counteracting inflation, maintaining the value of assets and currency
over time, and giving a sense of utility to spending. Examples of wells include repair
costs for damaged equipment (a common mechanic in many RPGs and MMORPGs),
trade transaction taxes (e.g., a percentage retained by the auction house in _World of_
_Warcraft_ ), irreversible consumption of items (healing potions, food that provides
temporary buffs, ammo), costs for learning new skills or crafting powerful items, the
purchase of items or services from Non-Player Characters (NPCs) that effectively
remove the currency from the game (e.g., fast travel, purchase of houses, or
untradeable cosmetic customizations), or the loss of items upon character death in
games with "hardcore" mechanics (such as in some modes of _Path of Exile_ ). The lack
of effective and meaningful wells is one of the most common causes of hyperinflation
and instability in virtual economies, making basic items extremely cheap and highlevel items inaccessible to new players.


Finally, there are flows **or transfers**, which are the mechanisms that allow the
exchange of resources and currency between players, or between players and the
system, without necessarily adding or removing net resources from the overall
economy (although often these exchanges can involve taxes that act as sinks).
Examples include player-to-player trading, the use of auction houses or playeroperated markets (such as the Grand Exchange in _Old School RuneScape_, Jagex,


69


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


2013), donations or gifts between guild members, or even stealing from other players
in games that allow it (such as in some _Ultima Online_ servers). These flows are vital
for the dynamism of the economy, allowing the specialization of players (some could
focus on gathering, others on crafting, others on pure trade) and the circulation of
goods.


Designing a virtual economy that is stable, engaging, and supports the game's
broader design goals requires scientific attention to several balancing principles. The
**control of inflation and deflation** is primary. Inflation, a general increase in prices
and a decrease in the currency's purchasing power, is often caused by an excess of
sources over wells and can devalue players' past efforts and make costs prohibitive
for newcomers. Deflation, a general decrease in prices, can be caused by an excess
of wells or a shortage of currency, and can disincentivize spending and economic
activity, leading to stagnation. Designers must constantly monitor and, if necessary,
adjust resource generation rates (sources) and removal mechanisms (wells) to
maintain a dynamic balance. **Vili Lehdonvirta and Edward Castronova (2014),** in
"Virtual Economies: Design and Analysis", provide an in-depth discussion of these
dynamics and the tools for managing them.


The **relative scarcity and abundance** of different resources directly affect their
perceived value and player strategies. A certain scarcity for the most powerful or
desirable items can make acquiring them more rewarding and meaningful, but too
scarcity can generate frustration or incentivize unwanted behaviors such as "gold
farming" or the use of bots. The **usefulness of resources** is another key factor:
resources must have a clear and perceived purpose within the game, whether it is to
improve the character's abilities, unlock new gameplay possibilities, achieve
aesthetic customization goals, or facilitate other playful activities. Resources that
have no clear utility or quickly become obsolete do not stimulate economic activity
and can weigh down the player's inventory.


The **prevention of economic exploitation is** a constant challenge. Designers must
try to anticipate and mitigate possible "exploits" that could destabilize the economy,
such as fraudulent duplication of objects ("duping"), inflationary generation of
currency through bots or flaws in the system, or market manipulation. This requires
robust system design and active monitoring of the post-launch economy.


The **impact on player motivation** is a central consideration. A well-designed
economy can significantly increase engagement by providing long-term goals (e.g.,
saving for an expensive and powerful item), incentivizing cooperation (e.g.,
exchanging complementary resources with other players) or competition (e.g.,
dominating a specific market), and providing opportunities for specialization and selfexpression (e.g., becoming a skilled craftsman, a cunning merchant, or a collector
of rare objects). Conversely, an economy that is perceived as unbalanced, unfair,
overly "grindy," or irrelevant to the core gameplay can be a significant source of
frustration and demotivation.


Finally, designers should consider the phenomenon of **Real Money Trading (RMT),**
which is the exchange of virtual goods, currencies, or services for real money, which
often takes place through unauthorized third-party marketplaces. RMT can have
complex and often negative impacts on the game's internal economy (e.g. by
distorting prices, incentivizing gold farming and the use of bots, creating inequalities
between players) and on the game experience (e.g. by devaluing achievements


70


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


obtained in-game). Designers and developers must decide what policy to adopt
towards RMT: prohibit and actively fight it (the most common position), tolerate it
within certain limits, or, in rare cases, attempt to integrate it in a controlled manner
into the game's business model (as was the case with _Diablo III_ 's failed Real Money
Auction House initially).


To address these complex design challenges, professionals make use of different
tools and methodologies. **Mathematical modeling and computer simulation** of the
economy can help test the impact of different source configurations, wells, spawn
rates, and player behaviors before the game launches or before implementing
significant changes. After launch, **game analytics** (see Chapter 8.3) are critical to
constantly monitor key economic metrics (such as the total amount of currency in
circulation, average prices of the most traded items, transaction rates, wealth
distribution among players) in order to identify emerging issues early and inform
necessary adjustments. Playtesting **focused specifically on the** game's economy,
involving players with different styles and goals, is crucial to gather qualitative
feedback on the perception of the economy, the ease of obtaining the necessary
resources, the usefulness and desirability of items, and the overall fairness of the
system.


Virtual economy design is, therefore, a highly specialized and inherently
interdisciplinary subfield of game design, requiring a solid understanding of economic
principles (micro and macro), behavioral psychology (how players make economic
decisions, often influenced by bias), systems theory, and data analysis. A wellmanaged and balanced virtual economy can massively enrich the gaming
experience, providing depth, longevity, and a strong sense of progression and
investment for players. Conversely, a poorly conceived or neglected economy can
quickly undermine the enjoyment and sustainability of an entire game world.


**4.3. Advanced and Engaging Progression Structures: Leveling**
**Systems, Skill Trees, Reputation Systems, Metagames, and**
**Sustainable End-Game Content Design**


**Player progression** is a central concept in game design, referring to how players
progress through a game's content, acquire new skills or upgrades, overcome
challenges of increasing complexity, and generally experience a sense of
development, growth, and fulfillment over time. A well-designed progression structure
is critical to maintaining long-term engagement, providing clear and intermediate
goals, meeting the player's need for expertise (as outlined by Self-Determination
Theory, see Chapter 2.1), and giving a sense of meaning to the investment of time
and effort. **Jesper Juul (2005),** in "Half-Real", discusses how the rules and structure
of games inherently create a path of learning and improvement for the player.
Progression structures can take very different forms, from linear and rigidly defined
to open, non-linear, or even adaptive.


The **most common progression models**, already mentioned in Chapter 4 of the
previous draft, can be further detailed. Linear **progression** guides the player along a
largely predetermined path, with a defined sequence of levels, missions, or narrative
events. This approach gives the designer strong control over the pace of the
experience and the difficulty curve, and is often used in heavily narrative games such
as _Uncharted 4: A Thief's End_ (Naughty Dog, 2016) or in classic platform games


71


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


where the level sequence is fixed, such as _Super Mario Wonder_ (Nintendo, 2023).
The main benefit is the ability to accurately orchestrate the emotional experience and
story presentation, but it can limit the player's replayability and agency on the overall
path. **Non-linear (or branching/open) progression**, on the other hand, gives the
player more freedom to choose the order in which to face challenges, explore the
game world, or develop their character. There can be multiple paths, alternate
endings, or a plethora of optional objectives that the player can pursue according to
their interests. Open-world games such as _The Elder Scrolls V: Skyrim_ (Bethesda
Game Studios, 2011) or _Grand Theft Auto V_ (Rockstar North, 2013) are paradigmatic
examples, where the main plot is just one of the many threads that the player can
follow. Even RPGs with significant narrative choices that branch out the story, such
as the _Mass Effect_ series (BioWare) or more recently _Baldur's Gate 3_ (Larian
Studios, 2023), implement complex forms of non-linear progression. This approach
increases the player's sense of autonomy and agency, greatly promotes replayability
and allows for more personalized experiences, but poses significant challenges in
terms of balance, narrative consistency and the risk of the player feeling lost or
overwhelmed by the amount of options. Finally, **adaptive (or dynamic) progression**
refers to game systems that actively modify the progression, difficulty, or even
content presented based on the player's performance, choices, or playstyle (tying
into the concept of Dynamic Difficulty Adjustment, DDA, discussed in Chapter 3.5
and Chapter 9.4). The goal is to keep the player in an optimal flow state, customizing
the experience in real time. Examples include the AI Director in _Left 4 Dead_ (Valve,
2008), which modulates the appearance of zombies and objects based on the team's
performance, or more subtle systems that could adjust enemy aggression or resource
availability. While promising, adaptive progression is difficult to implement and
balance properly, and if perceived by the player too obviously, it can undermine a
sense of accomplishment or fairness.


Regardless of the overall pattern, player progression manifests itself through a variety
of specific, often interconnected **mechanisms and systems** . **Experience point**
**(XP) and leveling systems** are among the most popular, especially in role-playing
games (RPGs) and many action games. Players earn XP by performing specific
actions (defeating enemies, completing quests, discovering new areas) and, upon
reaching certain XP thresholds, they "level up", typically gaining improvements to the
character's base stats (health, strength, intelligence), new abilities or access to higher
level equipment or content. This creates a very powerful positive feedback loop,
where effort is rewarded with a tangible increase in power and abilities. Games such
as the _Diablo_ series (Blizzard Entertainment) or _Borderlands_ (Gearbox Software) are
heavily centered on this type of progression.


Skill **trees or** skill unlock systems are often associated with leveling systems, but
they offer an extra layer of customization and strategic choice. Players can spend
skill points (earned through level-ups or other means) to unlock or improve specific
skills within a branching structure, allowing for the creation of different and
specialized character "builds". The skill tree of _Path of Exile_ (Grinding Gear Games,
2013) is an extreme example of complexity and flexibility, offering thousands of
interconnected nodes. Effective skill tree design presents significant choices, where
each skill unlocked offers a real change in gameplay or opens up new tactical
possibilities, avoiding "filler" skills or clearly suboptimal paths.


**Equipment-based progression (or "loot-driven progression")** is another
fundamental driver in many genres, particularly in action RPGs ("looter shooters"
such as _Destiny 2_ or _Warframe_, Digital Extremes, 2013; ARPG like the _Diablo_ series).


72


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


Here, acquiring increasingly powerful and higher-stat weapons, armor, attachments,
and other items is the primary means through which the player character becomes
more effective. This often combines with item rarity systems (common, uncommon,
rare, epic, legendary) and random "drop" mechanics (RNG), creating a strong
incentive to re-engage (farming) in the hope of obtaining the desired item. Balancing
these systems is crucial to prevent them from becoming overly "grindy" or based on
pure luck.


Narrative **progression** occurs when completing story arcs, discovering crucial story
information, developing relationships with characters, or reaching turning points in
the plot mark the player's progress. In heavily story-driven games like _God of War_
_Ragnarök_ or _Final Fantasy VII Rebirth_, narrative progression is often the main driver
that drives the player forward, even if supported by character and equipment
progression systems.


Reputation **or alignment systems** allow the player to influence their position towards
different factions, characters, or moral principles within the game world. The player's
actions (completing quests for a faction, making specific ethical choices, attacking
members of a group) can increase or decrease reputation or shift alignment,
unlocking different opportunities (new quests, access to special merchants, allies) or
consequences (hostility from certain groups, changes in the narrative). Games like
_Fallout: New Vegas_ (Obsidian Entertainment, 2010) or the _Fable_ series (Lionhead
Studios) are known for their complex reputation and morality systems.


Unlocking **areas or game content (gating)** is a common mechanism for structuring
progression and guiding the player. Access to new areas of the game world, new
modes, new challenges or new mechanics is often linked to the achievement of
certain progression milestones (a certain level, the possession of a key item, the
completion of a specific quest). This allows designers to gradually introduce
complexity and maintain a sense of discovery and novelty. For example, in
"Metroidvania" games such as _Hollow Knight_ or _Metroid Dread_ (MercurySteam &
Nintendo EPD, 2021), acquiring new movement skills (double jumping, sprinting, wall
climbing) unlocks access to new areas of the map that were previously unreachable,
creating a strong loop of exploration, skill acquisition, and reexploration.


An important concept related to long-term progression, especially in persistent online
games or "games as a service", is the **metagame** . The metagame refers to all
aspects of the game that exist "above" or "around" the main moment-to-moment
gameplay. It includes high-level strategies that players develop based on a collective
understanding of the most effective mechanics (often discussed in forums, online
guides, or streams), the "tier list" of the best-performing characters or weapons,
player-driven economic and political dynamics, and even community activities
outside of the game itself. A healthy and dynamic metagame can significantly
contribute to a game's longevity, but it requires constant monitoring and balancing by
designers to prevent it from becoming stagnant or dominated by a few optimal
strategies. For example, developers of competitive games like _League of Legends_ or
_Valorant_ regularly release balance patches to "shake up" the metagame and keep
the game fresh.


Finally, designing **sustainable end-game content** is a crucial challenge for games
that aim to keep players engaged for hundreds or thousands of hours after completing
the main progression (e.g., the narrative campaign or reaching the maximum level).


73


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


End-game content can include extremely difficult raids or dungeons that require high
team coordination (as in _World of Warcraft_ or _Destiny 2_ ), competitive PvP modes with
leaderboards and seasons, "infinite" or very long progression systems (e.g. Paragon
levels in _Diablo III_ ), seasonal events, or tools that allow players to create and share
their own content. The lack of meaningful end-game content is a common cause of
churn by more dedicated players.


Psychologically, a well-designed progression has a profound and positive impact. It
provides a **sense of competence and increasing mastery**, fulfilling one of the basic
needs of SDT. It offers **clear and achievable objectives** at different time scales,
which guide the player's behavior and structure their experience. It generates
**anticipation and anticipation** for future rewards and new possibilities, fueling
motivation. It provides **tangible feedback on the progress** made, through visual,
numerical or narrative indicators. And, finally, it promotes a **sense of investment** in
the game and in one's character, which can increase fidelity and persistence,
although designers should be aware of the risk of over-leveraging the sunk cost
fallacy (see Chapter 2.5).


The scientific design of progression structures requires careful consideration of the
player's learning curve, the desired pace for the game (pacing), the experiential goals
and the target audience. It is crucial to balance the feeling of constant progress with
appropriate challenges, avoiding periods of stagnation ("plateaus" that are too long)
or "walls" of difficulty perceived as insurmountable or unfair. Iterative playtesting,
supported by the analysis of telemetry data (e.g. quest completion rates, average
times to reach certain levels, dropout points), is essential to calibrate the speed,
clarity and gratification of progression systems, ensuring that they contribute to an
immersive and lasting gaming experience.


**4.4. Feedback as a Scientific Tool for Communication and**
**Learning: Types (Intrinsic/Extrinsic, Formative / Summative),**
**Channels (Visual, Auditory, Tactile), Timeliness, Specificity and**
**Impact on Motivation and Mastery (Shute, Hattie & Timperley)**


Feedback, in the context of game design, refers to any information, signal, or
consequence that the game system provides to the player in direct or indirect
response to their actions, performance, or changes in the state of the game. It is a
fundamental and ubiquitous communicative element, which acts as a bridge between
the player and the system, allowing the former to understand the dynamics of the
latter, to learn the rules and mechanics, to correct mistakes, to evaluate their progress
and, ultimately, to feel connected, competent and in control of the gaming experience.
Effective and well-designed feedback is crucial not only for learning and mastering
gaming skills, but also for intrinsic motivation, reducing frustration, and maintaining
the flow state. Research in educational psychology, particularly on the effects of
formative feedback, and human-machine interaction (HCI) has identified numerous
scientific principles for designing feedback systems that are truly effective.


**Valerie Shute (2008),** in an influential and comprehensive review of formative
feedback, outlined several key features and evidence-based recommendations,
many of which are directly and profitably applicable to game design. Similarly, the
work of **John Hattie and Helen Timperley (2007),** "The Power of Feedback",
analyzed the impact of feedback on learning, identifying the levels at which feedback


74


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


can operate (on the task, on the process, on self-regulation, on the self) and the
conditions that maximize its effectiveness. Also the expectation value theory of
**Wigfield and Eccles (2000),** discussed in Chapter 2.1, emphasizes how feedback,
by influencing beliefs of self-efficacy and expectations of success, can have a
significant impact on player motivation and persistence.


Feedback in games can be categorized according to multiple dimensions, each with
specific design implications. A key distinction is between **intrinsic feedback** and
**extrinsic feedback** . Intrinsic feedback is a natural, direct, and often physical
consequence of the action within the simulated game world. For example, in a racing
game like _Forza Motorsport_ (Turn 10 Studios, 2023), the sound of the engine revving
up, the feeling of losing tire grip, or the visual impact of a collision with another car
are all forms of inherent feedback. This type of feedback is often perceived as more
immersive and "realistic". Extrinsic feedback, on the other hand, is provided by
elements outside the simulated game world, typically through the user interface (UI)
or explicit notification systems. Examples include a "Mission Complete!" message
that appears on the screen, a score that increases, a health bar that empties, or a
pop-up announcing the unlock of an achievement. Both types of feedback are
important and often coexist: intrinsic feedback provides immediate, contextual
information, while extrinsic feedback can summarize performance, confirm the
achievement of goals, or provide meta-playful information.


Another important classification concerns the **function** of feedback, distinguishing
between **formative feedback** and **summative feedback** . Formative feedback is
provided _during_ the process of learning or performing a task, with the goal of driving
improvement and helping the player understand how they are progressing and what
they could do differently. It is process-oriented and learning-oriented. Summative
feedback, on the other hand, is typically provided _at the end_ of a task, level, or game
session, and is used to evaluate overall performance or the final result (e.g., a final
score, rank, ranking). In games, formative feedback is crucial for learning mechanics
and progression, while summative feedback can contribute to a sense of
accomplishment or provide a yardstick. **Shute (2008)** emphasizes that formative
feedback, to be effective, should answer three key questions for the student/player:
"Where am I going?" (Feed Up – clarity of objectives), "How am I doing?" (Feed Back

- information on current performance) and "Where to go next?" (Feed Forward –
indications for improvement or next steps).


Feedback also manifests itself through different **sensory channels** . **Visual**
**feedback** is the most pervasive in video games and includes graphical changes (an
enemy flashing when hit in _Hades_ ), special effects (explosions, bullet trails),
character reaction animations (a character staggering after a powerful blow), text
(damage messages, notifications), icons, and the entire user interface (HUD).
**Auditory feedback** is equally crucial, using sound effects (the "clink" of a coin being
collected, the sound of a weapon jamming, an alarm signal), dynamic music (which
changes intensity or theme based on the game situation, such as combat music that
activates when enemies are close in _The Elder Scrolls V: Skyrim_ ), and dialogue or
voice commentary (an NPC companion warning of danger). **Haptic (or haptic)**
**feedback**, provided primarily through controller vibration (such as "rumble" when
taking damage or shooting) or, in more advanced systems, through force feedback
(in racing wheels) or sophisticated haptic actuators (as in the PlayStation 5's
DualSense controller, which can simulate the tension of an arc or the texture of a
surface), It can significantly increase immersion and the feeling of physical impact.
The combined and synergistic use of multimodal feedback can make communication


75


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


richer, redundant (in a positive sense, to ensure that information is perceived) and
impactful.


To be scientifically effective, feedback in games should ideally adhere to a set of
principles established by research:


1. **Timeliness (Timeliness):** Generally, feedback should be provided as close
in time as possible to the action of the player who generated it, especially
for core actions and for learning motor or procedural skills. This helps the
player establish a clear cause-and-effect connection. Too much delay can
make feedback unnecessary or even confusing.
2. **Specificity and Clarity:** Feedback should be specific about what action or
performance it relates to, and in the case of corrective feedback, what went
wrong and, ideally, why. A generic "You failed!" is much less useful than a
message like "Missed target: You aimed too high" or "Incorrect crafting
combination: These materials are not compatible." Clarity implies that
feedback is easy to perceive and interpret, unambiguously.
3. **Actionability and Task/Process Orientation:** Effective feedback should,
whenever possible, provide information that the player can use to improve
their future performance or to better understand the system. It does not
necessarily have to provide the direct solution, but guide the learning and
problem solving process. Focusing on the task or process ("did you try
parrying instead of dodging for that type of attack?") is generally more useful
than focusing on the player's self ("you're not good at this") (cf. **Hattie &**
**Timperley, 2007** ).
4. **Consistency:** The feedback system should be internally consistent. Similar
actions should produce similar types of feedback, and cues (visual, auditory)
should have constant meaning within the game to avoid confusion.
5. **Appropriate Salience and Non-Intrusiveness:** Feedback should be
noticeable enough to be noticed by the player in the context of the action,
but not so intrusive, noisy, or overly frequent that it becomes a distraction,
disrupts flow, or increases cognitive load. The salience of feedback can be
modulated according to the importance and urgency of the information.
Ideally, much of the feedback should be seamlessly integrated into the
gaming experience (intrinsic feedback) so as not to interrupt the immersion.
6. **Balanced Frequency and Adapted Level of Detail:** Too little feedback can
leave the player confused and uncertain; too much feedback, especially if it
is redundant, unnecessary, or overly detailed for experienced players, can
be annoying. Some systems may adjust the amount or detail of feedback
based on the player's skill level or preferences.
7. **Positive Motivational Impact:** Feedback should be formulated in a way
that supports the player's intrinsic motivation, sense of competence, and
self-efficacy. Negative or corrective feedback should also be presented
constructively, emphasizing learning from mistakes and possibilities for
improvement, rather than simply penalizing or criticizing (cf. Dweck's
"growth mindset" theory **, 2006** ). Avoid feedback that may be perceived as
overly critical, demeaning, or undermines the player's willingness to keep
trying.
8. **Source Credibility:** If feedback comes from a character within the game,
their credibility and how it is perceived can affect their impact.


In addition to its informative functionality, the **aesthetic presentation** of feedback,
often referred to in game design jargon as " **juice** " or " **polish**," plays a significant role


76


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


in the player experience. Exaggerated but satisfying visual effects (e.g., shimmering
particles, controlled screen shakes), smooth and impactful reaction animations,
punchy and rewarding sounds, and an overall sense of system responsiveness can
make even the simplest and most repetitive actions enjoyable and engaging (see the
principles discussed by **Martin Kvale & Jory Prum** in their GDC Talks on game
audio, or by **Jan Willem Nijman & Rami Ismail** in their GDC Talk "The Art of
Screenshake"). This "layer" of aesthetic feedback contributes powerfully to the
visceral level of emotional experience (as defined by **Norman, 2004**, see Chapter
2.4) and can significantly increase the perception of the game's quality and polish.
Games like _Celeste_ or _Hades_ are excellent examples of how "juicy" feedback can
make core mechanics extremely satisfying.


Scientific feedback design involves an iterative process of design, prototyping, and
testing with users, using both qualitative methods (to understand how players
interpret and react emotionally to feedback, and whether they find it useful) and
quantitative methods (to measure the impact of feedback on performance, learning
rates, and engagement). Understanding when, how and why to provide feedback is
one of the most important skills for a game designer who aims to create clear,
formative and deeply satisfying experiences.


**4.5. Critical Gamification and Persuasive Design: Efficacy**
**Analysis, Ethical Risks (Manipulation, Exploitation), and Design**
**of** **Meaningful** **and** **Responsible** **Gamified** **Interventions**
**(Deterding, Bogost, Nicholson)**


The term **gamification** has experienced considerable diffusion and popularity since
the 2010s, referring to the application of elements, mechanics and principles of game
design in traditionally non-playful contexts, with the aim of influencing behavior,
increasing engagement, motivation, participation, learning or productivity. **Sebastian**
**Deterding, Dan Dixon, Rilla Khaled, and Lennart Nacke (2011),** in a seminal article
that helped define the field, propose a concise and widely cited definition:
"Gamification is the use of game design elements in non-game contexts" (p. 10).
These "game design elements" often include, but are not limited to, the use of **points**
**to** quantify actions and progress, **badges** or achievements to recognize
achievements and achievements, and **leaderboards** to stimulate competition and
social comparison (often collectively referred to as "PBLs"). Other common elements
include progression levels, structured challenges or missions, virtual rewards, avatar
customization, and providing instant feedback. The applications of gamification are
extremely vast and transversal, ranging from **education** (e.g. _Khan Academy_ which
uses points and badges to encourage math learning; _Duolingo_ that transforms
language learning into a playful experience with "streaks" and competitive leagues)
to **corporate training**, from **health and wellness** (fitness es. app such as _Nike Run_
_Club_ or _Fitbit_ that use challenges, goals and virtual rewards to encourage physical
activity; apps for therapeutic adherence) to **marketing and customer loyalty** (e.g.
loyalty programs of airlines or coffee chains that unlock status and privileges), from
**productivity in the workplace** (systems to incentivize the performance of
salespeople or participation in company initiatives) to **civic participation and social**
**change** (e.g. initiatives to encourage recycling, energy savings or participation in
public consultations).


The potential scientific basis for the effectiveness of gamification can be traced back


77


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


to some of the psychological principles discussed in this handbook. The use of points
and rewards can be interpreted as an application of the principles of **operant**
**conditioning** and positive reinforcement (see Section 4.1). If well designed,
gamification could theoretically support the fundamental psychological needs
identified by **Self-Determination Theory (SDT):** the need for **competence** through
the perception of progress, overcoming challenges, and feedback on results; the
need for **autonomy** by offering choices, control over one's actions, and opportunities
for expression; and the need **for relationality** through social, competitive or
collaborative elements (cf. **Mekler et al., 2017**, who found that meeting SDT needs
can mediate the effectiveness of gamification). The **Goal Setting Theory** (Locke &
Latham, 1990) suggests that clear and specific challenges and missions can motivate
action. Finally, many gamified systems rely on providing **frequent feedback** on
progress and performance, which is crucial for learning and motivation (see Section
4.4).


Despite the initial enthusiasm and widespread diffusion, gamification has received
numerous and well-founded **criticisms**, both from the academic community of game
studies and HCI, and from some game designers themselves. One of the most
frequent criticisms concerns the **oversimplification and trivialization of game**
**design** . Many gamification implementations boil down to the superficial and
mechanistic application of extrinsic elements such as points, badges, and
leaderboards (PBLs), without a real understanding of the deeper principles of game
design that generate meaningful engagement and intrinsic motivation. **Ian Bogost**
**(2011, 2015),** in works such as "Persuasive Games" and in his essay "Gamification
is Bullshit" (later renamed "Exploitationware"), has harshly criticized this trend, calling
it "pointification" and seeing it more as a form of marketing and "exploitation" than as
an authentic application of playful thinking. He argues that this approach reduces the
complexity and richness of the game to mere behavioral incentive mechanisms.


Another central criticism concerns the **excessive focus on extrinsic motivation** . As
discussed in Section 4.1, an overemphasis on external rewards (points, badges,
discounts) can undermine the intrinsic motivation for the activity itself, especially if
the activity was already interesting or meaningful to the user in itself (the overjustification effect of **Lepper, Greene and Nisbett, 1973** ). When extrinsic rewards
are removed or lose their perceived value, interest in the activity can drop
dramatically, sometimes below the initial level. This raises questions about the longterm sustainability of the engagement generated by many gamified systems.


**Ethical considerations** are particularly relevant. Gamification, as a form of
**persuasive design** (cf. **Fogg, 2003**, "Persuasive Technology: Using Computers to
Change What We Think and Do"), can be used to **manipulate** user behavior in nontransparent ways or for purposes that are not in their primary interest, but rather in
the interest of the organization implementing the system. The use of "dark patterns"
in gamified design to induce purchases, the excessive collection of personal data for
behavior tracking, or the creation of competition systems that generate stress and
anxiety are examples of ethical risks. The **surveillance** implicit in many gamified
systems, which require detailed monitoring of user actions, raises concerns about
privacy and the potential for social control. Rankings, while they may motivate some,
can also be demotivating and generate negative feelings (envy, frustration) for those
who are constantly at the bottom, especially if the comparison is not perceived as fair
or if success metrics are limited.


78


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


In addition, the **long-term effectiveness** of many gamification initiatives is doubtful.
The novelty effect can quickly fade if the gamified system isn't supported by intrinsic
value in the underlying activity or a design that evolves, delivers depth, and retains
interest over time. Many gamification systems fail because they fail to create an
authentic and meaningful gaming experience, but simply add a surface layer of
gameplay elements. **Scott Nicholson (2015),** in his work on "meaningful
gamification", proposes a "recipe" that emphasizes the importance of elements such
as play, exposure, choice, information, connection and reflection, going beyond
simple PBLs to create richer and more transformative experiences.


It is also important to clearly distinguish **gamification** from **serious games** . As
pointed out by **Deterding et al. (2011),** serious games are complete games, with a
system of rules and a game world, designed for a primary purpose other than pure
entertainment (e.g. education, training, simulation, persuasion, health). _Re-Mission_
(HopeLab, 2006), a game designed to help young cancer patients adhere to their
therapies, is an example of a serious game. Gamification, on the other hand, as
defined, applies only game _elements_ or _mechanics_ to non-playful contexts or
systems, without necessarily creating a complete and autonomous gaming
experience.


A scientific and critical approach to gamification therefore requires going beyond
hype and superficial implementations. It involves thoughtful **, user-centered design**,
starting with a deep understanding of the application context, target users, and their
intrinsic needs and motivations. It requires the selection and integration of game
design elements that are truly meaningful and that support the user's goals, rather
than being limited to extrinsic incentive mechanisms. **Iterative testing** is essential to
assess the real impact of the gamified system on engagement, motivation and
behavior, using both quantitative metrics and qualitative feedback. Finally, constant
**ethical reflection** is essential to ensure that gamification is used responsibly,
transparently and to promote the well-being and autonomy of users, rather than to
manipulate or exploit them. Academic research continues to explore the conditions
under which gamification can be truly effective and meaningful (cf. **Hamari, Koivisto,**
**& Sarsa, 2014**, a meta-analysis that found positive but variable effects; **Seaborn &**
**Fels, 2015**, for a critical review). The goal should be to create "meaningful play
experiences" ( **Salen** & Zimmerman, 2004) even when playing principles are applied
to contexts outside the game itself.


**4.6. Conclusions: Incentive Orchestration, Development and**
**Communication for Lasting Engagement**


This chapter explored three interdependent and fundamental components of game
design that, when orchestrated with scientific wisdom and human sensitivity, can
transform a set of mechanics into a deeply immersive and lasting experience: reward
systems, progression structures, and feedback mechanisms. We have seen how
understanding reinforcement psychology, while being cautious about intrinsic
motivation, can inform the creation of reward patterns that incentivize behavior and
signal success. However, it became clear that the most effective and sustainable
rewards are those that support the basic psychological needs of competence,
autonomy and relationality, rather than those that rely solely on extrinsic incentives.
