Designing complex virtual economies requires an almost "central banker" approach,
carefully managing the sources and wells of resources and currencies to maintain a


79


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


dynamic balance, prevent inflation or deflation, and ensure that the game's economy
is functional, fair, and contributes positively to the overall experience, providing
players with meaningful goals and opportunities for interaction and specialization.


Progression structures, whether linear, non-linear, or adaptive, are the engine that
guides the player through the gameplay experience over time, offering a sense of
growth, mastery, and discovery. Well-calibrated leveling systems, skill trees that offer
meaningful choices, content unlocking mechanisms that keep curiosity alive, and the
design of a robust metagame and end-game content are all crucial elements for longterm player retention and satisfaction.


Feedback, in all its forms – intrinsic and extrinsic, formative and summative,
conveyed through visual, auditory and tactile channels – acts as the nervous system
of the game, providing the player with the essential information to learn, adapt and
understand the consequences of their actions. Scientifically designed feedback,
which is timely, specific, actionable and motivating, is essential to facilitate mastery
and to keep the player in a state of flow.


Finally, the critical analysis of gamification has highlighted how the application of
these same principles of reward, progression and feedback in non-playful contexts
can be a powerful tool, but one that requires a deep understanding of playful design,
a strong ethical consideration and a focus on creating intrinsic value for the user, to
avoid superficial or manipulative implementations.


In conclusion, the skillful orchestration of incentives (through reward systems), player
development (through progression structures), and systemic communication
(through feedback) is an art and science that requires the game designer to carefully
balance psychological knowledge, economic principles, usability considerations, and
an unwavering commitment to creating experiences that are not only engaging in the
short term, but that also offer a path of growth, discovery and lasting and meaningful
satisfaction for each individual player.


80


GAME DESIGN MANUAL

# **Chapter 5**

### **Scientific Level Design:** **Space, Navigation, Rhythm** **and Challenge**


_Are video games bad for you? That's what they used to say about rock and roll._


_**-Shigeru Miyamoto-**_


81


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


Whether the play spaces manifest themselves as intricate three-dimensional
dungeons, labyrinthine cyberpunk cities, colorful two-dimensional platforming levels,
or vast procedurally generated galaxies, form the foundational stage on which the
player's interactive experience unfolds. **Level design**, the specific discipline that
deals with the conception, creation and orchestration of these environments, is a
crucial art and science that goes far beyond the simple aesthetic arrangement of
geometries and objects. Effective level design must support and enhance core game
mechanics, intuitively guide the player through space, offer a progression of
appropriate and engaging challenges, contribute significantly to atmosphere,
emotional tone, and storytelling (often through environmental storytelling), and
ultimately facilitate the achievement of states of flow and deep engagement. A
scientific approach to level design involves the conscious integration of principles
derived from cognitive architecture, the psychology of spatial and environmental
perception, learning theory and rigorous testing methodologies, in order to create
spaces that are not only aesthetically pleasing and thematically coherent, but also
functional, understandable, navigable and capable of generating the desired
dynamics and experiences in the player. This chapter will explore these principles,
analyzing how understanding human spatial perception and cognitive processes can
inform the creation of readable and meaningful spaces; how to design advanced
wayfinding systems and intuitive navigation; how to structure difficulty and learning
curves based on psychological models and empirical data; how to use procedural
content generation (PCG) to create breadth and variety, while maintaining control
over experiential quality; and, finally, how to apply the principles of accessibility and
universal design to ensure that game environments are usable by as many players
as possible.


**5.1. Fundamentals of Cognitive Architecture (Norman) and**
**Psychology of Spatial Perception (Gibson, Tuan, Lynch) in Level**
**Design: Creating Readable and Meaningful Spaces**


The design of play spaces, to be effective, cannot be separated from a deep
understanding of how human beings perceive, interpret, learn and interact with
environments, whether physical or virtual. Cognitive architecture and the psychology
of spatial and environmental perception offer a rich body of knowledge and principles
that can scientifically inform the level designer's decisions, helping him to create
spaces that are not only functional to the gameplay, but also intuitively navigable,
emotionally resonant and charged with meaning.


The principles of **cognitive architecture**, as articulated by **Donald Norman (1988)**
in his seminal text "The Design of Everyday Things" (originally "The Psychology of
Everyday Things"), although primarily focused on the design of objects and
interfaces, are extraordinarily pertinent to level design. Concepts such as
**affordances**, **signifiers**, **mapping**, **feedback** and **conceptual model** (already
introduced in Chapter 1.2 and Chapter 7) take on a crucial spatial value. The
**affordance** of a game environment refers to the perceived properties of space and
its elements that suggest to the player the possibilities of interaction. A rocky ledge
in _Uncharted 4_ "sluggish" from being grabbed and used to climb; a locked door but
with a visible lock "sluggish" from being opened with a key or forced; a vast plain
opened in _Red Dead Redemption 2_ "sluggish" exploration on horseback. A good level
design makes these environmental affordances clear and consistent with the
capabilities of the player character, reducing the need for explicit instructions and
allowing implicit learning of the possibilities of interaction. When an affordance isn't


82


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


immediately obvious, **signifiers** become crucial: a slight glow on an interactable
object, a visible crack on a wall that suggests its destructibility, or the sound of a
hidden mechanism can guide the player's attention. Spatial **mapping** is about the
consistency between the player's navigation actions (e.g., moving the analog stick)
and the resulting movement in 3D space, or the relationship between the activation
of a switch at one point on the level and the visible effect at another. Constant
environmental **feedback** (the sound of footsteps changing on different surfaces, the
reaction of objects to physics, changes in lighting) informs the player about their
interaction with space. Finally, effective level design helps the player build an
accurate **conceptual model** of the space and its rules, allowing them to plan routes,
anticipate hazards, and solve environmental puzzles. For example, the level design
of _Portal 2_ is entirely based on teaching and exploiting the conceptual model of how
portals work.


The **psychology of spatial perception**, which studies how we perceive the depth,
distance, shape, and orientation of objects and spaces, is equally fundamental.
**James J. Gibson's (1979)** theory of direct perception, with its original concept of
affordance as objective properties of the environment that offer possibilities for action
to an organism, emphasizes the importance of environmental optical information.
Level designers must consider how perspective, lighting, textures, and depth cues
(such as motion parallax, shading, linear perspective) affect the player's ability to
judge distances (crucial for jumps in a platformer like _Celeste_ ), perceive the
navigability of a path, or identify important objects. For example, the skillful use of
atmospheric fog or depth of field can guide the player's eye or create a sense of
vastness or mystery.


Environmental **psychology**, as defined by **Robert Gifford (2014),** explores the
transactional interrelationships between individuals and their socio-physical
environments. Concepts such as **environmental legibility** are crucial. A legible
environment is a space whose structure is easily understood and memorized,
allowing effective orientation. Urban planner **Kevin Lynch (1960),** in his classic "The
Image of the City", identified five key elements that contribute to the legibility of urban
spaces, but which are highly transferable to the design of complex game worlds:


1. **Paths:** Predominant channels of movement (roads, corridors, virtual rivers).
Their clarity, continuity and hierarchy are fundamental. In _Grand Theft Auto_
_V_, the Los Santos road network is a primary route system.
2. **Edges:** Linear elements that are not used or considered as paths by the
user, but that define the boundaries between areas or that interrupt
continuity (walls, coasts, impassable mountain ranges, the limits of the
game map).
3. **Districts:** Recognizable two-dimensional areas of the city (or game world)
that have a common character and a distinctive identity, in which the user
can mentally enter "inside". For example, the different themed areas of an
amusement park in _Planet Coaster_ (Frontier Developments, 2016) or the
distinct neighborhoods of Night City in _Cyberpunk 2077_ .
4. **Nodes:** Strategic points in an environment, such as important intersections,
squares, or places where paths converge, which act as focal and decisionmaking centers. A game's central hub, such as the Citadel in _Mass Effect_,
or a major transportation hub, can serve as nodes.
5. **Landmarks:** Distinctive, unique and easily identifiable point elements
external to the observer, which act as constant reference points for
orientation. They can be iconic buildings (the Eiffel Tower in a game set in


83


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


Paris), towering natural features (a volcanic mountain on the horizon in
_Breath of the Wild_ ), or unique structures created by the designer. Effective
landmarks are visible from afar and help build a mental map of space.


The conscious application of these elements of Lynch can transform a play space
from a confusing labyrinth to a navigable and memorable environment. In addition to
readability, environmental psychology explores the concept of **sense of place**, i.e.
the unique and meaningful quality that an environment assumes for an individual,
deriving from the combination of its physical characteristics, the activities that take
place there, the cultural or personal meanings associated with it, and the emotional
experiences lived in that place (cf. **Tuan, 1977**, "Space and Place: The Perspective
of Experience"). Level design that manages to create a strong sense of place, such
as the underwater city of Rapture in _BioShock_ (2K Boston/2K Australia, 2007) with
its decadent Art Deco architecture and oppressive history, or the initial village of
Shiganshina in _Attack on Titan 2_ (Omega Force, 2018) before its destruction, can
dramatically increase the immersion and emotional impact of the game.


Finally, evolutionary theories such as Jay Appleton's (1975) **perspective and refuge**
suggest that humans have an innate preference for environments that offer both a
good view of the surrounding environment (perspective, which allows them to identify
opportunities and dangers) and the possibility of hiding or protecting themselves
(refuge). This has direct implications for level design, especially in stealth games
(where shelters are crucial), survival games (where finding a safe haven is a primary
objective) or tactical games (where high positions offer an advantageous prospect).
The design of a camp in _Red Dead Redemption 2_ often offers both a good view of
the surrounding area and cover elements.


Scientifically oriented level designers, therefore, do not limit themselves to "sculpting"
geometries, but actively apply these principles of cognitive architecture and
environmental psychology to create spaces that are readable, navigable, meaningful,
emotionally resonant and that inherently support game mechanics and experiential
goals. This requires a deep understanding of how players perceive and interact with
spaces, and an iterative process of design and testing to validate the effectiveness
of design choices.


**5.2. Advanced Wayfinding and Intuitive Navigation: Landmark**
**Design, Spatial Affordances, Implicit Guidance (Leading Lines,**
**Composition), Cognitive Maps and Disorientation Prevention**


The player's ability to effectively navigate playful spaces, understand their position,
and orient themselves towards goals is critical to a smooth, rewarding, and
frustration-free gaming experience. **Wayfinding**, a term borrowed from architecture
and environmental psychology, refers to the cognitive and behavioral processes
involved in intentionally orienting and moving from one place to another within an


84


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


environment. Scientifically informed level design must devote meticulous attention to
the design of effective wayfinding systems, which can be either explicit (maps,
indicators) or, and often preferably, implicit and integrated into the environment itself.


As discussed in the previous section, the five key elements for the legibility of urban
spaces identified by **Kevin Lynch (1960)** - **Paths**, **Edges**, **Districts**, **Nodes** and
**Landmarks** - provide a powerful vocabulary and conceptual tool set for level
designers who aim to create navigable and memorable virtual game worlds.
**Landmark Design** is particularly crucial: the creation of unique, salient and easily
recognizable visual elements, strategically positioned within the play space, helps
players to build a **cognitive map** of the environment, i.e. a mental representation of
its spatial structure and the relationships between its components (cf. **Tolman, 1948**,
on the concept of "cognitive map" in animals; **Downs & Stea, 1973**, on application to
humans). Effective landmarks should be visible from different angles and distances,
acting as "anchors" for orientation. For example, the large clock tower in the Old
Yharnam district in _Bloodborne_ or Hyrule Castle visible from many areas of the map
in _The Legend of Zelda: Breath of the Wild_ serve as primary landmarks that constantly
help the player find their way around. Secondary or local landmarks, such as a
peculiar statue, an unusually shaped tree, or a building with a distinctive color, can
also define micro areas and facilitate short-range navigation.


Space **affordances**, as discussed by **Gibson (1979)** and applied to design by
**Norman (1988),** play a key role in intuitive navigation. The environment itself should
suggest the possibilities of movement and interaction. A well-traced path "forgives"
to be followed; a staircase "affords" to have climbed; A light at the end of a corridor
"affects" exploration in that direction. Level designers use a variety of **implicit**
**guidance** techniques to subtly direct the player without making excessive use of
explicit indicators that could break the immersion. Among these techniques, **leading**
**lines** are fundamental: architectural elements, patterns in the terrain, rivers, roads,
or even the direction of other characters' gaze can create visual lines that guide the
player's eye and movement to points of interest or objectives. The **visual**
**composition** of the scene, borrowing principles from painting and cinematography
(rule of thirds, vanishing point, contrast, color), can be used to create visual
hierarchies and to emphasize important paths or areas. Lighting is a particularly
powerful tool: well-lit areas tend to attract attention and suggest safety or importance,
while dark or shadowy areas can indicate danger, secrets, or secondary paths.
Games like _Alan Wake_ (Remedy Entertainment, 2010) or the _Thief_ series masterfully
use light and shadow not only to create atmosphere, but also as gameplay mechanics
and implicit driving. Spatial **sound design (3D audio)** can also contribute to
wayfinding, providing auditory clues about the location of important targets, enemies,
or environmental elements, such as the sound of a waterfall pointing to a water
source or the cry of a creature suggesting its direction.


Despite the effectiveness of implicit guidance, many games, especially those with
vast or complex worlds, also provide **explicit wayfinding tools** . In-game **maps** are
common, but their usefulness depends on their clarity, accuracy, ease of access, and
the player's ability to correlate the two-dimensional (or pseudo-three-dimensional)
representation of the map with the actual game environment. A good map should
allow the player to see their current location, objectives, discovered points of interest,
and ideally, set custom waypoints. Games like _Hollow Knight_ innovate the concept of
the map, requiring the player to purchase incomplete maps and actively update them
by exploring and finding the cartographer Cornifer, turning mapping itself into a game
mechanic. Objective **markers**, such as icons on the compass, arrows floating in the


85


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


world, or rays of light indicating the destination, are another common tool to guide the
player, especially in quests. While they can be very effective in reducing frustration
and keeping the player focused on the goal, overusing or overly prescriptive use of
them can undermine the player's sense of exploration, discovery, and autonomy,
leading to an experience of "following the icon" rather than active navigation. The
design of _Ghost of Tsushima_, with its innovative "wind guide" system that replaces
traditional HUD indicators with a diegetic signal (the wind blowing in the direction of
the target), is an example of an attempt to integrate explicit guidance in a more
immersive way.


Preventing **disorientation** is a crucial goal of navigation design. Spatial
disorientation can be extremely frustrating and can lead players to abandon the
game. The causes of disorientation can be many: excessively complex or labyrinthine
level layouts without sufficient landmarks; game cameras that behave unpredictably
or restrict your view; lack of clear feedback on location or direction; or environments
that lack distinctive features. To prevent disorientation, designers should:


1. **Create legible spaces:** Use Lynch's principles to structure environments in
a way that is understandable.
2. **Provide clear and consistent landmarks.**
3. **Design paths with good flow:** Avoid unnecessary dead ends or confusing
layouts.
4. **Ensure an effective camera:** The camera should support navigation and
spatial awareness, not hinder them.
5. **Offer optional orientation tools:** Maps, compasses, or the ability to view
the route to the current goal.
6. **Test navigability with real players:** Directly observing players as they try
to navigate a level is the best way to identify points of confusion or
frustration. Analyzing **heatmaps** generated by telemetry (see Chapter 8.3),
which show the most common paths players take and the areas where they
get stuck or move erratically, can provide valuable quantitative data for
wayfinding optimization.


Research in HCI and cognitive psychology on navigation in virtual environments (cf.
**Darken & Sibert, 1996** ; **Vinson, 1999** ) has informed many of these practices. For
example, it has been found that players tend to rely on different navigation strategies
(e.g., route-based orientation vs. orientation based on global cognitive maps) and
that the design of the environment can favor one or the other. Scientifically informed
level design considers this knowledge to create wayfinding systems that are not only
effective in guiding the player, but also support the sense of exploration, discovery,
and mastery of the play space, contributing positively to the overall gaming
experience and minimizing the frustration of simply "getting lost."


**5.3. Design of Difficulty and Learning Curves Based on**
**Psychological Models (Flow, Proximal Development Zone) and**
**Empirical Data (Playtesting, Analytics)**


Difficulty management and learning facilitation are two intrinsically linked and
absolutely central aspects in level design and, more generally, in game design. A
well-designed **difficulty curve** describes how the player's perceived challenge
evolves over time or as they progress through the game. The goal is to keep the


86


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


player in a state of **flow** (as theorized by **Csikszentmihalyi, 1990**, see Chapter 2.1),
i.e. in an optimal balance between the challenge presented and the player's skills,
thus preventing boredom (if the game is perceived as too easy) and excessive
frustration or anxiety (if perceived as too difficult or unfair). This difficulty curve is
closely linked to the player' **s learning curve**, which is the process by which he
acquires the knowledge, motor skills and strategies necessary to master the
mechanics and systems of the game. A scientific approach to the design of these
curves is based on the integration of established psychological models and empirical
validation through playtesting and data analysis.


Csikszentmihalyi's Flow Theory is particularly pertinent: the flow state, characterized
by deep concentration, loss of track of time, and intrinsic gratification, occurs when
there is a dynamic balance between the challenge of the activity and the individual's
perceived abilities. As the player's skills increase through learning and practice, the
challenge presented by the game must also increase proportionally to keep the player
within the "flow channel". **Sweetser and Wyeth (2005),** in their **GameFlow** model,
adapted the flow criteria specifically to the context of video games, identifying
"challenge" as one of the eight core components (along with focus, control, clear
goals, feedback, immersion, social opportunities, and interface clarity). A difficulty
curve that adapts or closely follows the player's learning curve is therefore essential
to meet this criterion.


Another relevant psychological concept is the **Zone of Proximal Development**
**(ZPD),** introduced by **Lev Vygotsky (1978).** The ZPD is defined as the difference
between what a student (or player) can do on their own and what they can do with
the help or guidance of another more experienced (or, by extension, with a good
scaffolding design in the game). The tasks found within the ZPD are those slightly
above the player's current skill level, but achievable with effort and support.
Presenting challenges within the ZPD promotes learning and a sense of competence.
Effective level design introduces new mechanics or increases the complexity of
challenges so that they remain predominantly within the target player's ZPD, while
providing sufficient "scaffolding" (temporary support, such as clear tutorials, formative
feedback, or the ability to experiment safely) to facilitate the transition to the next level
of proficiency. The principle of **scaffolding**, developed by **Wood, Bruner and Ross**
**(1976),** is therefore crucial: the game should provide the right kind and amount of
support when the player is learning something new, and then gradually withdraw that
support as the player becomes more competent. The first level of _Super Mario Bros._
(Nintendo, 1985) is a masterful example of implicit scaffolding: the placement of the
first Goomba, the first "?" block, and the first ravine teaches the fundamental
mechanics (jump, hit, avoid) sequentially and safely.


While each game can, and often should, have its own unique difficulty curve that is
carefully calibrated to the genre and desired experience, it is possible to identify some
**general patterns or archetypes of difficulty curves** . An **increasing linear curve**
implies a constant and gradual increase in difficulty from the beginning to the end of
the game. It's relatively simple to implement, but it may not fit well with all individual
learning paces or games that require complex mechanics to be introduced at specific
times. A **stepped curve**, on the other hand, has periods of relatively constant
difficulty, during which the player can consolidate the skills learned and feel
competent, followed by sharper increases in challenge, often coinciding with the
introduction of new mechanics, enemy types or environmental complexities. This
creates "plateaus" of mastery followed by new peaks of learning and challenge. Many
role-playing or strategy games implicitly adopt this pattern, with the introduction of


87


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


new areas or game systems. A **wave-like curve strategically** alternates periods of
high difficulty and tension with periods of relative calm, lower intensity, or
opportunities for exploration and recovery. This approach is very effective for
managing the **pacing** (pace) of the game, preventing player fatigue and creating a
more dynamic emotional arc. Action games like _DOOM Eternal_ often use this pattern,
alternating between intense combat arenas and sections of platforming or
exploration. There are also **exponential curves**, where the difficulty increases very
slowly at the beginning and then accelerates drastically towards the end, typical of
games that require a deep mastery of advanced mechanics to face the final
challenges (some "bullet hell" games or high-difficulty rhythm games). Finally, many
games feature **curves with intentional peaks and valleys**, where designers insert
specific difficulty spikes (such as the iconic and often brutal "boss fights" in
FromSoftware games such as _Dark Souls_ or _Bloodborne_ ) that serve as tests of skill
and turning points, often followed by significant rewards or periods of truce and
narrative progression.


It is crucial to understand that **difficulty is not an objective attribute of the game,**
**but a subjective perception of the player** . This perception is influenced by a myriad
of interconnected factors, which the designer must consider. These include the
**intrinsic complexity of the mechanics** (how many different actions the player has
to handle, how they interact with each other), the **cognitive demands** imposed on
the player (the attentional load necessary to monitor the environment, the amount of
information to be kept in working memory, the processing speed required to make
decisions under pressure), motor **demands** (the precision and speed of the controls
needed to perform actions, which are particularly relevant in action or fighting
games), the presence of **time pressure** (explicit or implicit time limits to complete
actions or levels, such as in classic arcade games), the **clarity of objectives and**
**feedback** (if the player does not understand what he has to do or how his actions
affect the game, the experience may seem arbitrarily difficult or frustrating), the
**player's familiarity with the game genre** and its conventions (players experienced
in a genre may perceive difficulty very differently from newbies), the **emotional and**
**physiological state of the player** (stress, fatigue, external distractions can
significantly increase the perceived difficulty), and finally the presence and
management of **randomness (RNG - Random Number Generation).** Excessive
randomness, especially if perceived as unfair or decisive for success beyond the
player's skill, can make difficulty unpredictable and deeply frustrating (e.g. a "critical
miss" at a crucial moment in a tactical RPG).


Designing and calibrating an effective difficulty curve and learning is an eminently
**iterative, evidence-based process** that requires a scientific approach. You start by
clearly defining the **desired experience** and target **audience** (a "hardcore" game for
veterans will have a very different curve from a "casual" game for a wide audience).
It is crucial **to gradually introduce game mechanics** and concepts, starting from
the basics and progressively building complexity, applying the principles of
scaffolding to support learning. Rapid **prototyping** of specific segments of the game
that represent different points on the desired difficulty curve is essential, followed by
**rigorous playtesting** with players representative of the target audience. During
playtesting, it is crucial to collect both **quantitative data** (using telemetry to track
metrics such as success/failure rates per challenge, completion times, specific points
where players get stuck ("choke points") or leave the game, number of attempts it
takes to overcome an obstacle) and **qualitative** feedback (through direct
observations, think-aloud protocols, post-session interviews, to understand the
subjective perception of difficulty by players, their moments of frustration or boredom,


88


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


and the learning strategies they adopt). The analysis of this data allows you to identify
discrepancies between the designed and perceived difficulty, and to **iterate on the**
**design** by changing the parameters of the level (e.g. enemy placement, space layout,
availability of resources), the mechanics themselves, the clarity of the feedback or
the structure of the tutorial. In some cases, especially for games intended for
audiences with very heterogeneous skill levels, you may consider implementing
**explicit player-selectable difficulty options** (Easy, Normal, Hard), or, with greater
caution and complexity, **Adaptive Dynamic Difficulty (DDA)** systems that try to
calibrate the challenge in real time (see Chapter 9.4).


A classic example of excellent learning curve design and difficulty, which teaches
implicitly through level design, is Super **Mario Bros.** _**World 1-1.**_ (Nintendo, 1985). As
analyzed by numerous designers and scholars (e.g. **Extra Credits**, **Game Maker's**
**Toolkit** ), this level masterfully and sequentially introduces all the fundamental
mechanics of the game (movement, jumping, running, hitting blocks, defeating
enemies, collecting power-ups) in a relatively safe environment that allows
experimentation and learning from mistakes, before gradually increasing complexity
and challenge. Evidence-based design, which combines the designer's insight and
vision with rigorous user testing cycles and careful data analysis, is key to creating
difficulty and learning curves that are not only effective in keeping the player in the
flow channel, but also foster a sense of progressive mastery and deep satisfaction
from overcoming significant challenges.


**5.4. Procedural Content Generation (PCG) for Play Spaces:**
**Advanced Algorithms (e.g. Wave Function Collapse, L-Systems),**
**Authorial Control vs. Emergence, and Evaluation of the**
**Experiential Quality of Generated Content**


**Procedural Content Generation (PCG)** in the context of game design refers to the
use of algorithms and computational processes to create game content – such as
levels, maps, terrain, objects, textures, characters, quests, narratives, and even
music – automatically or semi-automatically, with limited or indirect human input,
rather than through entirely manual and static creation by designers. As defined by
**Togelius et al. (2011),** PCG is "the algorithmic creation of game content". This
technique has become an increasingly important and versatile tool in the game
designer's repertoire, especially for the creation of vast, varied or replayable play
spaces. A scientific approach to PCG involves not only understanding the underlying
algorithms, but also a critical reflection on its objectives, its limitations, the tension
between authorial control and emergence, and methodologies for assessing the
experiential quality of the generated content.


There **are many goals and benefits** to using PCG in level design and other aspects
of the game. One of the main ones is the ability to generate **vastness and variety** of
content with a potentially lower development effort and production cost than manually
creating each individual asset or layer. This is especially evident in games with huge
open worlds, such as the almost infinite universe of _No Man's Sky_ (Hello Games,
2016), where planets, flora, fauna, and star systems are procedurally generated,
offering a virtually limitless exploration experience. PCG is also crucial for the
**replayability** of many genres, especially **roguelikes** and **roguelites** . Games such


89


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


as _Spelunky 2_ (Mossmouth, 2020), _The Binding of Isaac_ (Edmund McMillen & Florian
Himsl, 2011), or _Hades_ (Supergiant Games, 2020) use PCG to generate levels,
enemy layouts, and power-up sequences that are always different with each
playthrough, ensuring that each "run" is a unique and unpredictable experience,
requiring adaptability and continuous learning on the part of the player. **Derek Yu**
**(2016),** in his book on _Spelunky_, discusses in depth the design of PCG systems that
maintain a sense of consistency and balanced challenge. PCG can also be used to
create **surprise and discovery**, presenting players with new and unexpected
content that could not be easily anticipated. In addition, PCG systems can be
designed to be **adaptive**, generating content that changes to the player's playstyle,
skills, or preferences (a concept known as experience-driven PCG). **Yannakakis &**
**Togelius, 2018** ; see also Chapter 9.4). Finally, PCG allows the creation of content
that would simply be **impossible or prohibitive to achieve manually**, such as
generating infinitesimal details in terrain or simulating complex ecosystems.


However, PCG also has **significant disadvantages and challenges** . One of the
main ones is the difficulty for designers to maintain **precise authorial control** over
the quality, thematic consistency, emotional impact, and specific experience offered
by each instance of generated content. PCG results can sometimes be bland,
generic, repetitive, meaningless, or, at worst, unbalanced, confusing, or even
"broken" (e.g., levels that are impossible to complete, inconsistent geometries).
There is often a tension between the amount of variation that can be generated and
the average quality of the content. Procedurally generated content can sometimes
lack the "human touch," craftsmanship, artistic intentionality, and emotional or
narrative depth that characterize levels and meticulously hand-designed content.
Testing **difficulty** is another considerable challenge: how do you test and ensure the
quality of a system that can generate a virtually infinite number of content variations?
Finally, the **complexity of developing** procedural generators themselves should not
be underestimated: creating sophisticated, flexible, and reliable PCG algorithms is a
task that requires specialized skills in programming, mathematics, and, ideally, a
deep understanding of the design principles you want to incorporate into the
algorithm.


There is a wide range of **algorithmic approaches** to PCG for the creation of play
spaces, as extensively covered by **Short and Adams (2017)** in "Procedural Content
Generation in Games" and by **Hendrikx et al. (2013)** in their review. Some of the
most common and advanced approaches include:


  - **Noise-Based Algorithms:** Use mathematical noise functions, such as
Perlin noise (developed by **Ken Perlin, 1985** ) or Simplex noise, to generate
height maps for natural-looking terrain, biome distributions, procedural
textures, or the shape of caves and dungeons. _Minecraft_ uses noise
features extensively for the generation of its worlds.

  - **Agent-Based Systems:** Simulate the behavior of autonomous agents
moving in a space and modify it according to simple rules. For example,
agents who "dig" tunnels into a solid to create a dungeon, or agents who
simulate urban growth to generate cities.

  - **Generative Grammars:** They use formal rewriting rules, similar to the
grammars used in linguistics to describe sentence structure, to generate
complex spatial structures from initial symbols and production rules. The
**Shape Grammars** (cf. **Stiny & Gips, 1971** ) have been used to generate


90


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


architectures, while **Graph Grammars** can define the connectivity and
logical structure of layers.

  - **L-Systems (Lindenmayer Systems):** Developed by biologist **Aristid**
**Lindenmayer (1968)** to model plant growth, L-Systems are a type of parallel
grammar that can generate complex, organic fractal structures, applicable
to the creation of trees, road networks, or natural-looking dungeon layouts.

  - **Constraint Satisfaction Problems (CSPs):** In this approach, the designer
defines a set of constraints that the generated content must satisfy (e.g.
"each room in this dungeon must have at least two exits", "the main treasure
must be reachable from the initial room within a certain number of steps",
"there must be no unreachable areas"). Search algorithms (such as
backtracking or optimization algorithms) are then used to find solutions that
satisfy all (or as many as possible) of these constraints. The **Wave Function**
**Collapse (WFC)** algorithm, popularized by **Maxim Gumin**, is a recent and
powerful example of a local constraint and propagation approach that
generates consistent maps and tilemaps from a set of examples or
adjacency rules.

  - **Evolutionary Algorithms (EAs):** Inspired by the principles of biological
evolution (selection, mutation, crossover), EAs operate on a population of
possible solutions (e.g. layer layouts), evaluating them against a "fitness
function" (which measures their quality or compliance with certain design
criteria) and iteratively evolving the population towards better solutions (cf.
**Eiben & Smith, 2015** ). They can be used to generate levels that are, for
example, aesthetically pleasing, balanced, or offer a certain type of
challenge.

  - **Machine Learning (ML) for PCG:** This is a rapidly growing area of
research. You can train ML models (such as generative adversarial neural
networks - GANs, variational autoencoders - VAEs, or transformers) on
large datasets of human-created content (e.g. _Super Mario Bros._, _DOOM_
maps) to make the model learn the underlying stylistic and structural
patterns. The trained model can then be used to generate new original
content but in the same style (this approach is often called Example-Based
PCG). **Summerville et al., 2018** ).


Assessing **the quality of** procedurally generated content is an inherent and crucial
challenge. Evaluation metrics can be **objective/technical**, such as playability (is the
level completeable from start to finish?), the absence of bugs or geometric glitches,
compliance with explicit design constraints, or the computational performance of the
generator. However, these metrics often don't capture the **subjective experiential**
**quality** : how fun, engaging, aesthetically pleasing, of the right difficulty the level is,
or whether it offers a sense of coherence and meaning. To assess these aspects, it
is often necessary to resort to **playtesting with human users** (see Chapter 8) or the
use of **computational models of the player experience** (which try to predict the
player's response based on characteristics of the content, cf. **Yannakakis &**
**Togelius, 2018** ). **Smith and Whitehead (2010)** explored the use of computational
metrics (e.g., linearity, symmetry, enemy density) to guide level generation and to
approximate perceived quality.


Often, the most effective and practical approach is not a fully autonomous PCG
("push-button PCG"), but rather a **designer-assisted PCG** or **mixed-initiative**
**design** . In these paradigms, procedural algorithms serve as tools that augment the
capabilities of the human designer, generating a base of content, variations, or


91


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


suggestions, which the designer can then refine, customize, select, or correct. This
allows us to combine the advantages of PCG's speed, variety and surprise with the
authorial control, artistic vision and contextual sensitivity of the human designer (cf.
**Liapis, Yannakakis, & Togelius, 2014**, on their "Sentient Sketchbook" system that
exemplifies this approach). For example, a level designer might use a PCG algorithm
to generate the rough structure of a dungeon, and then manually populate it with
specific encounters, treasures, and narrative details.


PCG is an extremely active and promising field of research and development in game
design, which sits at the intersection of creativity, mathematics and computer
science. A scientific approach to its implementation requires a clear definition of the
design objectives to be achieved with procedurality, a careful choice and a thorough
understanding of algorithms and their parameters, a robust evaluation methodology
that considers both technical and experiential aspects, and a critical reflection on the
tension between the desired authorial control and the potential for emergence and
surprise that PCG can offer. Effective use of PCG can lead to more dynamic,
replayable games, and in some cases, playful experiences of an otherwise
unattainable scale and variety.


**5.5. Accessibility and Universal Design in Game Environments:**
**Principles, Specific Techniques for Level Design (e.g. multiple**
**paths, visual contrast, clear signage) and Testing with Users**
**with Disabilities**


Accessibility in game design, and particularly level design, refers to the ethical and
scientifically informed practice of creating game environments that can be perceived,
understood, navigated, and interacted with by as many people as possible,
regardless of their physical, sensory, or cognitive abilities. It is an imperative that
promotes inclusion and equity, and that, from a pragmatic perspective, can also
significantly expand the potential audience of a game. **Universal Design**, as
conceptualized by **Ron Mace** and developed by the Center for Universal Design at
North Carolina State University (cf. **Stephanidis, 2009**, for an overview), is a
philosophical approach and set of principles that aim to design products and
environments so that they are usable by everyone, to the greatest extent possible,
without the need for specialized adaptations or separate designs. While complete
universality is a difficult ideal to achieve in any context, adopting these principles can
guide level designers towards creating more flexible and accommodating spaces.


When designing for accessibility in gaming environments, it's critical to consider a
wide range of potential barriers that gamers with disabilities may face. As for **visual**
**impairments**, these range from complete blindness to low vision (reduced vision,
limited peripheral vision, contrast sensitivity) and color blindness. For blind gamers,
level design must consider comprehensive non-visual alternatives for navigation and
interaction, such as **highly accurate spatial sound design** that provides information
about the geometry of the space, the location of objects, enemies, and exits (e.g., the
use of binaural audio in games like _Hellblade: Senua's Sacrifice_, Ninja Theory, 2017,
although not primarily for blindness, demonstrates the potential of immersive audio),
haptic feedback, and, ideally, optional audio descriptions of environments. For
visually impaired players, level design should ensure a **high visual contrast**
between important elements (characters, interactable objects, hazards) and the
background, offer options to **enlarge the user interface and world elements**, and


92


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


allow **for color customization** or the use of high-contrast modes. The **legibility of**
**environmental elements**, the clarity of paths and the ability to perceive obstacles
and enemies without relying solely on subtle visual details are crucial. For example,
avoid excessively "noisy" environmental textures that can mask important objects.
For **color blindness**, it is crucial not to base crucial gameplay information (e.g.,
indicating a friendly or hostile enemy, the rarity of an item, the status of a mechanism)
solely on color distinctions (red/green, blue/yellow). Shapes, patterns, icons, text
labels, or brightness variations should also be used to convey this information, and
offer colorblind-specific modes that remap problematic colors, as is the case in many
modern games, such as _Overwatch 2_ or _Apex Legends_ .


For **hearing disabilities**, which include complete deafness or varying degrees of
hearing loss, level design must ensure that no gameplay-critical information is
conveyed _solely_ through sound. It is essential to provide **accurate and customizable**
**subtitles** for all dialogues and **captions for** important environmental sounds (e.g. an
alarm signal, the footsteps of an approaching enemy, the sound of a mechanism that
is activated). These captions should, ideally, also include an indication of the direction
the sound is coming from. Level design should avoid basing the solution of puzzles,
the identification of imminent dangers or the location of objectives solely on sound
cues. For example, if an enemy makes a characteristic sound before attacking from
behind, there should also be a visual indicator (e.g. a directional icon on the screen)
for players who cannot hear the sound alert. Games like _The Last of Us Part II_ have
been praised for their extensive accessibility options, including the display of sound
effects.


Motor **disabilities** can vary widely, affecting strength, dexterity, coordination,
reaction speed, or the ability to perform complex or prolonged inputs. Level design
can contribute to accessibility by offering **multiple paths** through an area, some of
which may require less motor accuracy or speed. The **generosity of hitboxes** for
interacting with objects or overcoming obstacles (e.g. the width of a platform to jump
on) can be a factor. Avoid sections that require extremely fast and precise input
sequences without alternatives or optional aids (such as "bullet time" or the ability to
slow down the game at certain times). The design of the spaces should also consider
ease of traversal, avoiding geometries that can easily trap the character or require
overly complex maneuvers to overcome simple obstacles. As discussed in Chapter
7.5, full control remapping options, support for alternate inputs, and the ability to turn
"hold" actions into "toggle" actions are key at the interface level, but level design
needs to be aware of how these options translate into interaction with space.


For **cognitive and learning disabilities**, which may affect attention, memory,
language comprehension, problem solving, or spatial orientation, level design should
aim for **clarity and the reduction of unnecessary cognitive load** . This includes
providing **clear, concise, and repeatable tutorials** for new mechanics introduced in
a level (see Chapter 2.3 on learning); offering **visual or textual reminders of current**
**goals** ; designing **robust wayfinding systems** with clear landmarks and, if
necessary, explicit guidance options (see Section 5.2); avoiding overly labyrinthine
or confusing level layouts without strong justification thematic or gameplay; and
structure learning **and difficulty gradually and progressively** (see Section 5.3). For
players with dyslexia, the use of readable fonts and options to adjust the size of the
text in all environmental elements that contain it (e.g. signs, notes) is important.
Offering options to simplify puzzles or reduce enemy density or aggression can make
the game more accessible to those who struggle with complex problem solving or
handling high-pressure situations.


93


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


The **seven principles of Universal Design** (Equity of Use, Flexibility in Use, Simple
and Intuitive Use, Perceivable Information, Fault Tolerance, Low Physical Effort, Size
and Space for Approach and Use – see Chapter 7.5 for a more detailed description)
can be applied specifically to level design. For example, "Flexibility in Use" could
translate into offering multiple routes with different levels of challenge or requiring
different skills to overcome. "Fault tolerance" could mean placing frequent
checkpoints before difficult sections, or designing platforms with slightly "magnetic"
edges to forgive small jump mistakes. "Perceivable Information" implies the use of
**clear signage** within the environment (e.g., standardized icons to indicate save
points, hazards, or types of interactions) and adequate **visual contrast** between
important elements and the background.


Resources such as the **Game** **Accessibility** **Guidelines**
**(gameaccessibilityguidelines.com),** developed by a consortium of industry
specialists, and initiatives from organizations such as **the AbleGamers Foundation**
or the **IGDA Game Accessibility Special Interest Group (SIG)** offer detailed
checklists, best practices based on research and first-hand experience from gamers
with disabilities, and practical tools for designers. These resources cover specific
aspects of level design such as designing accessible puzzles, managing the camera,
and creating enemy encounters that are challenging but not unfairly punishing for
players with certain limitations.


Fundamentally, a scientific and ethical approach to accessibility in level design
involves an **inclusive design process from the earliest stages**, actively
considering the needs of a wide range of users. This includes creating personas with
different abilities during the concept phase, prototyping accessibility options, and,
most importantly, **iterative testing with users with real disabilities** . Their direct
feedback is irreplaceable to identify barriers often invisible to able-bodied designers
and to validate the effectiveness of the accessibility solutions implemented.
Designing accessible play spaces does not necessarily mean "simplifying" play for
everyone or compromising the artistic vision, but rather offering **options, flexibility**
**and information redundancy** so that a wider audience can fully participate and
enjoy the gaming experience. Often, design solutions designed for accessibility end
up improving usability and the overall experience for all players.


**5.6. Conclusions: Level Design as an Orchestration of the**
**Player's Spatial and Temporal Experience**


This chapter explored the complex and multifaceted discipline of level design,
highlighting how a scientific approach, informed by the principles of cognitive and
environmental psychology, HCI and user research methodologies, is fundamental to
creating play spaces that are not only aesthetically satisfying, but also functional,
engaging and meaningful for the player. We have seen how understanding how
players perceive, navigate and build mental models of virtual environments is crucial
to designing readable and intuitive spaces. The application of concepts such as
spatial affordances, effective landmark design, and the skillful use of implicit guidance
through visual composition and lighting are essential tools to facilitate wayfinding and
prevent the frustration of disorientation.


The careful design of the difficulty and learning curves, based on psychological
models such as Flow and the Proximal Development Zone, and validated through the
analysis of empirical data from playtesting and game analytics, was found to be a key


94


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


element in maintaining player engagement, offering a dynamic balance between
challenge and perceived skill. We also examined the growing role of Procedural
Content Generation (PCG) as a tool for creating breadth, variety, and replayability in
gaming spaces, while acknowledging the challenges of authorial control, experiential
quality, and the need for appropriate evaluation methodologies. The mixed-initiative
approach, which combines the power of procedural algorithms with the vision and
refinement of the human designer, often emerges as the most promising solution.


Finally, we emphasized the essential importance of accessibility and Universal
Design in the design of game environments. Creating spaces that are perceivable,
operable, and understandable by as many players as possible, regardless of their
abilities, is not only an ethical imperative of inclusion, but a practice that often leads
to better, more robust design for everyone. Applying specific guidelines and testing
with users with disabilities are key steps in this process.


In conclusion, scientific level design is configured as a discipline that orchestrates the
player's spatial and temporal experience. The level designer, acting as an architect
of virtual worlds, must expertly balance the needs of the gameplay, narrative
objectives, aesthetic considerations and the cognitive and perceptual abilities of the
player. Through an iterative process of design, prototyping, testing, and evidencebased analysis, the scientifically informed level designer can transform empty spaces
into "fields of possibility" (Salen & Zimmerman), capable of generating significant
challenges, rewarding discoveries, deep emotions, and ultimately memorable and
impactful gaming experiences. Level design isn't simply about creating maps; it is the
sculpture of the player's own experience within the game world.


95


GAME DESIGN MANUAL

# **Chapter 6**

### **World-Building and** **Interactive Fiction: Creating** **Credible Universes and** **Immersive Stories**


_Games are the only form of media that consumers want to be longer_


_**Jesse Schell**_


96


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


The ability to tell stories and build imaginary worlds is one of the oldest and most
powerful human faculties. In video games, **interactive narrative** and **world-building**
(the creation of fictional universes) are fundamental components that can transform
a simple set of mechanics into a deeply immersive, emotionally resonant and
culturally meaningful experience. While not all games place storytelling at the center
of their experience – think abstract games like _Tetris_ or many puzzle games – those
that choose to incorporate story elements, complex characters, or rich, detailed
settings can benefit enormously from a scientific and methodological approach to
their design. This chapter will delve into the complexities of creating interactive
narratives and believable virtual worlds. We will start by re-examining the historical
and contemporary debate between **narratology** (the study of stories) and **ludology**
(the study of games), looking for integrated models that recognize the specificity of
videogame storytelling. We will then explore different **structures and technologies**
**for interactive fiction**, analyzing the evolution from classic branching choices to
dynamic systems driven by artificial intelligence and emergent storytelling. Next, we
will address the **scientific and artistic principles of advanced world-building**,
discussing how to create fictional universes that are internally coherent, plausible and
endowed with a historical, cultural and social depth capable of supporting the player's
immersion, also taking inspiration from masters of subcreation such as J.R.R.
Tolkien. We will then focus on **psychologically grounded character design**,
examining how to create player characters (PCs) and non-player characters (NPCs)
that are memorable, have believable motivations, capable of complex story arcs and
dynamic relationships, and how behavioral AI can contribute to their emotional and
social expression. Finally, we will analyze the **advanced techniques of**
**environmental storytelling and implicit narrative**, i.e. how to construct meaning
and convey the story through the active exploration of the environment by the player,
the interpretation of clues and his participation in narrative co-construction. The
overall goal is to provide designers with the theoretical and practical tools to create
narratives and worlds that are not simple backgrounds for gameplay, but interacting
systems that deeply enrich the gaming experience, making it potentially
transformative.


**6.1. The Narratology vs. Ludology Debate Reconsidered:**
**Towards Integrated Models of Play and Storytelling (Jenkins,**
**Juul, Ryan)**


The academic study of narrative in video games has historically been characterized
by a lively, and sometimes heated, debate between two main perspectives:
**narratology** and **ludology** . Understanding the origins, key positions, and evolution
of this debate is essential for a scientific and informed approach to narrative design
in games, as it has shaped the way we think about the relationship between story
and gameplay.


**Narratology**, as a discipline, deals with the study of narrative in all its forms,
analyzing its structure, functions, mechanisms and conventions. Drawing on a long
and rich tradition of literary, semiotic and structuralist studies, with seminal figures
such as **Vladimir Propp (1928)** and his "Morphology of the Fairy Tale", **Roland**
**Barthes (1966)** and his "Introduction to the Structural Analysis of Tales", and **Gérard**
**Genette (1972)** with "Figure III. Narrative Discourse", narratology has developed a
sophisticated conceptual apparatus to analyze elements such as the distinction


97


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


between story (fabula/narrative content) and discourse (plot/how the story is told),
narrative time (order, duration, frequency), point of view (focus), character
characterization and narrative functions. When video games began to feature
complex plots and characters, it became natural for many scholars to apply these
narratological tools to analyze them, treating them, to some extent, as new forms of
storytelling. Games such as Infocom's text adventures (e.g. _Zork_ ) or the graphic
adventures of Sierra and LucasArts (e.g. _King's Quest_, _The Secret of Monkey Island_ )
seemed to lend themselves well to this type of analysis.


However, in the early 2000s, a new perspective emerged, **ludology** (from the Latin
_ludus_, game), which claimed the specificity of games as a medium and the need for
an analytical approach focused primarily on their intrinsic playful components: the
rules, mechanics, game systems, and gameplay experience. Scholars such as
**Gonzalo Frasca (2003),** in his influential article "Simulation versus Narrative:
Introduction to Ludology", and **Jesper Juul (2005),** in "Half-Real: Video Games
between Real Rules and Fictional Worlds", argued that indiscriminately applying the
categories of traditional narratology to video games risked obscuring their unique
qualities, particularly their interactive, systemic, and rule-based nature. The most
radical ludologists argued that games are not primarily stories to be "read" or
"interpreted" in the traditional sense, but rather systems to be "played" and
"mastered". They emphasized the importance of gameplay as a primary experience,
the simulative nature of many games (allowing players to experiment with rule
systems and observe their consequences), and the potential for the emergence of
unpredictable dynamics and outcomes. For some, storytelling in games was seen as
a secondary element, a "reward" or a simple "frame" for the mechanics (the so-called
"carrot narrative"). This led to a period of heated debate, often labeled the
"narratology vs. ludology" dispute.


With the passage of time and the maturation of Game Studies as an interdisciplinary
field, the debate has evolved towards more nuanced, synthetic and integrated
positions. Many scholars and designers today recognize that a rigid dichotomy
between narrative and gameplay is often counterproductive and that, indeed, these
two aspects can – and in many successful games do – enrich and enhance each
other. **Henry Jenkins (2004),** in his essay "Game Design as Narrative Architecture",
proposed a conciliatory vision, suggesting that the game designer should be seen
not so much as a writer of linear stories, but as a "narrative architect". In this
perspective, the designer creates spaces, systems and contexts rich in narrative
potential (the **embedded narrative**, which includes the background of the characters,
the history of the world, scripted events, objects with a story), which the player can
then explore, activate and interpret through their actions and interactions, thus
generating an **emergent narrative** ), which are unique and personal stories that are
dynamically created through gameplay. An example of embedded narrative would be
the detailed lore of the _Mass Effect_ universe, while Commander Shepard's specific
choices and their consequences, as well as the relationships developed with
teammates, contribute to an emergent narrative unique to each player.


**Marie-Laure Ryan (2001, 2006),** in seminal works such as "Narrative as Virtual
Reality: Immersion and Interactivity in Literature and Electronic Media" and "Avatars
of Story", explored in depth the relationship between interactivity, immersion and
narration, proposing models for understanding how players "inhabit" the narrative
worlds of games and how the player's agency can be integrated into narrative
structures. Ryan distinguishes between different forms of narrative interactivity, from
the external (where the user chooses predefined paths) to the internal (where the


98


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


user influences the development of the story through actions within the diegetic
world).


**Jesper Juul (2005),** despite being a key figure in ludology, in "Half-Real" analyzes
the complex and often tense coexistence between the **real rules** of games (formal
and abstract systems) and the **fictional worlds** they represent. He argues that video
games are "half real" (the rules are concrete and impose real consequences within
the game) and "half fiction" (the world, characters and story are imaginary
representations). The pleasure of play often derives precisely from this interaction
between the abstraction of rules and the concreteness of fiction. For example, in _Red_
_Dead Redemption 2_, the rules of combat and survival are "real" within the game
system, but are lived through the fiction of being an outlaw in the Wild West.


A contemporary scientific approach to narrative design in video games therefore
tends to:


1. **Recognizing the Specificity of the Gaming Medium:** Games tell stories
and create meaning in ways that are unique and distinct from literature, film,
or theater, primarily through **interactivity**, **player agency**, **systems**
**simulation,** and **emergent** gameplay. It's not simply about "adding a story"
to a game, but about integrating storytelling with mechanics in an organic
way.
2. **Adopt an Integrated and Interdisciplinary Approach:** Use the concepts
and analytical tools of classical narratology (e.g. to analyze plot structure,
character development, themes) and semiotics, but integrating them with
the perspectives of ludology (to understand how game mechanics, rule
systems and emerging dynamics can convey, support or even generate
narrative) and cognitive psychology (to understand how players perceive,
interpret, and engage with interactive narratives).
3. **Focus on Player Experience:** Investigate empirically (through playtesting,
interviews, analysis of game data – see Chapter 8) how players interpret
narratives, how their choices affect their perception of the story, what
emotions are evoked, and how the narrative experience contributes to
overall engagement.
4. **Consider a Wide Spectrum of Relationships Between Game and Story:**
Recognize that there is no single "correct" way to integrate narrative and
gameplay. They can range from games with a strong emphasis on
**cinematic and linear storytelling** (such as the _Uncharted_ series), to
games that offer **branching narrative choices** with significant
consequences (such as _Detroit: Become Human_ ), to games where the
**narrative is primarily environmental or implicit**, leaving a lot of room for
interpretation and reconstruction by the player (such as _Dark Souls_ or
_Inside_, Playdead, 2016), up to games that are real **emerging "story**
**generators"** (such as _Dwarf Fortress_ or _The Sims_, where unique stories
arise from systemic interactions and the unpredictable actions of characters
or players). Even seemingly abstract or purely mechanical games can have
a "gameplay narrative," which is the story of the player's actions, challenges,
and achievements within the rules system.


The challenge for the contemporary game designer, therefore, is not so much to
choose between "narrative" or "gameplay", but rather to find the right balance, the
right form of integration and the right synergy between these two fundamental


99


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


aspects, based on the specific experiential objectives of the game you intend to
create. This requires a deep understanding of both storytelling techniques (traditional
and interactive) and the unique ways in which interactive systems and game
mechanics can create meaning, convey themes, and generate deep and lasting
narrative engagement. Academic research and design practice continue to explore
formal models for interactive narrative, technologies for its implementation (such as
narrative AI), and methods for assessing the impact of storytelling on the overall
player experience.


**6.2. Advanced Structures and Technologies for Interactive**
**Narrative: From Complex Branched Choices (e.g. "Choice-Based**
**Games") to Narrative AI (Drama Management, Story Generation)**
**and Emerging Dynamic Systems**


Interactive storytelling, as a distinctive form of video game storytelling, manifests itself
through a variety of structures and technologies, each with its own affordances,
limitations, and implications for the player's agency and overall narrative experience.
Technological evolution and the increasing sophistication of design thinking have led
to a move beyond simpler models, paving the way for more dynamic, personalized
and emerging narrative systems.


One of the most traditional and intuitive models for implementing narrative
interactivity is that of **branching narratives** . Popularized by the "Choose Your Own
Adventure" series of gamebooks and early text and graphic adventures such as _Zork_
(Infocom) or _The Secret of Monkey Island_ (LucasArts, 1990), this approach presents
the player with a series of **explicit choice points**, typically in the form of dialogue
options or action decisions. Each choice leads to a different narrative segment, a
different plot development or an alternative outcome. The overall structure can be
visualized as a tree or a graph with multiple paths, where the designer has strong
authorial control, predefining all options and their consequences. The player's agency
is primarily **local**, exercised at the moment of choice, but the global impact of that
choice is confined to the paths and outcomes predicted by the designer. Modern
narrative games such as those developed by Telltale Games (e.g. _The Walking Dead:_
_Season One_, 2012, acclaimed for the emotional impact of its choices) or by Quantic
Dream (e.g. _Detroit: Become Human_, 2018, with its complex network of ramifications
and multiple endings) have taken this model to remarkable levels of sophistication,
often using "delayed consequences" mechanics where choices made in one moment
influence subsequent events. However, the main challenge of this approach remains
the **combinatorial explosion** : the number of branches and the amount of unique
content to be created (dialogue, animations, scenes) can grow exponentially with
each significant choice point, making development extremely expensive and
complex. To manage this complexity, designers often resort to "diamond" or "spindle"
structures (cf. **Chris Crawford**, on the "story net"), where narrative branches, after
splitting, tend to converge again towards common nodes of the main plot, thus limiting
long-term divergence and the amount of unique content needed. This, however, can
sometimes lead to the perception of an " **illusion of choice** " on the part of the player,
i.e. the feeling that their decisions, while seeming significant at the moment, actually
have a limited impact on the final outcome or on the pivotal points of the story (cf.
**Salen & Zimmerman, 2004**, on the concept of "meaningful play" and the importance
of player actions having discernible and integrated consequences). **Janet Murray**
**(1997),** in "Hamlet on the Holodeck", analyzes the potentials and limits of branching
structures, seeing them as a primary but not unique form of interactive storytelling.


100


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


An alternative or complementary approach is **state-based narrative**, often referred
to as the creation of a "responsive world." In this model, narrative progression is not
so much determined by explicit choices at predefined branching points, but by the
player's actions that change the **state of the game world** (a set of variables, Boolean
flags, counters, states of relationship with NPCs, etc.), and the world, including its
inhabitants, reacts dynamically to those changes. The player's actions – such as
completing a specific quest, defeating a key enemy, acquiring an important item,
choosing a certain dialogue option, or even simply visiting a certain area – can
change these status variables, unlocking new dialogue, events, quests, or altering
NPCs' behavior and attitudes towards the player. This allows for a more fluid, organic,
and less rigidly structured narrative than pure ramifications, offering a sense of
**systemic agency**, where player impact manifests itself through interaction with game
systems rather than just isolated dialogue choices. Many open-world RPGs, such as
the _Fallout_ series (Bethesda Game Studios) or _The Elder Scrolls_, extensively use
state-based systems to manage the progression of the countless quests and the
reactions of different factions and characters to the player's actions. For example, in
_Fallout: New Vegas_ (Obsidian Entertainment, 2010), the player's reputation with the
various factions of the Mojave Wasteland is a crucial status variable that dynamically
changes based on their actions, opening or closing entire story lines and affecting
the final outcome of the game. The main challenge of this approach lies in the
complexity of managing a large number of interdependent states and the need to
carefully design all possible combinations and their narrative consequences to avoid
inconsistencies, logical loops or "breaks" of history. Technologies such as
sophisticated quest engines, visual scripting systems, and state databases are critical
to effectively implementing state-based storytelling.


A further step towards greater player dynamism and agency is represented by
emergent **narrative.** As conceptualized by **Henry Jenkins (2004)** and discussed in
the context of complex systems (see Chapter 3.6), emergent narrative refers to
stories that are not primarily prescribed or predetermined by the designer, but that
are **generated spontaneously** by the complex and often unpredictable interactions
between game systems, the player's free actions, and, in multiplayer contexts,
interactions between multiple players. In this paradigm, the designer does not create
a specific story to tell, but rather a "story generator" or a "narrative possibility space".
The player's agency is maximum, as he becomes the main author (or co-author) of
his own unique and personal stories. Replayability is inherently high, as each game
session can produce a radically different narrative. Classic examples of games that
excel at generating emergent narratives include sandbox games like _Minecraft_,
where adventures and "stories" arise from the challenges of survival, constructive
creativity, and random encounters; life simulations like _The Sims_ (Maxis), where
Sims' lives, relationships, and dramas emerge from their simple behavioral rules and
the player's choices (or omissions); and games strategies with a high degree of
systemic simulation such as _Crusader Kings III_ (Paradox Development Studio, 2020),
where complex interactions between characters, dynasties, religions and politics
generate unique and often hilarious family sagas and political intrigue; or survival
games such as _RimWorld_ (Ludeon Studios, 2018), where an "AI Storyteller"
orchestrates random events and challenges that combine with the player's decisions
to create stories of unique and often tragic colonies. Many of the most memorable
experiences in online multiplayer games, such as the great battles, betrayals, or
alliances in _EVE Online_, are examples of emergent narratives created entirely by
players. The main challenge of designing for narrative emergence lies in the difficulty
for the designer to ensure a specific quality or narrative coherence, to convey specific
themes or messages directly, and to prevent excessive freedom from leading to


101


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


chaos or lack of meaning. **Marie-Laure Ryan (2006),** in "Avatars of Story", discusses
how interactivity can lead to a "narrative as performance" or a "narrative as
exploration of a database", concepts that are closely linked to the idea of emergence.


A particularly active and promising area of research and development is that of **AI-**
**Driven Interactive Narrative** . The goal is to use AI systems, often referred to as " **AI**
**Storytellers** " or " **Drama Managers**," to create narrative experiences that are both
dynamic, personalized, consistent, and capable of maintaining a dramatic arc or
desired emotional impact. These systems typically monitor the player's actions, the
state of the game world, and, ideally, a model of the player (their preferences, their
emotional state), and actively intervene to guide the narrative. This can be done by
introducing specific events, changing NPC behavior, adjusting the pace of the story,
or selecting and presenting snippets of narrative content in order to create a more
immersive experience or conform to a predefined dramatic structure (e.g.,
maintaining tension, creating emotional arcs, ensuring significant turning points
occur). Several approaches have been explored: **planning-based** systems, where
AI uses planning algorithms (such as those used in robotics or logistics) to find
sequences of actions and events that lead to certain narrative goals (cf. **Young &**
**Riedl, 2004**, on the IPOCL system; **Porteous, Cavazza, & Charles, 2010** ); **agent-**
**based systems**, where the narrative emerges from the interactions of autonomous
non-player characters, endowed with their own goals, beliefs, desires and emotionalbehavioral models (cf. **Cavazza, Charles, & Mead, 2002** ; **Mateas & Stern, 2005**, on
their pioneering work on _Façade_, an "interactive drama" featuring AI-driven NPCs
capable of natural language conversation and complex emotional behaviors); and,
more recently, machine learning-based approaches, which aim to make AI learn
narrative models from large existing story corpora or recordings of game sessions,
and then generate or adapt new narratives dynamically (cf. **Guzdial & Riedl, 2018** ).
The challenges in this field are enormous: creating narrative AIs that are truly flexible,
creative, capable of maintaining narrative coherence over long durations, balancing
authorial control with player agency (a central tension, as discussed by Mateas and
Stern), and producing stories that are not only logically valid but also emotionally
resonant and humanly meaningful, it is still a largely research objective. However,
simpler "drama management" systems, such as the **AI Director** in _Left 4 Dead_ and
its sequel, which dynamically modulates pacing, the appearance of special enemies
and the distribution of resources to create an experience of tension and challenge
constantly calibrated to the performance of the team of players, already demonstrate
the potential of an AI that orchestrates the game experience in real time, although
more focused on gameplay than explicit plot.


In addition to these main structural models, there are other techniques and
approaches that enrich the landscape of interactive fiction. Spatial **narrative** and
**environmental storytelling**, which will be discussed in detail in Section 6.5, are
based on storytelling through space design, object layout, and environmental clues,
leaving it up to the player to explore and reconstruct the story. The " **string of pearls"**
model, often used in open-world games with a main storyline, combines more linear
and strongly authorial narrative segments (the "pearls", which represent the key
moments of the story) with periods of more open, non-linear gameplay (the "thread"
that connects them), during which the player has more freedom to explore, complete
side tasks or pursue their goals. This offers a compromise between authorial control
over the progression of the main story and the player's agency in determining their
own pace and intermediate activities. Games like _The Witcher 3_ or _Red Dead_
_Redemption 2_ make effective use of this structure. Finally, approaches such as
**modular narrative** or " **quality-based narrative** " (proposed by interactive fiction


102


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


designers such as **Emily Short** ) are based on the creation of numerous small
narrative modules or self-consistent scenes, each with its own triggers and
conditions, which can be dynamically activated and combined by the system in
different ways based on the player's actions and a set of rules or heuristics that
evaluate the "quality" or narrative appropriateness of a possible sequence of events.


The choice of the most suitable interactive narrative model or combination of models
depends crucially on the specific objectives of the game, the genre to which it
belongs, the development resources available (complex branching narratives or
advanced AI systems are very expensive to produce), the target platform and, above
all, the type of experience and the degree of agency you intend to offer the player. A
scientific approach to interactive narrative design involves a clear understanding of
the affordances, constraints, and psychological implications of each model;
prototyping and iterative testing of narrative systems with players to assess their
impact on engagement, immersion, perceived coherence and sense of agency; and
a constant search for innovation in structures and technologies to push the
boundaries of interactive storytelling. Research in this field is inherently
multidisciplinary, drawing from computer science (AI, expert systems, natural
language generation), HCI (interaction design, agency), narratology (narrative
structures, character theory), and cognitive psychology (text comprehension,
memory, decision-making).


**6.3. Scientific and Artistic Principles of Advanced World-**
**Building: Internal Coherence and Verisimilitude, Deep Lore**
**Creation,** **Development** **of** **Complex** **Fictional** **Cultures**
**(Languages, Religions, Social Systems) and Environmental**
**Immersion (Wolf, Tolkien as a Case Study)**


**World-building** is the meticulous and often complex process of creating a cohesive,
detailed, and internally logical fictional universe that serves as a setting, context, and,
