in many cases, a character within a narrative or playful work. A well-built world can
dramatically increase the player's **immersion** (cf. **Brown & Cairns, 2004** ; **Jennett**
**et al., 2008**, on immersion as a multidimensional construct), provide fertile ground for
the development of meaningful stories and believable characters, and offer a
meaningful context for game mechanics and player actions. **Mark J.P. Wolf (2012),**
in his encyclopedic text "Building Imaginary Worlds: The Theory and History of
Subcreation", analyzes in depth this process of "undercreation" (a term coined by
**J.R.R. Tolkien** ), highlighting its history and techniques. A scientific and artistic
approach to advanced world-building involves the rigorous application of principles
of internal coherence, verisimilitude, the creation of a deep story, the development of
complex fictional cultures (with their languages, religions, social and political
systems) and the design of an environmental immersion that stimulates the player's
curiosity and suspension of disbelief.


A fundamental pillar of effective world-building is internal **consistency** . This principle
states that the fundamental rules, laws (physical, magical, technological, social),
history, geography, biology and culture of the fictional world must be consistent with
each other and remain relatively stable over time, unless specific and motivated
changes are an integral part of the narrative or evolution of the world itself. Internal
contradictions, logical inconsistencies, or inexplicable violations of previously
established rules of the world can severely undermine the credibility of the fictional


103


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


universe and break the player's immersion, causing them to step out of the "magic
circle." To ensure consistency, designers can draw on principles of **formal logic**
(ensuring that the "laws of the world" do not contradict themselves), **causality** (events
should have comprehensible causes within the logic of the world, even if this logic is
fantastic), and **systematicity** (Elements of the world, such as ecosystems, political
systems, technologies, or magical systems, should interact in predictable, albeit
complex and emergent, ways.) For example, if in a fantasy world such as that of _The_
_Elder Scrolls V: Skyrim_ it is established that a certain school of magic has specific
limitations or requires certain components, the sudden introduction of a character or
event that blatantly violates these rules without an adequate narrative explanation
(e.g., a divine power, an ancient artifact) can compromise the coherence of the
magical system and, consequently, of the world. Creating a "world bible" or a detailed
internal wiki is a common and scientifically sound practice for documenting all the
rules and details of the world, ensuring consistency between different members of a
development team and over the course of long-term projects.


Closely linked to coherence is **verisimilitude (believability or plausibility).**
Importantly, verisimilitude does not necessarily imply photographic realism or
adherence to the laws of our world. A world populated by dragons, elven magic, and
steam technologies can be perfectly plausible if its components, however fantastic,
are presented in an internally coherent way and if the player is induced to accept
them within the premises established by the world itself (Coleridge's "voluntary
suspension of disbelief"). Verisimilitude is based on the ability of the fictional world to
be perceived as plausible _within its own rules and internal logic_ . Principles derived
from **the psychology of perception and cognition** are fundamental here: the world
must be presented in such a way that the player can form a coherent and functioning
mental model. Vivid sensory details, character motivations that appear
psychologically grounded (even when applied to non-human species), and
environmental reactions that follow an internal (albeit fictitious) logic increase
credibility. The use of **analogies and familiarity** can help: even the most alien or
fantastical worlds often draw, consciously or unconsciously, from elements,
archetypes or structures familiar to the player (borrowed from mythology, real history,
science or other works of fiction) to make their concepts more accessible and initially
more believable, before introducing elements of greater originality. The narrative
technique of " **show, don't tell** " is particularly effective in building verisimilitude:
demonstrating the rules, customs and characteristics of the world through the
environment itself, the actions of the characters and the gameplay, rather than
explaining them only through long and boring text expositions or unnatural dialogues,
increases immersion and allows the player to "discover" the world in a more organic
way. For example, the city of Rapture in _BioShock_ (2K Boston/2K Australia, 2007)
communicates its failed objectivist philosophy and decadence more through its
crumbling Art Deco architecture, graffiti, scattered audio recordings, and the
desperate behavior of its Splicer inhabitants, than through direct explanations.


The **creation of a deep story ("deep lore" or backstory)** is another element that
contributes significantly to the richness and immersiveness of a fictional world. This
involves the development of a previous history for the world, which may include
creation myths, historical eras, major past events (wars, cataclysms, technological or
magical discoveries), the rise and fall of civilizations, and biographies of important
historical figures. While not all of this "lore" is explicitly presented to the player during
the main gameplay, its existence (even if it's just in the designer's notes or in a world
bible) can inform the design of the current world, its characters, its conflicts, and its
mysteries, creating a sense of depth and lived history. The player can perceive that


104


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


the world has its own existence that precedes and transcends his individual
experience. **J.R.R. Tolkien** 's universe, with Middle-earth, is the quintessential worldbuilding case study based on deep history and meticulously invented languages. In
video games, series such as _The Elder Scrolls_, _Mass Effect_, _Dragon Age_ (BioWare)
or _Warhammer 40,000_ (Games Workshop, with its numerous video game
adaptations) are known for the vastness and complexity of their lore, which the most
passionate players love to explore through in-game texts, dialogues or external
resources. **Ernest Hemingway** 's "iceberg theory", applied to world-building,
suggests that only a small part of the world's story and details should be explicitly
visible to the player (the tip of the iceberg), while most remain submerged, implied or
suggested, creating a sense of depth, mystery and inviting interpretation and
speculation by the community.


The **development of complex fictional cultures** is another crucial aspect of
advanced world-building. This goes beyond simply defining different "races" or
"factions" with superficial traits, and involves creating coherent systems of values,
beliefs, mythologies, religions, rituals, social and political structures, economic
systems, laws, everyday customs, art forms, architecture, and even languages or
dialects specific to the different groups that populate the game world. For example,
in _Mass Effect_, the different alien species (Turians, Asari, Salarians, Krogans,
Quarians, etc.) are characterized not only by a distinctive physical appearance, but
also by complex and well-defined interspecies histories, philosophies, governmental
structures, and tensions, which affect their interactions with Commander Shepard
and each other. The creation of a fictional language, such as Dothraki or High
Valyrian for the "Game of Thrones" series (adapted from the novels of George R.R.
Martin), or Tolkien's elven languages, represents an extreme level of detail but one
that can greatly increase credibility and immersion. While game designers rarely
create complete languages, using hints of dialects, specific idioms, unique writing
systems, or consistent nomenclature for place and person names can significantly
enrich the cultural texture of a world.


**Ambient immersion** is the desired result of effective world-building, i.e. the player's
feeling of being "present" and absorbed in the game world (see Chapter 2.1 on
presence in VR, but the concept is broader). This is facilitated not only by the
coherence and depth of the world, but also by its sensory presentation: detailed and
atmospheric graphics, a rich and spatialized sound design, and a user interface that
is as diegetic as possible or integrated into the environment can help reduce the
distance between the player and the virtual world. Attention to small environmental
details, the responsiveness of the world to the player's actions (even those not
directly related to the main objectives), and the creation of a unique "sense of place"
(Tuan, 1977) for each area of the game are fundamental to immersion.


Finally, world-building must always consider the player' **s role and perspective**
within the world. Is the player a native of that world, with pre-existing knowledge and
connections, or a foreigner who discovers it for the first time (an "outsider" or a
"chosen one")? Does he have previous knowledge about the world that the designer
can exploit or subvert, or does he learn it entirely through the game experience? Do
his actions have the potential to significantly influence the history, politics, or physical
state of the world, or is he rather an observer or minor participant in events? These
choices profoundly affect how the world is presented and how the player relates to it.


A scientific and artistic approach to world-building, therefore, is not a simple exercise


105


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


in unbridled imagination, but a disciplined process of systemic design, requiring
research (historical, cultural, scientific, depending on the theme), meticulous attention
to internal coherence, understanding of the psychology of perception and immersion,
and constant iteration based on player feedback. The use of "world bibles", detailed
maps, historical timelines, and even the consultation of experts (linguists, historians,
anthropologists, depending on the desired depth) can be valuable tools in this
process. The goal is to create a fictional universe that is not only a stage for
gameplay, but a living, breathing and memorable entity, capable of capturing the
player's imagination and sustaining it for the entire duration of the gaming experience
and, ideally, even beyond.


**6.4. Psychologically Grounded Character Design: Creating**
**Memorable Player Characters (PCs) and Non-Players (NPCs),**
**Credible** **Motivations,** **Complex** **Story** **Arcs,** **Dynamic**
**Relationships, and Advanced Behavioral AI for Emotional and**
**Social Expression**


The characters, whether they are the player-controlled avatar (PC or Player
Character) or the autonomous entities that populate the game world (NPCs or NonPlayer Characters), are often the main vehicle through which storytelling is
experienced, interaction with game systems and emotional bonds are formed.
Designing characters that are memorable, psychologically believable, have
understandable motivations and are able to evolve over time (story arcs) is a crucial
aspect of narrative game design and world-building. A scientifically informed
approach to character design draws on principles of personality psychology, social
psychology, theories of emotion, and narratology, integrating them with the specific
needs of gameplay and the potential offered by behavioral artificial intelligence.


The design of the **Player Character (PC),** or avatar, is particularly delicate because
it represents the direct extension of the player within the game world, his primary
point of interaction and, often, a vehicle for identification or projection. Key aspects
to consider include the **agency** the PC offers the player (the ability to take meaningful
actions and influence the world) and the degree of **identification** or **distancing** you
want to create. Some games, such as _The Elder Scrolls_ series or _Cyberpunk 2077_,
offer extensive aesthetic and background customization of the PC (often a "silent
protagonist" or with dialogue chosen by the player), allowing the player to project
themselves or a desired role. Other games, such as _The Witcher 3_ or _God of War_
_Ragnarök_, feature PCs with strongly predefined personalities, stories, and
motivations (Geralt of Rivia, Kratos), and the player experience focuses on playing
that role and exploring its emotional and moral nuances. In both cases, the
**motivations of the PC** within the story (e.g. saving the world, seeking revenge,
finding one's own identity) must be clear and, ideally, aligned or at least compatible
with the gameplay objectives offered to the player, to avoid ludonarrative dissonance
(cf. **Hocking, 2007** ). The **character arc**, or how the PC changes and evolves (or
resists change) over the course of the story in response to events, challenges, and
choices made (by the player through the PC), is crucial to a satisfying narrative. A
static PC can be less engaging than one who faces internal conflicts, learns from his
mistakes and matures. The **skills and mechanics** available to the PC (his "moveset",
his magical abilities, his social skills) are not only gameplay tools, but must also be
consistent with his narrative identity and background. For example, Faith Connors'
parkour abilities in _Mirror's Edge_ are intrinsically tied to her identity as a "runner" and
the dystopian world she moves through.


106


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


**Non-Player Characters (NPCs or NPCs)** populate the game world and perform a
myriad of crucial functions: they can be allies who support the PC (teammates in
_Mass Effect Legendary Edition_, each with their own personality, story and abilities),
antagonists who present challenges and conflicts (the iconic GLaDOS in _Portal 2_,
with its sarcastic and manipulative artificial intelligence), quest dispensers that
provide objectives and narrative context, sources of information and "lore", simple
extras that contribute to the atmosphere and credibility of the world ("flavor
characters"), or even obstacles or puzzles themselves. NPC **behavioral credibility**
is critical to the suspension of disbelief. They should act in ways that are consistent
with their stated or implied motivations, personality, social role within the game world,
and situational context. Behavioral **Artificial Intelligence (AI)** (see Chapter 9.4)
plays a key role in this, going beyond simple patrolling or attacking routines to include,
ideally, the ability to perceive the environment, make decisions based on goals and
internal states, interact with other NPCs, and react flexibly and non-repetitively to the
player's actions. **Nico Frijda (2007),** in his work "The Laws of Emotion", discusses
how emotions are intrinsically linked to "action readiness", a concept that can inform
the design of believable and functional emotional behaviors for NPCs. For example,
a frightened NPC might try to flee, hide, or plead, while an angry NPC might become
aggressive or insult.


Secondary NPCs, not just the main characters in the plot, can also benefit from a
certain **psychological depth** to make the world more alive and interesting. **Keith**
**Oatley (1999, 2011),** cognitive psychologist and novelist, has explored how fiction
(and by extension narrative games) can function as a kind of "simulation of social
life", allowing us to enter the minds of others, understand their emotions and explore
complex interpersonal dynamics. Creating NPCs with personal goals, fears, hopes,
meaningful relationships with other characters, and a previous story (even if not all
explicitly revealed to the player) makes them more three-dimensional and
memorable. The player could discover these details through optional dialogue,
observing their daily routines, or finding letters and diaries. Games like _Shenmue_
(Sega AM2, 1999) were pioneers in trying to create NPCs with complex daily routines
and seemingly independent lives.


Each NPC should have a clear **narrative and/or play function** within the overall
design. An NPC that serves no purpose risks being perceived as "noise" or filler.
Dialogue is a primary tool for revealing NPCs' personality, motivations, knowledge,
and emotional state. It should be written naturally (avoiding overexposure or purely
functional dialogue), consistent with the character and their cultural background, and,
ideally, should offer the player meaningful interaction options that can affect the
relationship or unlock new information. The use of professional voice actors and
expressive facial animations (such as those achieved with performance capture in
games like _The Last of Us Part II_ or _Hellblade: Senua's Sacrifice_ ) can greatly increase
the credibility and emotional impact of NPCs. The **relationship between the PC and**
**NPCs** is a particularly important aspect, especially in role-playing games. How NPCs
react to the PC and how these relationships can dynamically evolve based on the
player's actions, dialogue choices, or reputation is a powerful driver of engagement
and personalization of the narrative experience. Systems of "approval" or "affinity"
with companions, such as those present in many BioWare games (e.g. _Dragon Age:_
_Origins_, 2009) or in _Persona 5_, where cultivating relationships unlocks gameplay
benefits and narrative insights, are examples of designs that focus on interpersonal
dynamics.


In character design, designers can draw on **narrative archetypes** (e.g., the hero, the


107


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


mentor, the shadow, the trickster, as described by **Carl Jung (1968)** or popularized
in the context of screenwriting by **Joseph Campbell (1949)** in "The Hero with a
Thousand Faces" and Christopher **Vogler (1998)** in "The Writer's Journey").
Archetypes can provide a solid and instantly recognizable foundation for creating
characters with universal resonance. However, it is crucial to avoid falling into flat,
predictable, or worse, offensive stereotypes. A mature and scientifically conscious
use of archetypes uses them as a starting point from which to develop more complex,
nuanced, contradictory and unique characters, which can surprise the player and
challenge his expectations. The ethical and diverse representation of characters is a
crucial responsibility of the designer (see Section 1.6).


Creating **effective antagonists** is often crucial to the quality of an engaging story. A
memorable antagonist is not simply "evil," but has **understandable motives**, even if
twisted or morally reprehensible. His actions should stem from internal logic, past
trauma, a distorted worldview, or goals that, from his point of view, are justified. An
antagonist who is purely "evil because yes" is rarely interesting or fearsome. It must
also represent a **significant challenge** for the protagonist, both in terms of gameplay
(a difficult boss, a cunning opponent) and narrative (questioning the values or abilities
of the PC). Finally, the most complex antagonists can have an **intricate relationship**
**with the protagonist**, perhaps reflecting dark aspects, sharing a common past, or
representing an ideological alternative that forces the PC (and the player) to confront
difficult choices. Antagonists such as Handsome Jack from _Borderlands 2_ (Gearbox
Software, 2012), with his narcissistic charisma and pseudo-heroic motivations, or
Vaas Montenegro from _Far Cry 3_ (Ubisoft Montreal, 2012), with his unpredictable
madness, are examples of antagonists who have left a deep mark on players.


**Methodologies for character design** include the creation of **character sheets**
**(character sheets or character bios),** detailed documents that describe physical
appearance, backstory, personality (perhaps using psychological models such as the
Big Five), fundamental motivations, internal and external conflicts, key relationships
with other characters, and the expected development span for the character over the
course of narration. For AI-driven NPCs, **behavioral modeling** is essential, defining
their daily routines, their reactions to specific environmental events or stimuli, their
decision trees, and, in more advanced systems, their emotional patterns and longterm goals. **Character-focused playtesting** is crucial for assessing how players
perceive PCs and NPCs, whether they find them believable, empathetic, interesting,
or irritating, whether dialogue and interactions are working as intended, and whether
dynamic relationships develop organically and meaningfully.


In conclusion, psychologically grounded character design is an art that benefits
enormously from a solid understanding of human psychology (personality,
motivation, emotions, social cognition), classical and contemporary narrative
theories, and careful integration with game mechanics and AI capabilities. Wellcrafted characters, whether they are avatars of the player or autonomous entities of
the game world, can transform a good game into an unforgettable experience,
capable of eliciting deep emotional responses and remaining etched in the memory
of the players long after the game is over.


**6.5. Advanced Techniques of Environmental Storytelling,**
**Implicit Narrative and "Lore Delivery": Construction of Meaning**
**through Active Exploration, Interpretation of Clues and Player**


108


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


**Participation in Narrative Co-Construction**


In addition to explicit storytelling conveyed through dialogues, cutscenes or diegetic
texts, video games have a powerful tool for telling stories and building worlds in a
more subtle, integrated and often more immersive way: **environmental storytelling**
**and** **implicit** narrative. These techniques are based on the player's ability to observe,
explore, infer and interpret clues scattered throughout the game environment, thus
becoming an active participant in the co-construction of meaning and story. A
scientific approach to these forms of storytelling requires an understanding of the
psychology of perception, spatial cognition, semiotics and theories of interpretation,
as well as careful design of spaces and objects.


Environmental **storytelling**, as discussed by **Henry Jenkins (2004)** and many level
designers (e.g. **Matthias Worch**, known for his work on games such as _Unreal_ and
_Dead Space_ ), is the art of telling stories through the design and arrangement of
elements within the play space. Instead of "telling" the player what happened or how
the world is made, you "show" it through the environment itself. A messy room with
overturned furniture, dried blood stains on the walls, and an open journal on a desk
in a horror game like _Amnesia: The Dark Descent_ tells a story of struggle, despair,
and mystery in a way that is far more powerful and immediate than a lengthy
expository text. Key principles of effective environmental storytelling include
consistency of clues with the overall story and world-building, attention to
**meaningful detail** that can add depth and nuance, the ability to create temporal
**layering** (the environment can reveal traces of past events that have overlapped over
time, such as the ruins of an ancient civilization discovered beneath a modern city in
_Horizon Zero Dawn_, Guerrilla Games, 2017), and the ability **to evoke questions** in
the player's mind, encouraging them to investigate, formulate hypotheses and
actively fill in narrative "gaps". **Visual composition**, **atmospheric lighting**,
**evocative sound design**, and **digital mise-en-scène** (the deliberate arrangement
of objects, characters, and lights within a scene to communicate meaning, a concept
borrowed from film and theater) are crucial tools for directing the player's attention
toward the important narrative elements of the environment and for creating the
emotional tone desired. For example, the city of Rapture in _BioShock_ is a masterpiece
of environmental storytelling: its ruined Art Deco architecture, propaganda posters,
disturbing audio recordings (audiologs) left by its inhabitants, and the macabre layout
of certain environments tell the story of its utopian collapse far more effectively than
any direct narrative.


**Implicit narrative** refers more generally to all those narrative elements that are not
openly stated or explained explicitly, but that the player must actively infer, interpret
or reconstruct based on clues, suggestions, symbolism or recurring patterns. This
approach is often based on Hemingway's "iceberg theory", where only a small part
of history and the world is visible on the surface, while most remains submerged, to
be discovered through careful exploration and reflection. Games such as the _Dark_
_Souls_ series (FromSoftware) are known for their implicit and fragmented narrative,
conveyed mainly through item descriptions, enemy and boss design, level
architecture, and rare cryptic dialogue. This "narrative archaeology" requires a
particularly active and motivated player, who is willing to investigate, connect the dots
and participate in discussions with the community to decipher the complex "lore" of
the game. Although this approach may be difficult for some, for others it is a source
of deep engagement and satisfaction, transforming the game into a real
hermeneutical puzzle. **Frictional Games**, with titles such as _SOMA_ (2015) in addition


109


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


to the aforementioned _Amnesia_, is another studio known for its masterful use of
implicit narrative and environmental storytelling to create oppressive atmospheres
and stories that raise complex philosophical questions.


The **specific techniques** for implementing environmental storytelling and implicit
narrative are numerous and varied. The arrangement of **remains and traces** (objects
left behind by absent characters, corpses in meaningful poses, ruins that tell of
ancient battles, graffiti that express dissent or hope, footprints that suggest a path)
can suggest past events or the presence of other beings. **Embedded texts**, such as
personal journals, letters, emails, audio or video recordings, fictional newspaper
articles, technical manuals, or fragments of books found in the game world, can
provide fragments of story, lore, or characterization of characters, often from a
subjective and potentially unreliable perspective, which the player must critically
evaluate. Games such as _Gone Home_ (The Fullbright Company, 2013) or _What_
_Remains of Edith Finch_ (Giant Sparrow, 2017) base their narrative almost entirely on
the exploration of a domestic environment and the discovery of these narrative
objects. **Narrative architecture**, or the way in which the design of buildings,
settlements, and spaces themselves reflects the culture, history, function, or
psychological state of its inhabitants, is another powerful tool. An imposing, wellmaintained temple suggests a thriving and powerful religion, while a dilapidated and
oppressive prison communicates despair and brutality. Visual **symbolism**, through
the use of colors, shapes, recurring images, or objects with a specific cultural or
thematic meaning within the game, can add layers of meaning and invite
interpretation. Finally, **dynamic environmental changes**, i.e. the way the game
world transforms in response to the player's actions or the progress of the main
storyline, can visually reflect the consequences of choices and reinforce the player's
sense of agency over the story. For example, seeing a previously hostile area
become safer and more prosperous after completing a series of quests to clear it, as
in some RPGs, provides powerful narrative and spatial feedback.


The **player's role** in environmental storytelling and implicit narrative is inherently
active and **co-constructive** . The designer provides the clues, disseminates the
fragments of information, and creates the space for interpretive possibilities, but it is
the player who, through careful exploration, observation, inference, hypothesis, and
the connection of seemingly disparate elements, builds his or her own understanding
of history and the world. This process of "sense-making" can be extremely rewarding
and profoundly increase the sense of immersion, discovery and "ownership" of the
story. As discussed by **Umberto Eco (1979)** in his concept of "Open Work", texts
(and by extension games) that leave room for interpretation and active participation
of the user can generate a deeper and more lasting engagement. However, this
approach also presents challenges: there is a risk that some players will miss crucial
clues, interpret them in ways that are radically different from what the designer
intended (although some ambiguity may be desirable), or become frustrated by the
lack of an explicit, linear narrative. Therefore, good implicit narrative design often
requires a careful balance between providing enough clues to guide interpretation
and leaving enough room for the player to discover and construct meaning.


The **scientific evaluation** of the effectiveness of environmental storytelling and
implicit narrative is complex and relies predominantly on **qualitative methods** (see
Chapter 8.2). **Directly observing player behavior** during playtesting (noticing
whether players dwell on certain environmental details, actively exploring optional
areas, looking confused, or verbally making assumptions about the story) can provide
valuable insights. **Post-session interviews** or **think-aloud** protocols can be used


110


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


to ask players what they think happened in a certain area, what story the environment
is telling, or how they interpret specific clues or symbols. Analyzing **online**
**discussion forums**, subreddits dedicated to a game, or "lore explanation" videos
created by the gaming community (such as the overwhelming ones for the _Souls_
series or _Bloodborne_ ) can reveal how the community collectively interprets and
discusses the implicit narrative elements, and whether particularly popular or
controversial theories or readings emerge.


A scientific and artistic approach to environmental storytelling and implicit narrative
therefore involves an **intentional and layered design of narrative cues** within the
playful space, a deep consideration of how these cues will be perceived, cognitively
processed, and interpreted by players (drawing on the psychology of perception,
semiotics, and theories of reception), and **iterative** testing to ensure that the desired
message (or the desired space for interpretation and co-construction) is
communicated effectively and that the discovery experience is rewarding. Games
such as the aforementioned _BioShock_, _Gone Home_, _What Remains of Edith Finch_,
the _Dark Souls_ series, or even more atmosphere-focused titles such as _Inside_
(Playdead, 2016) or _Little Nightmares_ (Tarsier Studios, 2017), are masterful
examples of how environmental storytelling and implicit narrative can be used to
create deep, evocative and memorable ones, often with minimal use of direct
exposure, relying instead on the player's intelligence and curiosity.


**6.6. Conclusions: Narrative and the World as Interacting**
**Systems for a Transformative Gaming Experience**


This chapter navigated the complex and fascinating waters of interactive narrative
and world-building in the context of game design, highlighting how an approach that
integrates artistic sensitivity, scientific rigor and psychological understanding is
fundamental to creating gaming experiences that not only entertain, but can also
deeply engage, stimulate reflection and even transform the player's perception.


We started by reconsidering the historical debate between narratology and ludology,
recognizing that an integrated vision, which values both the specificity of gameplay
and the power of storytelling, is the most productive for contemporary game design.
The exploration of the different models of interactive narrative, from classic branching
structures to emerging dynamic systems and the frontiers of narrative AI, highlighted
the wide range of tools available to the designer to orchestrate the player's agency
and the progression of the story, each with its own trade-offs between authorial
control and user freedom.


Next, we delved into the scientific and artistic principles of advanced world-building,
emphasizing the importance of internal coherence, verisimilitude (even in fantastical
contexts), the creation of a deep story, and the development of complex fictional
cultures to construct universes that are not only believable, but also capable of
sustaining deep and lasting immersion. The example of masters of subcreation such
as Tolkien reminds us of the power of worlds rich in detail and lived history.


The focus then shifted to psychologically grounded character design, recognizing
how memorable player and non-player characters, endowed with credible
motivations, capable of complex narrative arcs and dynamic relationships, are often
the beating heart of the most impactful narrative experiences. Understanding the


111


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


psychology of personality and emotions, combined with the potential of behavioral
AI, offers tools to create characters that appear alive and capable of eliciting a
genuine empathic response.


Finally, we explored advanced techniques of environmental storytelling and implicit
narrative, highlighting how the construction of meaning can occur through the player's
active exploration of the environment, the interpretation of subtle clues and his
participation in the co-construction of the story. This approach, which enhances the
player's intelligence and curiosity, can lead to particularly deep and personal forms
of narrative engagement.


In conclusion, the narrative and the game world should not be considered as simple
containers or decorations for the mechanics, but as **interacting systems** that,
together with the gameplay, contribute to creating a holistic and meaningful
experience. A scientific approach to narrative design and world-building involves
intentional design, a deep understanding of the psychological mechanisms of
immersion and interpretation, the use of research methodologies to test and validate
design choices, and a constant reflection on the potential of the video game medium
as an art form capable of telling unique stories and exploring the human condition in
new and powerful ways. The real challenge, and the great opportunity, for the
scientifically informed game designer lies in knowing how to orchestrate this complex
symphony of elements to create playful experiences that not only entertain, but can
also touch, make people think and, sometimes, even transform those who live them.

# **Chapter 7**

### **UI/UX Scientific Design for** **Games: Intuitive Interfaces** **and Optimal Experiences**


_I believe that games are an art form. I also believe that they are a form of_

_entertainment. And I believe they can be both at the same time_


_**-Warren Spector-**_


114


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


The **user interface (UI)** represents the complex set of elements through which the
player interacts with the game system, giving commands and receiving information.
**User experience (UX),** on the other hand, is a more holistic and subjective concept,
encompassing all of the player's perceptions, emotions, and responses that result
from using, or even simply anticipating use, of the game, and its interface. In the
context of game design, a well-designed UI and an overall positive UX are absolutely
critical. Not only do they ensure that players can understand the mechanics, navigate
the environments, and interact effectively with game systems, but they also contribute
significantly to immersion, flow, enjoyment, and overall satisfaction. Conversely, a
cumbersome, confusing, inefficient, or aesthetically unpleasing user interface can
erect insurmountable barriers, rendering even the most brilliant game mechanics or
compelling narratives inaccessible, and turning a potential positive experience into a
source of frustration and, ultimately, abandonment of the game. This chapter will
explore the scientific fundamentals of UI and UX design for games, drawing heavily
from the established principles of **Human Computer Interaction (HCI)** and
**cognitive psychology** . Advanced principles for creating playful interfaces that
minimize cognitive load and maximize clarity and effectiveness of feedback will be
discussed. **Player-Centered Design methodologies will be presented**, such as
the use of personas, use scenarios and iterative prototyping. The theoretical models
and metrics for the **scientific evaluation of Game UX will be explored**, and
advanced testing methodologies will be examined, including remote usability testing,
eye tracking and physiological analysis. Finally, the crucial importance of **inclusive**
**design and accessibility** of interfaces will be reiterated, to ensure that gaming
experiences are usable by as many people as possible. The goal is to provide
designers with the tools to create interfaces that are not simple graphic "overlays",
but transparent, intuitive and empowering bridges to the heart of the gaming
experience.


**7.1. Advanced Principles of HCI and Cognitive Psychology for**
**the Design of Playful Interfaces: Minimizing Cognitive Load,**
**Maximizing Clarity and Feedback**


Human-Computer Interaction (HCI) is the discipline that, as defined by the ACM
SIGCHI, deals with the design, evaluation and implementation of interactive
computer systems for human use and the study of the phenomena that surround
them. Many of its principles and methodologies, developed and validated over
decades of research, are directly and profitably applicable to game design, although
the gaming context presents unique challenges and objectives, such as the centrality
of fun, engagement and emotional experience, which go beyond the traditional
objectives of efficiency and productivity of HCI in work contexts (cf. **Dix et al., 2004**,
"Human Computer Interaction"; **Preece, Rogers, & Sharp, 2015**, "Interaction
Design: Beyond Human Computer Interaction"). A scientific approach to the design
of game interfaces (Game UI) is based on understanding how players perceive
information, process stimuli, learn control systems and manage their limited cognitive
resources.


A primary goal in Game UI design is **to minimize unnecessary cognitive load**
imposed on the player. As discussed in Chapter 2.3, John Sweller's (1988, 1994)
**Cognitive Load Theory (CLT)** posits that human working memory has limited
capacity. An interface that presents too much information at once, requires the player


115


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


to remember complex commands, or uses obscure terminology, can overload
working memory, leading to confusion, errors, frustration, and a reduced ability to
learn and enjoy the game. To minimize cognitive load, designers should adhere to
principles such as:


  - **Recognition over Recall:** Making objects, actions, and options visible or
easily accessible, rather than requiring the player to remember them from
memory (one of Nielsen's usability heuristics **, 1993** ). For example, a visual
inventory in an RPG like _Baldur's Gate 3_ is preferable to a system that
requires the player to type in the names of the items they own.

  - **Simplicity and Visual Clarity:** Use clean layouts, a clear visual hierarchy
for information (the most important information should be more salient), and
consistent use of universally understandable or well-explained icons and
symbols. The minimalist interface of games like _Journey_ or _Inside_ is an
example of how simplicity can increase immersion and reduce distractions.

  - **Logical Grouping (Chunking):** Organizing information and controls into
logical and meaningful groups, exploiting the ability of working memory to
handle "chunks" of information (cf. **Miller, 1956** ). For example, group all
your character-related options (inventory, skills, stats) into one main menu.

  - **Progressive Disclosure:** Presenting information and interface options
gradually, only when they are necessary or relevant to the player's current
context, especially for new users. This avoids overwhelming beginners with
too much complexity at first. Many complex strategy games, such as _Europa_
_Universalis IV_ (Paradox Development Studio, 2013), use detailed tooltips
and contextual tutorials to apply this principle.


Maximizing **the clarity and effectiveness of the feedback** provided by the interface
is another key pillar. As discussed in Chapter 4.4, feedback informs the player about
the consequences of their actions and the state of the system. The user interface is
one of the primary channels for conveying this feedback. Donald Norman's **(1988)**
**principles** on "gulfs" of execution and evaluation are particularly pertinent here. The
**Execution Gulf** (the discrepancy between the player's intentions and the actions
allowed by the system) can be bridged by an interface that makes clear the
**affordances** of the interactable elements (what can I do with this button, with this
object?) and that provides an intuitive **mapping** between physical controls (buttons,
sticks, touches) and actions in the game. For example, the aiming interface in a
shooter must clearly indicate where the player is aiming and which weapon is
selected. The **Rating Gulf** (the discrepancy between the state of the system and the
player's perception of it) is filled with timely, specific, and understandable feedback
that communicates the outcome of actions and relevant changes in the state of the
game. For example, when the player presses a button to activate a skill in a MOBA
like _Dota 2_, the UI should immediately indicate if the skill was successful, if it is in
cooldown (showing a timer), and what effects it has produced (damage dealt, buff
applied, etc.).


Other HCI principles crucial to Game UI include:


  - **Consistency:** Use consistent visuals, terminology, and interaction patterns
within the game (internal consistency) and, where appropriate, with platform
or genre conventions (external consistency). This reduces the learning
curve and increases predictability. For example, using the ESC key to
access the pause menu is an almost universal convention in PC gaming.


116


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


  - **Error Prevention and Recovery:** Design the interface to minimize the
possibility of costly or fatal errors (e.g., deleting a save, discarding a rare
object) through confirmation prompts, "undo" options, or making dangerous
actions less accessible. If an error occurs, the interface should provide clear
messages about what happened and, if possible, how to recover.

  - **Flexibility and Efficiency of Use:** The interface should be easy to use for
novices, but also offer shortcuts, customization options (e.g. keybinding,
HUD layout) and faster interaction methods for experienced players, so as
not to hinder their efficiency.

  - **Aesthetic and Minimalist Design:** The interface should be aesthetically
pleasing and consistent with the tone and art style of the game, but without
sacrificing clarity and functionality. Purely decorative elements or irrelevant
information can create "visual noise" and distract from gameplay. A
minimalist design, which presents only essential information at the right
time, is often preferable. The _Dead Space interface_, which integrates many
elements of the HUD diegetically on the suit of the protagonist Isaac Clarke,
is a famous example of aesthetic, immersive and functional design.


The application of these principles, derived from decades of research in HCI and
cognitive psychology, is not a magic formula, but a guide for an iterative and informed
design process. Each game presents unique interface challenges, and the optimal
solution often emerges from a deep understanding of the target player, experiential
goals, and rigorous prototyping and testing cycles. The ultimate goal is to create an
interface that is so intuitive and effective that it becomes almost "transparent",
allowing the player to fully focus on the gaming experience and interaction with the
game world, rather than fighting with menus or deciphering incomprehensible
information.


**7.2.** **Player-Centered** **Design** **for** **UI/UX:** **Methodologies**
**(Personas, Use Scenarios, User Journeys), Iterative Prototyping**
**(Low to High Fidelity) and Co-Design with Players**


**Player-Centered Design (PCD),** a playful adaptation of the more general principles
of User-Centered Design (UCD), is a philosophical approach and set of
methodologies that place the needs, wants, abilities, limitations, and context of use
of target players at the center of every single step of the user interface (UI) and user
experience (UX) design and development process Overall. As widely discussed in
the HCI literature (e.g. **Norman & Draper, 1986** ; **ISO 9241-210** ), UCD is based on a
deep empirical understanding of users and their active involvement in the design
cycle. Applied to games, PCD does not simply mean "giving players what they want"
uncritically, but rather understanding their intrinsic motivations, mental patterns,
expectations, and potential sources of frustration, to create experiences that are not
only usable and efficient, but also deeply engaging, meaningful, and enjoyable.


A PCD process for a game's UI/UX typically consists of several interconnected and
iterative phases. The first step is the **in-depth understanding of the context of use**
**and the target players** . This involves answering fundamental questions: Who are
our players? What is their age, their cultural background, their previous experience
with video games (in general and with the specific genre of our game)? What are
their cognitive and motor skills (also considering diversity and accessibility)? What
are their primary motivations for playing the game (see Chapter 2.2 on player


117


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


taxonomies and motivational profiles such as those of Quantic Foundry)? On which
platforms will they play (PC, console, mobile) and in which contexts (short and
fragmented gaming sessions on a mobile device during a commute, or long and
immersive sessions at home on a gaming PC)? To answer these questions, UX
designers and researchers can use a variety of qualitative and quantitative methods,
such as interviews with target players, surveys, analysis of competing or similar
games, and the study of demographics and behavioral data.


Based on this understanding, we proceed to create **Personas** . Personas, as
conceptualized by **Alan Cooper (1999)** in "The Inmates Are Running the Asylum,"
are fictional but archetypal characters, based on real research data, representing the
different significant segments of the target audience. Each person has a name, age,
background, goals, motivations, frustrations, and a level of familiarity with technology
and games. For example, for a complex RPG, you might define personas like "Elena,
the 35-year-old hardcore RPG player looking for strategic depth and a mature story"
or "Marco, the casual gamer, 22, new to RPGs, looking for an accessible experience
and the ability to play with friends." Personas help the development team maintain
an empathetic focus on the needs of different types of players throughout the design
process, avoiding designing based solely on their personal preferences ("selfreferential design").


Next, the **Use Cases or Usage Scenarios** and the **User Journeys (or Player**
**Journeys)** are defined. Use scenarios describe specific tasks or situations in which
a player (represented by a person) interacts with the game's interface to achieve a
goal. For example, one scenario might be, "Elena wants to compare two pieces of
equipment in her inventory to decide which one to equip." User journeys map the
entire player experience through different stages of the game (e.g., onboarding, first
combat, inventory management, social interaction, long-term progression),
identifying points of contact with the interface, possible actions, thoughts, and
emotions of the player at each stage. These tools help identify functional and
experiential interface requirements and predict potential usability issues or
frustration.


The next stage is solution **design**, which is inherently **iterative** and relies on rapid
**prototyping** and **evaluation** cycles. Prototyping allows design ideas to be brought
to life in tangible forms that can be tested with players, even very early in the
development process. It often starts with **low-fidelity prototypes**, such as paper
prototypes, digital wireframes created with tools such as Balsamiq or Figma, or
simple storyboards that illustrate the flow of interaction across different screens.
These prototypes are quick and inexpensive to create and modify, and are ideal for
testing basic concepts, information layouts and navigation flows, collecting qualitative
feedback at the earliest stages. For example, you could test on paper the flow of
creating a character or navigating the menus of an in-game store. Next, we move on
to **high-fidelity prototypes**, which are digital, interactive, and closer to the look and
feel of the final interface, created with advanced prototyping tools (such as Figma,
Adobe XD, Axure RP) or directly within the game engine (e.g., using Unreal Engine's
UMG system or Unity's UI Toolkit). These prototypes allow you to test more detailed
aspects of the interaction, visual aesthetics, animations, and the overall feel of the
interface. **Houde and Hill (1997)** proposed a useful model for thinking about the
different roles of prototypes, distinguishing between those focused on "role" (how the
product fits into the user's life), "implementation" (how it works technically), and "look
and feel" (the sensory and interactive experience). In game design, prototyping is
crucial not only for the UI, but for testing fundamental game mechanics (as discussed


118


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


in Chapter 3).


Each prototyping iteration is followed by an **evaluation** phase with real players
(UI/UX-focused playtesting, see Section 7.4 and Chapter 8). The feedback collected
during these tests (usability issues, points of confusion, suggestions, performance
metrics) is used to inform the next design iteration, in a continuous cycle of
improvement. This iterative, evidence-based approach is at the heart of PCD and
helps to reduce risk, identify problems early, and converge towards design solutions
that are truly effective and satisfying for players.


An increasingly important aspect of PCD is **Co-Design (or Participatory Design),**
which actively involves players not only as test subjects, but as real partners in the
creative process. This can be through design workshops with players, joint
brainstorming sessions, or the use of tools that allow players to create and share their
own content or interface changes. Although it requires careful management, codesign can lead to more innovative solutions that are deeply aligned with the needs
and desires of the gaming community (cf. **Sanders & Stappers, 2008**, on co-creation
and participatory design). For example, many games with strong modding support,
such as _Skyrim_ or _Minecraft_, benefit tremendously from implicit co-design by their
community, which creates alternative interfaces, usability improvements, and new
features.


In summary, Player-Centered Design for UI/UX in games is a methodological process
that starts from a deep empathic understanding of target players, uses tools such as
personas and user journeys to define requirements, relies on iterative cycles of
prototyping (from low to high fidelity) and empirical evaluation with real users, And,
ideally, it engages players as co-creators of the experience. This scientific and
systematic approach is essential to create interfaces that are not only aesthetically
pleasing, but that are above all intuitive, efficient, accessible and capable of fully
supporting the desired gaming experience, acting as a transparent and empowering
bridge between the player and the game world.


**7.3. Scientific Evaluation of Game UX: Overall Theoretical**
**Models (e.g. Abeele's LEX, Calvillo-Gámez's Core Elements of**
**the Gaming Experience), and Development of Reliable Objective**
**and Subjective Metrics**


The scientific evaluation of the User Experience in Games (Game UX) is a complex
and multidimensional process that goes far beyond the simple measurement of
usability or the absence of errors. As defined by **ISO 9241-210 (2019),** UX
encompasses "a person's perceptions and responses that result from the use or
expectation of use of a product, system, or service." In the specific context of video
games, this definition is enriched with hedonian, emotional, social and narrative
dimensions that are central to the gaming experience. **Marc Hassenzahl (2003,**
**2010),** in his UX model, distinguishes between **pragmatic qualities** (related to utility,
usability and efficiency, i.e. "doing things") and **hedonic qualities** (related to
pleasure, stimulation, identification, beauty and meaning, i.e. "feeling things"). Both
of these dimensions are crucial for a successful Game UX, and their evaluation
requires specific theoretical models and a diverse set of objective and subjective
metrics.


119


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


Several **theoretical models** have been proposed to capture the complexity of Game
UX. The **Player Experience (PLEX) framework**, developed by **Veronique Van**
**Vlasselaer (formerly Abeele) and colleagues (Abeele, Van Rooy, & Zaman,**
**2010; IJsselsteijn, de Kort, & Poels, 2013, who contributed to the Game**
**Experience Questionnaire based in part on these concepts),** identifies a series
of experiential categories that players can experience, such as challenge, curiosity,
fantasy, camaraderie, competition, discovery, self-expression, thrill, control,
submission (understood as relaxation or pastime). This framework aims to provide a
rich vocabulary to describe the nuances of the gaming experience. Another influential
model is the **Core Elements of the Gaming Experience (CEGE),** proposed by
**Calvillo Gámez, Cairns, and Cox (2010, 2015),** which identifies central elements
such as immersion, flow, competence, autonomy, relationality (in line with SDT),
presence, challenge, positive and negative emotions, and storytelling. These
theoretical models not only help structure the understanding of Game UX, but also
provide a basis for the development of measurement tools, such as questionnaires
and interview protocols. For example, the **Game Experience Questionnaire (GEQ)**
(IJsselsteijn, de Kort, & Poels, 2013) is a widely used self-report tool that measures
several components of the post-session gaming experience, including competence,
immersion, flow, tension, challenge, positive and negative affect, and return to
experience. Its modular structure allows researchers to select the scales that are
most relevant to their study objectives. Other questionnaires, such as the **Player**
**Experience of Need Satisfaction (PENS)** (Ryan, Rigby, & Przybylski, 2006), focus
specifically on the satisfaction of SDT's basic psychological needs (competence,
autonomy, relationality) during play.


The development and use of **reliable and valid metrics** is crucial for a scientific
evaluation of Game UX. These metrics can be categorized into **subjective** and
**objective** . Subjective **metrics** are based on the player's self-assessment of their
experience. Key tools include:


  - **Standardized Questionnaires:** Such as the GEQ, the PENS, the
Immersive Experience Questionnaire (IEQ) by **Jennett et al. (2008),** or the
Flow Short Scale by **Engeser and Rheinberg (2008).** These tools offer the
advantage of being psychometrically validated and allowing comparisons
between different studies, but they may not capture unique aspects of a
specific game.

  - **Custom Questionnaires:** Developed ad hoc to measure specific aspects
of the experience of a particular game, often using Likert scales, semantic
differentials or open questions. They require careful design and pilot
validation.

  - **Qualitative Interviews:** As discussed in Chapter 8.2, in-depth interviews
and focus groups allow you to explore the player's subjective experience in
a rich and detailed way, going beyond the numbers to understand the "why"
of their reactions.

  - **Think Aloud Protocols:** Asking players to verbalize their thoughts and
feelings while playing provides real-time insights into their UX.

  - **Diary Studies:** Players write down their experiences, thoughts, and
emotions over an extended period, offering a longitudinal view of UX.


Objective **metrics**, on the other hand, seek to measure aspects of the player's
experience or behavior without relying directly on their self-report. These include:


120


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


  - **Behavioral Data (Telemetry and Game Analytics):** As discussed in
Chapter 8.3, automatic tracking of players' actions within the game (game
time, completion rates, choices made, movement patterns, social
interactions, mistakes made) can provide indirect indicators of UX. For
example, a high churn rate at a certain point in the game can signal a UX
problem (frustration, boredom, confusion). How often you use certain
features or how much time you spend on certain activities can indicate
engagement or interest.

  - **Performance Measures:** In specific tasks, metrics such as time to complete
the task, number of errors, or score obtained can be used as indicators of
usability and effectiveness of the interface in supporting the player's goals.

  - **Physiological and Biometric Measurements (see also Chapters 7.4 and**
**9.5):**


1. **Eye Tracking:** To analyze where players direct their visual
attention, identify points of confusion in the interface, or measure
pupillary dilation as an indicator of cognitive load or arousal.
2. **Electrocardiogram (ECG) and Heart Rate Variability (HRV):** To
measure physiological arousal, stress, or emotional involvement.
3. **Galvanic Skin Response (EDA/GSR):** Another indicator of
emotional arousal.
4. **Facial Electromyography (fEMG):** To detect micro facial
expressions associated with positive emotions (zygomatic major
muscle) or negative emotions (corrugator eyebrow muscle).
5. **Electroencephalogram (EEG):** To study patterns of brain activity
related to attention, cognitive load, flow, or specific emotional
responses. These measures offer the potential for a more objective
assessment of the player's internal states, but their implementation
is technically complex and the interpretation of the data requires
expertise and caution, since the relationship between physiological
signals and subjective experience is not always unambiguous.


It is crucial to emphasize the importance of a **multi-method approach** and
**triangulation** in Game UX evaluation. No single metric or method can capture the
entirety of such a complex experience. Combining subjective data (e.g.
questionnaires, interviews) with objective data (e.g. telemetry, physiological
measures) allows to obtain a richer, more robust and valid understanding. For
example, if telemetry data shows that players are repeatedly failing in a certain
section, qualitative interviews can help understand _why_ they are failing (e.g., unclear
mechanics, ambiguous feedback, excessive difficulty).


In addition, UX **is always contextual** . As pointed out by **McCarthy and Wright**
**(2004)** in their work on experience as a dialogic and situated process, a player's
perception and response are influenced not only by the design of the game, but also
by its internal state (mood, fatigue, expectations), the physical environment in which
it plays (at home alone, with friends, in a noisy place), the platform used and the
broader social and cultural context. UX assessment should, as far as possible, seek
to account for these contextual variables or, in experimental studies, to control for
them. For example, testing a mobile game in a quiet lab may not reflect the real-world
experience of a user playing on the bus with many distractions.


A scientific approach to Game UX evaluation therefore involves a clear definition of


121


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


the aspects of the experience that are intended to be measured (based on solid
theoretical models), the rigorous selection and application of an appropriate set of
metrics and methods (both subjective and objective), a careful and critical analysis
of the data collected, and the use of the results to inform iterative cycles of design
and improvement. The ultimate goal is to create games that not only perform well
(usability), but also deliver deeply engaging, emotionally resonant, and personally
meaningful experiences for players. Continuous research in the field of Game UX
strives to develop increasingly refined theoretical models and increasingly valid and
reliable measurement tools to support this complex process.


**7.4. Advanced and Combined UI/UX Testing Methodologies:**
**Remote and In-Lab Usability Testing, Heuristic Assessment**
**(Nielsen, Pinelle), Cognitive Walkthrough, Advanced Eye-**
**Tracking (Scan Patterns, Dynamic Areas of Interest) and**
**Multimodal Physiological Analysis (ECG, EDA, fEMG)**


Systematic and scientific evaluation of the user interface (UI) and user experience
(UX) through rigorous testing with real users is an essential cornerstone of PlayerCentered Design and an evidence-based approach to game design. In addition to the
more general playtesting methods discussed in Chapter 8, there are specific and
advanced methodologies, often borrowed from HCI and psychological research, that
can provide deep insights into interface quality and overall experience. These
methodologies include different forms of usability testing, expert heuristic
assessments, the use of eye-tracking techniques to understand visual attention, and
multimodal physiological analysis to infer the


the player's affective and cognitive developments. A scientific approach often
combines these methods to achieve a more complete and triangulated view.


**Usability Testing**, as described extensively by classic HCI authors such as **Jakob**
**Nielsen (1993)** and **Jeffrey Rubin and Dana Chisnell (2008)** in "Handbook of
Usability Testing", remains a fundamental technique. These tests involve users
representative of the target audience performing typical tasks with a prototype or
version of the game (e.g. "complete the tutorial", "find and equip a new object",
"navigate to this location on the map"), while qualified observers collect data on their
performance (success rate, time taken, number of errors), on their thought processes
(through the "think-aloud" protocol), **Ericsson & Simon, 1980** ) and their subjective
feedback (via post-task interviews or questionnaires such as the System Usability
Scale - SUS, **Brooke, 1996** ). Usability testing can be conducted **in the lab (in-lab),**
offering a controlled environment and the possibility of direct observation and detailed
recording, or **remotely**, where players test the game in their natural environment
using software for screen sharing, recording and remote communication. Remote
tests can be **moderated** (with a researcher guiding the session in real-time) or
**unmoderated** (where players follow predefined instructions and their behavior is
automatically recorded or tracked). Usability testing is particularly effective in
identifying specific interaction issues, points of confusion in the interface, and barriers
to understanding the mechanics. For example, a usability test on _The Witcher 3_ 's
crafting interface could reveal whether players easily understand how to find recipes,
how to get the necessary materials, and how to craft items.


**Heuristic Evaluation** is a usability inspection method in which a small group of


122


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


experienced evaluators (usually 3-5) examines the interface and judges its
compliance with a set of recognized usability principles (the "heuristics"). The ten
original usability heuristics by **Jakob Nielsen (1994)** (System state visibility;
Correspondence between system and real world; User control and freedom;
Consistency and standards; Error prevention; Recognition rather than recall;
Flexibility and efficiency of use; Aesthetic and minimalist design; Helping users
recognize, diagnose, and recover from errors; Help and documentation) are a
common starting point. **Heather Desurvire, David Kondziela and Joeક્ષी (1992,**
**1993)** have proposed specific adaptations of these heuristics for games, and more
recently **Pinelle, Wong and Stach (2008)** have developed a set of "Heuristics for
Playability" (PLAY Heuristics) covering aspects such as game mechanics, usability,
mobility, storytelling, feedback, etc. Heuristic evaluation is a relatively quick and
inexpensive method of identifying potential usability issues before engaging end
users, but its effectiveness depends heavily on the experience and expertise of the
evaluators.


**Cognitive Walkthrough** is another usability inspection technique, focused on
making it easy for new users to learn an interface (cf. **Wharton et al., 1994** ).
Evaluators put themselves in the shoes of a novice user trying to complete a specific
task for the first time, and ask themselves a series of structured questions at each
step of the interaction: Will the user try to achieve the right effect? Will the user notice
that the correct action is available? Will the user associate the correct action with the
effect they are trying to achieve? If the correct action is performed, will the user see
that progress is being made towards solving the task? This method is especially
useful for evaluating onboarding, tutorials, and learning basic mechanics. For
example, a cognitive walkthrough could be used to analyze whether the initial tutorial
of a complex strategy game like _Stellaris_ effectively guides a new player through the
first steps of managing their empire.


**Advanced Eye-Tracking** (see also Section 7.3 and 9.5) provides quantitative and
qualitative insights into players' visual attention. In addition to generating **heatmaps**
(showing the most fixed areas of the screen) and **gaze plots** (showing the sequence
of eye fixations), advanced analysis of eye-tracking data can reveal specific **visual**
**scanning patterns** associated with certain tasks or levels of expertise, identify **Areas**
**of Interest (AOIs)** dynamic (e.g., an enemy moving, a HUD element changing value)
and measuring metrics such as the time to first fixation on a target, the total duration
of fixations, or the number of fixations before taking an action. This data can be used
to assess whether crucial interface information is actually seen and processed by
players, whether the visual layout guides attention effectively, or whether there are
distracting or systematically ignored elements. For example, in a MOBA game like
_League of Legends_, eye-tracking could reveal how often players check the minimap,
their ability cooldowns, or teammates' health bar, informing the design and placement
of these crucial HUD elements. **Duchowski (2007)** provides a complete technical
treatment of eye-tracking.


**Multimodal Physiological Analysis** (see also Section 9.5) combines the
measurement of different biometric responses to obtain a more complete picture of
the player's affective and cognitive state during interaction with UI and UX. This may
include simultaneous recording of:


  - **Electrocardiogram (ECG)** for heart rate (HR) and heart rate variability
(HRV), which may indicate arousal, stress, or mental engagement.


123


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


  - **Galvanic Skin Response (EDA/GSR)** for emotional arousal.

  - **Facial Electromyography (fEMG)** to detect micro-expressions associated
with positive emotions (e.g. activation of the zygomaticus major muscle for
smiling) or negative emotions (e.g. activation of the corrugator muscle of the
eyebrow for concentration or frustration).

  - **Electroencephalogram (EEG)** to measure patterns of brain activity related
to attention, cognitive load (e.g., power in the alpha and theta bands), or
emotional responses (e.g., alpha frontal asymmetry).

  - **Skin Temperature** or **Respiration** . The combination of these measures (cf.
**Nacke, Grimshaw, & Mandryk, 2010**, for an example of a multimodal
approach) can help to triangulate the player's experience, for example by
identifying moments of high frustration (high arousal EDA, corrugator
activation, specific EEG patterns) associated with a particular interface
element or difficult task, even when the player may not explicitly verbalize
such frustration. However, the implementation of these methods requires
specialized equipment, expertise in signal acquisition and analysis, and
great caution in interpretation, since the relationship between physiological
signals and subjective experience is complex and influenced by individual
and contextual factors.


A scientific approach to UI/UX assessment often benefits from the **strategic**
**combination of these advanced methods** . For example, a usability test in the lab
could be enhanced with eye-tracking to understand where players are looking while
verbally expressing their difficulties, and with EDA measures to detect spikes in
arousal during moments of confusion or success. The results of an expert-led
heuristic assessment can guide the design of specific tasks for subsequent usability
testing with users. Large-scale A/B testing data can be supplemented with qualitative
interviews to understand the reasons behind the observed performance differences.


The choice of the most appropriate testing methodologies depends on the specific
objectives of the evaluation (e.g. identifying basic usability issues, optimizing a
specific flow, measuring the emotional impact of an interface, comparing different
design alternatives), the stage of the game development cycle (faster and cheaper
methods such as heuristic evaluation or testing on low-fidelity prototypes are suitable
for the early stages, while more complex and expensive methods can be used for
refinement and validation), the available resources (time, budget, equipment,
expertise) and the characteristics of the game and its target audience. The important
thing is to take a systematic, iterative, and evidence-based approach, using the data
collected to inform design decisions that lead to more intuitive, efficient, accessible
interfaces, and ultimately more positive and engaging user experiences.


**7.5. Accessibility of Interfaces and Inclusive Design for All**
**Players: Adherence to Standards (e.g. CVAA, EN 301 549),**
**Specific Guidelines (e.g. Game Accessibility Guidelines, APX)**
**and Assistive Technologies**


As already introduced in Chapter 5.5 in relation to gaming environments, the
principles of **inclusive design** and **accessibility** are of even more critical importance
when applied to the design of user interfaces (UIs) in video games. An inaccessible
interface can, in fact, completely preclude the gaming experience for a large portion
of potential players with disabilities, representing not only a loss for the latter, but also


124


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


a missed opportunity for developers in terms of audience and cultural impact. The
goal of inclusive design is to create interfaces that are **perceivable, operable,**
**understandable, and robust** for as many people as possible, regardless of their
physical, sensory, or cognitive abilities. This approach not only benefits players with
permanent disabilities, but often improves the experience for everyone, including
people with temporary disabilities (e.g., a broken arm), situational (e.g., playing in a
noisy environment or with direct sunlight on the screen), or aging-related.


Adherence to international and industry-specific **standards and guidelines** is a
critical starting point. In the United States, the **Communications and Video**
**Accessibility Act (CVAA)** imposes accessibility requirements for advanced video
communication equipment and services, including certain aspects of game
interfaces. In Europe, the **EN 301 549** standard establishes accessibility
requirements for ICT products and services, which is also applicable to video games
purchased by public bodies. On a more game-specific level, the **Game Accessibility**
**Guidelines (gameaccessibilityguidelines.com),** curated by a consortium of
specialists and developers, offer an extremely detailed and practical set of
recommendations divided into categories (Motor, Cognitive, Vision, Hearing,
Speech) and priority levels (Basic, Intermediate, Advanced). Other important
resources include the work of the **AbleGamers Foundation (ablegamers.org),**
which provides consulting, accessibility reviews (through the APX - Accessible Player
Experiences program) and promotes awareness, and the **IGDA Game Accessibility**
**Special Interest Group (SIG),** which brings together developers, researchers and
advocates to advance accessibility in games.


The **key accessibility considerations of the game UI** can be grouped by the type
of interaction or perception involved. On the **input side**, it is crucial to offer a
**complete and flexible remapping of controls**, allowing players to reassign any
game action to any key, button or stick on their preferred input device. This is
essential for players with motor limitations who may not be able to use standard
control layouts. Support for a **wide range of input devices** (keyboard and mouse,
different types of gamepads, specialized controllers such as the Xbox Adaptive
Controller, and even voice or eye-tracking input, where technologically feasible)
further expands accessibility. It is important **to avoid actions that require complex**
**simultaneous inputs** (e.g., pressing three keys at once), **extremely fast and**
**repeated button mashing**, or the need **to hold keys for extended periods ("hold**
**actions")** without offering alternatives such as "toggle" modes (single press to turn
on/off) or options to adjust stick sensitivity and dead zones and mouse. Games like
_The Last of Us Part II_ offer a level of control customization and options for "hold vs.
toggle" actions that has been widely praised.


For **visual output** and graphical user interface (GUI) design, considerations include
**scalable text size** across all interface elements (menus, subtitles, object
descriptions), high **contrast** between text and background and between important UI
elements, and the ability to enable **specific high-contrast modes** . For players with
**color blindness** (which affects a significant percentage of the population, especially
males), it is crucial **to avoid basing crucial information solely on color** (e.g.,
indicating the rarity of an item, the status of an enemy, or an item that can only be
interacted with a specific color). Shapes, patterns, distinctive icons, text labels, or
brightness variations should also be used to convey this information in a redundant
manner. Many modern games, such as _Forza Horizon 5_ (Playground Games, 2021),
offer customizable **colorblind filters** that remap problematic colors. The **clarity of**
**icons and symbols** is equally important; they should be universally understandable


125


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


or accompanied by optional text labels. You need **to minimize visual clutter on the**
HUD and in the menus, presenting only the essential information at the right time and
allowing customization of the displayed elements. Finally, it is crucial **to avoid visual**
**elements that flash or twinkle at specific frequencies** (typically between 3Hz and
55Hz) that could trigger seizures in individuals with photosensitive epilepsy; there are
specific guidelines (such as those based on the Harding test) for prevention.


Regarding **auditory output**, accessibility for players with hearing disabilities (from
complete deafness to different degrees of hearing loss) requires the provision of
**accurate, timely, and customizable subtitles** for all spoken dialogue, and ideally
**captions for** ambient sounds and sound effects that are important to gameplay (e.g.,
"enemy footsteps to the left", "alarm sounds", "door opens"). Subtitles and captions
should offer options to adjust the text size, color, background (to improve contrast),
and speaker indication. It is also good practice to allow players to **separately adjust**
**the volume** of different sound categories (music, sound effects, dialogue, voice
chat). Basically, no critical gameplay information should be conveyed _solely_ through
sound; there should always be **visual or tactile alternatives** (e.g., a visual indicator
on the screen showing the direction of an enemy attack that has been signaled by a
sound).


For **cognitive and learning disabilities**, which can affect attention, memory,
language comprehension, problem solving, or spatial orientation, the user interface
should aim for maximum **clarity, simplicity, and consistency** . This includes
providing **clear, concise, repeatable and accessible tutorials in different modes**
(text, images, videos, interactive guided practice) for learning game mechanics and
interface features. It's helpful to offer **persistent or easily accessible reminders of**
**your current goals** and control scheme (e.g., a command legend that can be quickly
viewed). The **language** used in the interface (menus, instructions, descriptions)
should be as simple and clear as possible, avoiding unnecessary technical jargon or
overly complex sentences. For players with dyslexia, the use of **readable sans-serif**
**fonts**, options to increase letter and line spacing, and the ability to adjust text size
are important. Offering the ability to **adjust the pace of the game** (e.g. slowing down
the speed of certain events, allowing more time to read texts, or make decisions in
non-competitive games) can be of great help.


Integrating accessibility should not be seen as a task to be relegated to the end of
the development process or as the creation of a separate "easy mode". On the
contrary, the principles of **Inclusive Design** and **Universal Design** suggest
considering accessibility from the very early stages of concept and design, trying to
create a basic experience that is as flexible and accommodating as possible for a
wide range of users. This often involves offering **options and customizations** that
allow players to tailor the interface and gameplay experience to their individual needs
and preferences.


The **inclusive design process** requires **awareness and continuous training** from
the entire development team on the different accessibility needs. It's helpful to include
**personas with disabilities** in your use cases to guide your design. Flexible **design**
of information input, output, and presentation systems is critical. But above all,
**iterative testing with users with real disabilities** is essential. Direct feedback from
players with different types of disabilities is the most effective and authentic way to
identify accessibility barriers that are often invisible to able-bodied designers and to
validate the effectiveness of the implemented solutions. Organizations like


126
