GAME DESIGN MANUAL


AbleGamers often facilitate these tests or provide expert advice.


A scientific approach to UI accessibility is based on the application of evidence-based
design principles (many of which derive from research in HCI and psychology), an indepth understanding of human abilities and limitations (drawing from ergonomics,
cognitive science, and rehabilitation medicine), and the continuous empirical
validation of solutions through rigorous testing with a wide and diverse range of users.
Designing inclusive interfaces not only makes games accessible to more people,
promoting equity and social inclusion, but often leads to better, more robust, more
flexible, and more usable design for all players, regardless of their abilities.


**7.6. Conclusions: The Interface as a Transparent and**
**Empowering Bridge to the Playful Experience**


The user interface and overall user experience, as explored in this chapter, represent
the crucial and often delicate bridge that connects the player to the virtual worlds,
complex mechanics, and compelling narratives created by the game designer. A welldesigned interface, which is informed by the scientific principles of Human-Computer
Interaction, cognitive psychology and an empathetic and player-centered approach,
has the potential to become almost invisible, a natural extension of the player's
intentions that allows him to fully and frictionlessly immerse himself in the gaming
experience. Conversely, a cumbersome, confusing, inefficient, or inaccessible UI can
erect insurmountable barriers, turning even the most brilliant game concept and
innovative mechanics into a source of frustration, exclusion, and ultimately
abandonment.


We have seen how the fundamentals of HCI, applied with sensitivity to the playful
context, can guide the creation of interfaces that minimize cognitive load, maximize
information clarity and provide timely and effective feedback. The adoption of a
Player-Centered Design process, which uses methodologies such as the creation of
personas, the definition of use scenarios and user journeys, and above all iterative
prototyping (from sketches on paper to interactive digital prototypes), has been
fundamental to align the design with the real needs, skills and expectations of players.


The scientific evaluation of Game UX, a multidimensional construct that embraces
usability, involvement, pleasure, challenge and emotional impact, requires the use of
specific theoretical models (such as PLEX or CEGE) and the development of reliable
metrics, both subjective (questionnaires, interviews) and objective (behavioral data,
physiological measures). Advanced and combined testing methodologies, ranging
from remote and laboratory usability testing, expert heuristic evaluation, to eyetracking and multimodal biometric analysis, provide designers and researchers with
increasingly sophisticated tools to deeply understand the player's interaction with the
interface and the resulting experience.


Finally, the commitment to inclusive design and accessibility of interfaces emerged
not only as an essential ethical and social responsibility, but also as an indicator of
excellence in design and attention to user diversity. Designing interfaces that are
perceivable, operable, understandable, and robust for all players, regardless of their
abilities, adhering to recognized standards and guidelines, and actively involving
users with disabilities in the testing process, not only expands a game's potential
audience, but often leads to more elegant, flexible, and universally better design


127


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


solutions.


In conclusion, the user interface and user experience, when designed with a
scientific, empathetic and iterative approach, cease to be simple graphic "overlays"
or sets of menus, to become real **transparent and empowering bridges** to the heart
of the gaming experience. They are the language through which the game
communicates to the player and the player expresses his agency in the game world.
Their excellence lies not in their visibility, but in their ability to fade, leaving the player
free to explore, experiment, learn, get excited and, above all, to fully enjoy the rich
and engaging world that the game designer intended to create.


128


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


129


GAME DESIGN MANUAL

# **Chapter 8**

### **User Research and Scientific** **Playtesting: Advanced** **Methodologies and Data** **Analysis**


_The opposite of play is not work. It's depression_


_**-Brian Sutton-Smith-**_


130


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


Game design, especially when it pursues a scientific and evidence-based approach,
cannot be separated from a rigorous, systematic and continuous interaction with the
players, the real recipients and co-creators of the gaming experience. Playtesting
and, more generally, **user research (RU)** are fundamental and irreplaceable
processes for collecting empirical data on the actual gaming experience, for
validating (or refuting) the design hypotheses formulated, for identifying usability,
understanding, balancing or engagement issues, and for informing the iterative
decisions that drive development towards a higher quality final product. This chapter
will delve into scientific methodologies of playtesting and user research applied to
game design, exploring the crucial role of iterative playtesting within the development
cycle. Advanced qualitative research methodologies, such as phenomenological
interviews and virtual ethnographic studies, will be presented and discussed, allowing
to grasp the depth and subjectivity of the player's experience. Next, quantitative
research techniques will be examined, with a focus on game analytics, advanced
telemetry for large-scale behavior understanding, and the design of controlled
experiments to test specific hypotheses. The importance of a mixed-methods
approach, which integrates the strengths of both traditions, will also be emphasized.
Finally, a substantial section will be dedicated to the essential ethical considerations
that must guide any form of research conducted with human participants in the playful
context, from the management of informed consent to data privacy and the protection
of vulnerable subjects. The overall goal is to provide designers and researchers with
the methodological tools to transform design insights and hypotheses into
scientifically validated knowledge, recognizing and valuing the player not as a simple
"tester", but as an active and informed partner in the process of creating and refining
the gaming experience.


**8.1. Playtesting as a Training and Summative Research Method**
**in the Scientific Development Cycle of the Game: Objectives,**
**Phases and Best Practices (Fullerton, Isbister)**


Playtesting is the systematic process of observing and collecting feedback from
individuals (the "playtesters") while playing a version of the game in development,
which can range from a low-fidelity print or digital prototype to alpha, beta, or even
the full game in post-launch. Far from being a simple "QA" activity to find bugs
(although it can also contribute to this), playtesting is a fundamental form of **research**
**with users specifically focused on the gaming experience and interaction with**
**gaming systems** . As brilliantly argued by **Tracy Fullerton (2008, 2014)** in her text
"Game Design Workshop: A Playcentric Approach to Creating Innovative Games", a
"Playcentric" approach to design, which places the act of playing and the player's
experience at the center of every decision, requires continuous and iterative cycles
of playtesting. **Katherine Isbister (2016),** in "How Games Move Us: Emotion by
Design", further emphasizes how playtesting is crucial to understanding the
emotional impact and overall player experience. In a scientific game development
cycle, playtesting fits into a logical, repeating sequence:


1. **Formulation of Design Hypotheses and Objectives:** The designer (or
team) formulates specific ideas, concepts, and hypotheses about how
certain mechanics, systems, interfaces, narratives, or aesthetic elements


131


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


will affect the player's experience (e.g., "this new parry mechanic will
increase the sense of mastery and reduce frustration in combat").
2. **Prototyping:** A playable version of the game, or a specific portion of it, is
created that implements the ideas and hypotheses to be tested. The fidelity
of the prototype can vary enormously depending on the development phase
and the objectives of the test (see Chapter 10.2).
3. **Planning the Playtest:** You define the specific objectives of the playtest
session (what do you want to find out?), the research questions, the profile
of the participants to be recruited (who should be as representative as
possible of the target audience), the tasks or game scenarios to be
proposed, the metrics to be collected (qualitative and/or quantitative) and
the logistics of the session.
4. **Conducting the Playtest:** The prototype is tested with recruited players, in
an environment that can range from a formal usability lab to more
naturalistic or remote settings. During the session, players' behaviors are
observed, their verbal comments are collected (often using the "think-aloud"
protocol), and questionnaires can be administered or interviews can be
conducted.
5. **Data Analysis and Interpretation:** The data collected (observation notes,
interview transcripts, questionnaire responses, performance metrics,
telemetry) are systematically analyzed to identify significant patterns, design
issues (e.g., points of frustration, misunderstood mechanics, imbalances),
positive aspects of the experience, and insights for improvement.
6. **Design Iteration:** Based on the analysis of the playtest results, the game
design is modified, refined, or, if necessary, radically rethought. The
changes are implemented in a new prototype or build of the game, and the
cycle begins again with a new playtesting phase.


This iterative cycle of "hypothesis design -> prototype -> testing -> analysis ->
redesign" is the engine of evidence-based and player-centered game design. The
**specific goals of playtesting** vary significantly depending on the **stage of the**
**game's development cycle** . In the **early stages of concept and pre-production**,
playtesting often focuses on testing the **feasibility and enjoyment potential of core**
**core mechanics** . Ultra-low-fidelity prototypes (e.g., prototypes on paper, board
games that simulate digital mechanics, or simple "digital sketches") are frequently
used to answer questions such as: "Is this basic idea understandable? Is it inherently
engaging? What are the fundamental 'verbs' of the game and how do they feel?". An
example is the playtesting on paper of the turn-based combat system of _Slay the_
_Spire_ (MegaCrit, 2019) before even writing a line of code, to validate the flow of cards
and synergies. In this phase, the "fun factor" is explored and the designer's initial
assumptions about the desired experience are validated.


During the **production phase**, when the game begins to take a more defined shape,
playtesting becomes more structured and focuses on a wider range of aspects. UI **/UX**
**testing is conducted** to identify problems with controls, menu navigability, clarity of
information presentation, and accessibility (see Chapter 7). Specific tests are carried
out for the **balance of the gameplay**, to verify that the game is neither too easy nor
too difficult, that there are no dominant strategies or "exploits" that break the
experience, and that the different options available to the player (classes, weapons,
skills) are reasonably balanced (see Chapter 3.5). Level **design testing** (see Chapter
5) assesses the navigability of spaces, the flow of experience through levels, clarity
of objectives, and progression of the difficulty curve. If the game has a narrative
component, you test your **understanding of the story**, your emotional involvement


132


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


with the characters, and the impact of your narrative choices. While the primary goal
of design playtesting is not "bug hunting," technical issues or glitches also often
surface during these sessions and are reported to the QA team.


In **post-production, alpha, and beta testing**, playtesting often expands to a larger
number of participants (sometimes thousands, in the case of open betas) to gather
feedback at scale, identify rarer or hardware-specific issues, and to get a broader
view of the overall reception of the game by the target audience before launch. For
online games, this phase often includes **stress tests** to check the stability of servers
and network infrastructure under load. The data collected during beta testing can also
be valuable for planning early post-launch updates, further balancing the game, or
identifying areas that could benefit from additional content. For example, open betas
of games like _Diablo IV_ or _Call of Duty_ are crucial for gathering feedback on class
balance, server stability, and overall experience before official launch.


There are different **types of playtesting**, each with its own advantages and
disadvantages. Internal **playtesting**, conducted by members of the development
team themselves, is useful for quick checks, testing core functionality, and very early
iterations. However, it is limited by the team's deep familiarity with the game (which
can lead to not seeing obvious problems for a new player) and possible internal bias
(the "tunnel vision" or reluctance to criticize one's own work). Playtesting **with friends**
**and family** can provide honest and helpful feedback in the very early stages, but
even here participants may be less critical so as not to hurt the designer's feelings,
or they may not be representative of the actual target audience. Formal **playtesting**
**with external users**, which involves the targeted recruitment of players who match
the target audience profile and conducting structured sessions (often in a dedicated
usability lab or controlled environment), is generally considered the most rigorous
method of obtaining high-quality data. **Remote playtesting**, in which players test the
game in their own home environment, often providing feedback through online
questionnaires, video recordings of their gameplay (with audio commentary), or
gameplay sessions shared via streaming with a moderator, has become increasingly
popular, especially for its ability to reach a larger, geographically distributed audience
and to observe the game in a more naturalistic context. Finally, **quantitative**
**playtesting**, which relies on the use of **game analytics and telemetry** (see Section
8.3) to automatically track the behavior of a large number of players, provides largescale data that can supplement and validate qualitative insights gained from smaller
sessions.


To conduct effective and scientifically sound playtesting, it is crucial to adhere to
some **methodological best practices** . First of all, it is crucial **to define clear and**
**specific objectives** for each playtest session: what do you want to learn? What
aspects of the design are being tested? What are the research questions that are
being answered? Secondly, it is necessary **to recruit the right participants**, i.e.
players who are as representative as possible of the target audience for which the
game is intended, considering factors such as age, gender, previous gaming
experience and preferences. Third, it is essential to create a **testing environment**
**that is comfortable, welcoming, and non-judgmental** for participants. Players
should feel free to express their honest opinions and criticism, even negative ones,
without fear of being judged or offending the designers. Importantly, you are testing
the game, not the player's skill or intelligence. During the session, the
observer/moderator should **carefully observe** not only what the players say, but also
their nonverbal behavior (facial expressions, body language, moments of hesitation,
sighs of frustration), which can reveal a lot about the lived experience. It's important


133


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


**to ask open-ended, neutral, non-addressing questions** (e.g., "What do you think
of this part?" instead of "Don't you think this part is fun?"), encouraging players to
process their thoughts and provide specific examples. The role of the designer or
researcher during playtesting is primarily to **actively listen and learn**, resisting the
temptation to defend their design choices or "correct" the player. After the session, it
is crucial **to systematically document the findings**, identifying key issues, behavior
patterns, meaningful quotes, and suggestions that emerged. Finally, the core
principle is rapid **iteration** : playtest feedback should be used to make concrete
changes to the game's design, and these changes should be retested as soon as
possible, in a continuous cycle of "build test analyze iterate."


Iterative playtesting, conducted with methodological rigor and a genuine desire to
understand the player experience, is one of the most powerful and irreplaceable
practices for scientific, user-centered game design. It transforms the design process
from an activity based on mere assumptions or personal preferences to one that is
informed by empirical observation and oriented towards continuous improvement,
significantly increasing the likelihood of creating a gaming experience that is not only
functional and hassle-free, but also deeply engaging, satisfying and memorable for
the target audience.


**8.2. Advanced Methodologies of Qualitative Research with**
**Players: Phenomenological Interviews, Thematic Analysis and**
**Grounded Theory of Game Reports, Virtual Ethnographic**
**Studies and Netnography of Player Communities**


While the most common qualitative research methods in playtesting, such as semistructured interviews and focus groups (already introduced in Chapter 1.4 and
discussed more extensively in Chapter 7.4 in relation to usability testing), provide
valuable insights, there are more advanced and specialized qualitative approaches
that can offer an even deeper, richer, and nuanced understanding of subjective
experiences. of the co-constructed meanings and socio-cultural contexts of the
game. These methods are particularly useful when you want to explore complex
phenomena, generate new theories, or understand the "lived experience" of players
holistically.


**Phenomenological interviews** represent a particularly powerful approach to
exploring the subjective nature of the gaming experience. Based on the philosophical
tradition of phenomenology (cf. **Husserl, Heidegger, Merleau Ponty** ) and
methodologically adapted by researchers such as **Amedeo Giorgi (2009)** or **Max**
**van Manen (1990),** phenomenological interviews aim to grasp the essence of a
specific experience (e.g. "the experience of deep immersion in a VR game", "the
experience of significant but motivating frustration in a 'souls-like' game", "the
experience of forming an emotional bond with a non-player character") as it is
experienced and described by the participant. Unlike semi-structured interviews that
are more focused on specific aspects of design, phenomenological interviews are
typically very open-ended, starting with a broad question (e.g., "Can you describe to
me a particularly memorable experience you had playing X?") and encouraging the
participant to recount their experience in detail, with the researcher intervening
minimally, mainly to ask for clarification or insights. Data analysis focuses on
identifying the essential structures and invariant themes of the lived experience,
trying to go beyond superficial descriptions to grasp the deep meaning that the


134


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


experience had for the participant. This approach can be extremely useful for game
designers who want to understand the more elusive experiential qualities and deep
emotional impact of their games, such as the experience of catharsis or personal
transformation that some players bring back after playing titles such as _Gris_ (Nomada
Studio, 2018) or _Hellblade: Senua's Sacrifice_ .


Thematic **analysis**, as described by **Virginia Braun and Victoria Clarke (2006),** is
a flexible and widely used method for identifying, analyzing, and reporting patterns
within qualitative data, such as interview transcripts, game reports, forum posts, or
user reviews. Although it can be used more or less inductively (bottom up, where
themes emerge from the data) or deductively (top down, where predefined themes
based on existing theories are sought), rigorous thematic analysis involves a
systematic process of familiarization with the data, generating initial codes, searching
for themes, reviewing themes, defining and naming themes, and finally production
of the report. In game design, thematic analysis can be used to analyze large
amounts of qualitative feedback from playtests to identify the most recurring design
issues, the most valued features, or the different interpretations that players give of
a mechanic or narrative event. For example, analyzing online discussions about an
open world game like _Starfield_ (Bethesda Game Studios, 2023), one could identify
recurring themes related to the vastness of the world, the quality of the main quests,
or the perception of repetitiveness of certain activities.


**Grounded Theory**, originally developed by **Barney Glaser and Anselm Strauss**
**(1967),** is a systematic methodological approach that aims to generate theories that
are grounded in the qualitative data collected. Unlike approaches that start from a
pre-existing theory to be tested, Grounded Theory is an inductive process of
theoretical discovery. It involves iterative cycles of data collection (often interviews or
observations), data coding (open, axial, and selective), writing theoretical memos,
and theoretical sampling (selecting new participants or contexts to deepen or saturate
emerging theoretical categories). Although it requires significant effort, Grounded
Theory can be very useful in game design to develop new understandings or
theoretical models on complex aspects of the player experience that are not well
explained by existing theories, or to understand emerging dynamics in new game
genres or player communities. For example, you could use Grounded Theory to
develop a model of the different coping strategies that players adopt when faced with
monetization mechanics perceived as aggressive in F2P games.


Virtual **ethnographic studies** (or **netnography**, cf. **Kozinets, 2010, 2015** ) adapt
traditional ethnography methods (prolonged participant observation, informal
interviews, analysis of cultural artifacts) to the study of communities and cultures that
form in and around online gaming environments and digital platforms. Researchers
such as **Bonnie Nardi (2010)** in her study of _World of Warcraft_ ("My Life as a Night
Elf Priest") or **T.L. Taylor (2006)** in "Play Between Worlds: Exploring Online Game
Culture" (which examines the culture of _EverQuest_ ), have shown how long-term
immersion in online gaming communities can reveal complex social dynamics,
cultural norms, the rituals, power hierarchies, conflicts, forms of social capital and
identities that players build and negotiate in these virtual spaces. For designers of
online multiplayer games, MMORPGs, or games with strong social components,
insights from virtual ethnographic studies can be invaluable in understanding how
game systems are actually used and appropriated by players, what social dynamics
emerge (often unforeseen by the original design), and how to design systems that
better support the formation of positive and sustainable communities. For example,
a netnographic study of discussions on Reddit or Discord related to a competitive


135


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


game could reveal the root causes of perceived toxicity and suggest possible design
or community management interventions.


Other advanced qualitative methods that can be applied include **Critical Discourse**
**Analysis (CDA),** which examines how language and representations in games (and
discussions about them) construct and reflect power relations, ideologies, and social
inequalities (cf. **Fairclough, 2010** ). This can be useful for critically analyzing
representations of gender, ethnicity, or class in games. **Autoethnography**, where
the researcher uses his own subjective experience of gambling as a primary datum,
analyzing it in a reflective way and connecting it to broader theories, can offer unique
insights, although it requires a great awareness of one's own biases (cf. **Ellis,**
**Adams, & Bochner, 2011** ). Finally, **narrative analysis of game accounts** (e.g.,
game journals, "Let's Play" videos, detailed reviews) can reveal how players
construct meaning and co-create stories through their interaction with the game.


The choice of these advanced qualitative methods depends on the nature of the
research questions, the desired depth of understanding, and the resources available
(many of these methods are time-consuming). The analysis of qualitative data, as
already mentioned, is an interpretative process that requires methodological rigor,
transparency and constant reflexivity on the part of the researcher to ensure the
credibility and reliability of the results. Techniques such as **triangulation** (use of
multiple data sources or methods), **member checking** (validation of interpretations
with participants) and **audit trail** (detailed documentation of the research process)
can help to increase rigor. For a scientifically oriented game designer, mastering
these advanced qualitative methods opens the door to a richer, deeper, and more
humanly centered understanding of the gaming experience, going beyond the
surface of observable behaviors to grasp the meanings, emotions, and contexts that
shape it.


**8.3. Game Analytics and Advanced Telemetry for Understanding**
**Behavior at Scale: Definition of Meaningful Key Performance**
**Indicators (KPIs), Player Behavioral Segmentation, Predictive**
**Modeling (Churn, Engagement) and Systematic A/B Testing**


While qualitative methods offer depth of understanding about the subjective
experience of a limited number of players, quantitative methods, in particular the use
of **telemetry** and **game analytics**, allow data on the behavior of a very large number
of players (often millions, in the case of successful live games) to be collected and
analyzed automatically and continuously. This data-driven approach has become
indispensable for modern game design, especially for games as a service (GaaS),
balancing complex systems, optimizing the user experience at scale, and making
evidence-based design and business decisions. **Magy Seif El-Nasr, Anders**
**Drachen and Alessandro Canossa (2013),** in their text "Game Analytics:
Maximizing the Value of Player Data", provide a complete and fundamental treatment


136


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


of this field.


Telemetry refers to the technical process of automatically collecting detailed data
about players' interaction with the game system. This data, which can include a
myriad **of in-game events** (e.g., every click, every character movement, every skill
used, every enemy defeated, every item collected or purchased, every mission
completed or failed, every social interaction), is typically sent by game clients (PC,
console, mobile devices) to central servers where it is stored, processed, and made
available for analysis. Designing an effective telemetry system requires careful
planning of which events to track (to avoid collecting unnecessary or overly granular
data that burdens the system) and how to structure the data to facilitate later analysis.
For example, tracking players' location in the game world at regular intervals allows
you to create **heatmaps** that visualize the busiest areas or the points where players
get stuck or die most often, as has been done in games such as _Tom Clancy's The_
_Division_ (Ubisoft Massive, 2016) to optimize level design and match placement.


Game **analytics** represent the application of data analysis techniques – ranging from
descriptive statistics and visualizations to more advanced methods of data mining,
machine learning and predictive modeling – to telemetry data and other sources of
information (such as sales data, social media feedback, survey results) to extract
meaningful and actionable insights. The goal is to transform the "big data" generated
by players into useful knowledge to improve game design, player experience and, in
commercial contexts, business performance. A crucial first step in the analysis is the
definition of meaningful **Key Performance Indicators (KPIs),** i.e. specific metrics
that reflect design or business objectives. For an F2P mobile game, important KPIs
could include **player retention** (Day-1, Day-7, Day-30 retention), **conversion rate**
(percentage of players making in-app purchases), **Average Revenue Per User**
**(ARPU)** or Per Paying User (ARPPU), and average **length of game** sessions. For a
single-player RPG, relevant KPIs could be the completion rate of the main campaign,
the average time to complete certain quests, or the relative usage of different classes
or skills.


One of the most powerful applications of game analytics is **player behavioral**
**segmentation** . Instead of relying on predefined taxonomies (such as Bartle's),
clustering algorithms (e.g., k-means, hierarchical clustering) can be used on
telemetry data to identify groups of players exhibiting similar behavior patterns (e.g.,
players who prefer exploration, PvP-focused players, players who interact a lot with
crafting systems, players who frequently get stuck in tutorials). Understanding these
emerging behavioral segments allows designers to personalize the experience,
balance the game for different styles of play, or target specific communications and
offers. For example, **Drachen et al. (2009)** used the analysis of game data from
_Tomb Raider: Underworld_ to identify different playstyles and navigation issues.


Predictive **modeling** through machine learning techniques is another area of great
interest. You can build models to predict, for example, **churn** (probability that a player
will leave the game within a certain period), **future engagement**, or the probability
that a player will make a purchase. These models, once trained on historical data,
can be used to identify players at risk of churn and to implement targeted
interventions (e.g. offering them a special incentive, simplifying a challenge perceived
as too difficult), or to optimize monetization strategies in a more personalized way.
However, the use of predictive models also raises important ethical questions related
to transparency, potential manipulation, and the risk of creating "filter bubbles" or


137


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


overly optimized experiences for value extraction.


Systematic **A/B testing** (or multivariate testing), as already mentioned in Chapter
7.4, is a fundamental practice made possible and scalable by game analytics. It
allows you to rigorously compare the impact of different versions of a feature, UI
element, mechanic balance, or monetization offer on specific KPIs, presenting the
different variants to random segments of the player population and statistically
measuring differences in results. This continuous experimentation approach is at the
heart of the "data-informed design" philosophy (rather than "data-driven design" to
emphasize that data informs but does not entirely replace the designer's vision)
adopted by many successful companies, especially in the mobile and online gaming
industries.


The **analysis techniques** used in game analytics are different and range from the
simplest to the most complex. **Descriptive statistics** (means, medians, standard
deviations, frequency distributions) and **data visualizations** (bar charts, column
charts, scatter plots, interactive dashboards) are key to exploring data and
communicating results in a way that is understandable. Cohort **analysis** tracks the
behavior of groups of players who started playing at the same time, to understand
how their engagement and retention evolve over time. **Funnel analysis** examines
player switching rates through a series of sequential steps (e.g., the onboarding
funnel of new users, from the first game launch to the completion of the tutorial and
the first significant game session), to identify the points where the most dropouts
occur. Data **mining** techniques such as association analysis can reveal hidden
patterns (e.g., "players who use weapon X also tend to buy item Y").


However, the effective use of game analytics also presents **challenges** . The first is
the management and processing of **large volumes of data (Big Data),** which
requires adequate technological infrastructures (scalable databases, distributed
processing tools) and specialized skills (data scientists, data engineers). Player
**privacy** and compliance with regulations such as the GDPR are imperative ethical
and legal considerations (see Section 8.5). Another challenge is the **correct**
**interpretation of the data** : it is essential to remember that "correlation does not imply
causation". Observing that two variables are related does not necessarily mean that
one causes the other; There may be hidden variables or confounding factors. To
establish causal relationships, more rigorous experimental designs are required (see
Section 8.4). Finally, there is the risk of a **"tyranny of metrics",** i.e. focusing
excessively only on aspects of the game that are easily measurable and quantifiable
(such as playtime or average spend), at the expense of more elusive but equally
important experiential qualities, such as emotional depth, narrative meaning or player
creativity. A balanced scientific approach requires integrating quantitative insights
from game analytics with the wealth of qualitative understanding gained through other
user research methods.


In conclusion, game analytics and advanced telemetry offer game designers
unprecedented tools to understand player behavior at scale, to optimize game
systems in an evidence-based way, and to personalize the gaming experience.
However, their use requires not only technical and analytical skills, but also a solid
understanding of game design principles, constant critical reflection on data
interpretation, and a strong commitment to ethical practices that place player wellbeing and positive experience at the center of every decision.


138


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


**8.4. Design and Conduct of Experimental and Quasi-**
**Experimental Studies in Game Design: Manipulation of Game**
**Variables, Control of Confounding Factors, Reliable Outcome**
**Measures,** **Rigorous** **Statistical** **Analysis** **and** **Cautious**
**Interpretation of Results**


While game analytics provide observational data at scale and qualitative methods
offer depth of understanding, **experimental** and **quasi-experimental studies**
represent the most rigorous methodological approach to establishing **cause-and-**
**effect relationships** between specific game design variables and outcomes related
to player experience or behavior. These methods, borrowed from behavioral sciences
and psychological research, allow designers and researchers to test specific
hypotheses in a controlled way, isolating the impact of certain design choices.


The core of a **true experimental study lies** in the **systematic manipulation of one**
**or more independent variables (VIs)** by the researcher/designer, and in the
measurement of the effect of such manipulation on one or more **dependent**
**variables (VDs** ), maintaining constant or statistically controlling other potentially
confounding variables. The VI is the feature of the game or interface that is
deliberately modified (e.g. two different versions of a tutorial, the presence or
absence of a certain reward mechanic, different difficulty levels for the same task,
different interface layouts). VD is the outcome measure that is presumed to be
influenced by VI (e.g., time to completion of a task, score obtained, level of
engagement measured with a validated questionnaire, heart rate, success rate in a
challenge). A crucial element for causal inference in real experiments is the **random**
**assignment** of participants to different experimental conditions (the different levels
of VI). Random assignment helps ensure that groups are likely to be equivalent at
the start of the study with respect to unmeasured individual variables, thereby
reducing the risk that observed differences in VD are due to pre-existing factors rather
than manipulation of VI.


There are several **common experimental designs** . In a **between-subjects design**,
different participants are assigned to different experimental conditions. For example,
to test the effectiveness of two tutorials (Tutorial A vs. Tutorial B) for a new strategy
game, one group of players would receive Tutorial A and another group, randomly
assigned, would receive Tutorial B. Their subsequent performance on a standardized
task or their short-term engagement could be compared. In a **within-subjects**
**design or repeated measures design**, the same participants are exposed to all
experimental conditions. For example, each player could try Tutorial A first and then
Tutorial B (or vice versa, with the order of conditions **counterbalanced** between
participants to control learning, fatigue, or carryover effects). This design has the
advantage of requiring fewer participants and better controlling individual differences
(since each participant acts as a control of himself), but it may be subject to effects
of order or practice. **Factorial designs** allow two or more VIs (called "factors") to be
manipulated at the same time, allowing you to study not only the **main effects** of
each VI on VD, but also their **interactions** (i.e., whether the effect of one VI depends
on the level of another VI). For example, you could conduct a 2x2 experiment
manipulating the type of feedback (positive vs. negative) and the difficulty of the task
(high vs. low) to see how these two factors interact in influencing player motivation.


When random assignment of participants to conditions is not possible or practicable


139


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


(often for ethical or logistical reasons), it is referred to as **quasi-experimental**
**studies** . For example, if you want to compare the engagement of players who
voluntarily choose to play a game with a new feature versus those who play the
version without the feature, you don't have a random assignment. Quasiexperimental designs (such as non-equivalent control group designs or interrupted
time-series designs) attempt to approximate the conditions of a true experiment by
using statistical or matching techniques to control for confounding factors, but causal
inference is generally weaker (cf. **Shadish, Cook, & Campbell, 2002**, "Experimental
and Quasi-Experimental Designs for Generalized Causal Inference").


Conducting an experimental study in game design requires meticulous planning. It
starts with **the formulation of a clear and testable hypothesis** (e.g. "Players
exposed to Tutorial B will show a significantly higher completion rate of the first level
than players exposed to Tutorial A"). The study is then designed, choosing the most
appropriate experimental design, operationally defining the VIs and VDs, selecting or
developing **reliable and valid outcome measures** (e.g. standardized psychometric
questionnaires, objective performance metrics, physiological measures), recruiting
an adequate sample of participants (the **statistical** power of the of the study, i.e. the
ability to detect an effect if it exists, depends on the sample size, the magnitude of
the expected effect and the chosen level of alpha significance), and preparing the
experimental materials and procedures in a standardized way to ensure that all
participants receive consistent instruction and treatments within their condition.
During **data collection**, it is critical to minimize observer bias and ensure data
integrity.


**Statistical analysis** of the collected data is a crucial step in determining whether the
observed differences between groups or conditions are **statistically significant** (i.e.,
if they are unlikely to be due to pure chance) and for estimating the **magnitude of**
**the effect (** i.e., the practical magnitude of the observed difference or relationship).
Depending on the type of data and the experimental design, appropriate statistical
tests can be used such as the Student's t-test to compare the means of two groups,
the Analysis of Variance (ANOVA) to compare the means of multiple groups or for
factorial designs, the chi-square test to analyze categorical data, or regression
models to examine the relationships between continuous variables (cf. **Field, 2013**,
"Discovering Statistics Using IBM SPSS Statistics", for a practical guide to the
application of these tests).


Interpreting **the results** requires caution and critical thinking. A statistically significant
result does not necessarily imply that the effect is large or practically significant. It is
important to consider the magnitude of the effect, the limitations of the study (e.g.,
sample-specific characteristics that could limit generalizability, potential uncontrolled
confounders), and the theoretical and practical implications of the results for game
design. The **internal validity** of the study (the confidence that the VI actually caused
the changes in VD) and the **external validity** (the possibility of generalizing the
results to other player populations, other games, or other contexts) should be
carefully evaluated.


In the context of game design, experimental studies can be used for a wide range of
purposes. **A/B testing** of specific features or interface elements (as discussed in
Section 8.3 and Chapter 7.4) is a simple but powerful form of experiment, widely used
in the industry, especially for online and mobile games, to optimize metrics such as
conversion rates or engagement. Experiments can be conducted to **evaluate the**


140


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


**effectiveness of different types of tutorials** or feedback systems on learning
mechanics or game performance, such as comparing a text tutorial with an interactive
one for a strategy game. The **impact of specific mechanics on player**
**engagement, emotions, or social behavior** can be studied, for example by
comparing a version of a game with a social reward mechanic (e.g., bonuses for team
play) and a version without such a mechanic, and then measuring levels of
cooperation or social satisfaction. Academic research on games often uses
experimental designs to investigate the **cognitive, emotional, or behavioral effects**
of playing certain types of video games (e.g., the impact of action games on reaction
speed or visual attention, cf. **Green & Bavelier, 2003** ; or the effects of violent games
on aggression, a complex and debated area of research, cf. **Ferguson, 2015**, for a
critical meta-analysis).


Although designing and conducting rigorous experimental studies can be timeconsuming and resource-intensive, and require methodological and statistical
expertise, they provide the strongest evidence to support causal claims in game
design and to build a scientific knowledge base on "what works, for whom, and in
what contexts". Integrating experimental approaches within the iterative development
process, even in simplified forms or focused on A/B testing, can lead to more
informed design decisions and games that more effectively achieve their experiential
goals.


**8.5. Ethics of Research in Games and with Players: Detailed**
**Informed Consent, Privacy and Anonymity Management of Data**
**(Especially Biometric and Telemetry), Protection of Vulnerable**
**Participants, Role of Ethics Committees (IRBs) and Debriefing**
**Practices**


Any form of research involving human participants, including playtesting, surveys,
experiments, and the collection of telemetry data in the context of game design, must
be conducted with the utmost attention to **fundamental ethical principles** and with
full respect for the rights, dignity, and well-being of players. The power of games to
influence behavior and emotions, combined with the growing ability to collect detailed
player data, imposes an increased ethical responsibility on designers and
researchers. International reference documents such as the **Nuremberg Code**
**(1947),** the **Declaration of Helsinki (World Medical Association, originally 1964,**
**with subsequent revisions),** and especially the **Belmont Report (National**
**Commission for the Protection of Human Subjects of Biomedical and**
**Behavioral Research, 1979)** in the United States, they established the pillars of
research ethics with human subjects. The Belmont Report, in particular, identifies
three fundamental ethical principles: **Respect for People** (which implies the
autonomy and protection of those with diminished autonomy), **Beneficence**
(maximizing benefits and minimizing harms), and **Justice** (equitable distribution of
the burdens and benefits of research). Added to these are the specific codes of ethics
of professional associations such as the **American Psychological Association**
**(APA),** which provide detailed guidelines for psychological research, many of which
are relevant to player studies. Texts such as "Ethics in Research with Human
Participants" by **Sales and Folkman (2000)** offer practical guides for the application
of these principles.


A central aspect of ethical research is **Informed Consent** . Before participating in any


141


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


research or playtesting activity, players must be provided with clear, complete and
understandable information about the nature of the study, its purposes, the
procedures that will be followed (e.g. what they will have to do, how long it will last,
whether they will be recorded), the potential risks (physical, psychological, social,
privacy) and benefits (for themselves or for scientific knowledge/game improvement),
about their unconditional right to withdraw from participation at any time without any
penalty or negative consequences, and about how their personal and game data will
be collected, used, stored, protected and how long it will be retained. Consent must
be obtained voluntarily, without any form of coercion or undue influence (e.g.,
excessive reward offers that could compromise judgment). Usually, consent is
documented through a written form that the participant (or a parent/legal guardian in
the case of minors) signs after having had the opportunity to ask questions and
receive satisfactory answers. For **minors**, both the consent of the parent/guardian
and the consent of the minor is typically required, presented in language appropriate
to his or her age and ability to understand. Particular care must be taken when
working with other **vulnerable populations**, such as people with cognitive disabilities
or individuals in situations of dependency, ensuring that their consent is truly free and
informed.


Minimizing **risks and maximizing benefits (the principle of Beneficence and Non-**
**Maleficence)** are equally crucial. Researchers and designers must carefully weigh
all potential risks associated with participation. In games, these risks could include:
excessive **stress or frustration** due to high difficulty or punishing mechanics (e.g.
testing a particularly difficult boss fight); **motion sickness** or physical discomfort in
VR experiences (see Chapter 9.2); **exposure to potentially disturbing content**
(graphic violence, mature themes, psychological horror), especially if not anticipated
by the participant; **negative social interactions** or harassment in multiplayer
contexts; or **privacy risks** if personal or in-game data is handled inappropriately. It
is the researcher's responsibility to implement measures to mitigate these risks (e.g.,
allowing frequent breaks, offering options to interrupt the experience, providing
content warnings, moderating interactions). The benefits of research, whether for the
participant (e.g., fun, learning, a small compensation for time, the satisfaction of
contributing) or for society or scientific knowledge, must be carefully weighed against
the risks. If the risks outweigh the potential benefits, the study may not be ethically
justifiable.


**Managing data privacy, confidentiality, and anonymity** is critical, especially in
the age of big data and advanced telemetry. Players have the right to know what data
is collected about them (demographic data, questionnaire responses, audio/video
recordings of sessions, automatically tracked game data, biometric data), how this
data will be used (e.g. to improve the game, for scientific publications, for marketing
purposes), who will have access to it, how it will be protected from unauthorized
access and how long it will be stored. Confidentiality means that information that
could identify a participant (name, email address, face in recordings) will not be
disclosed outside the research team or will be de-identified in public reports.
Complete **anonymity**, where not even researchers can link data to a specific
participant, is more difficult to ensure in many game research contexts (especially if
you use existing game accounts or make video recordings), but you should take every
possible measure to protect the identity of participants. The data collected, especially
sensitive data such as biometric data or personal opinions expressed during
interviews, must be stored securely (e.g. encryption, secure servers) and used only
for the purposes for which consent was obtained. Compliance with data protection
regulations such as the **General Data Protection Regulation (GDPR)** in the


142


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


European Union is a legal requirement as well as an ethical one.


The principle of **Justice** requires that the selection of research participants be fair
and that the benefits and burdens of research be distributed fairly. Exploiting
vulnerable populations (e.g., students forced to participate for credit, economically
disadvantaged people being offered disproportionate compensation) or unjustifiably
excluding certain groups from the potential benefits of research should be avoided
(e.g., testing a game only on a sample of young white males, limiting the
generalizability and inclusiveness of the design).


**Debriefing** is an important practice, especially at the end of studies that may have
involved a certain level of stress, frustration, or if mild deception has been used
(which, as mentioned above, should be avoided unless it is absolutely necessary for
the objectives of the research, does not involve significant risks, and is followed by
full disclosure). During the debriefing, participants are given a full explanation of the
real purpose of the study, all their questions are answered, any misunderstandings
or false beliefs induced by participation are corrected, and support or resources are
offered if needed. The debriefing is also an opportunity to thank participants for their
input and to gather further informal feedback.


The **right to withdraw** from participation, at any time and for any reason, without any
penalty or loss of benefits to which one would otherwise be entitled (such as the
promised compensation for time already dedicated), must be clearly communicated
and fully respected.


Finally, in many institutional contexts (universities, research centers) and in some
large development companies, research protocols involving human participants must
be reviewed and approved by an **independent Institutional Review Board (IRB, or**
**Research Ethics Committee - REC)** prior to the start of the study. These committees
are tasked with assessing the ethical compliance of the research, the validity of
informed consent, the adequacy of participant protection measures, and the balance
between risks and benefits. Even when not formally requested, designers and
researchers should consider the principles that guide IRB reviews as a best practice.


In the specific context of gaming research, some **ethical best practices** include:
being particularly transparent with players about the **collection of telemetry data**
and its use, especially in live games; being aware of **potentially sensitive game**
**content** and providing appropriate warnings to participants prior to testing; and being
prepared to **handle frustration** or other negative emotions that may emerge during
the playtest, offering the possibility of taking breaks or interrupting the session;
ensure that any **fees** for participation are fair and non-coercive; and clarify the
**intellectual property of** any ideas, feedback, or content generated by players during
playtesting or research sessions, especially if they are not employees of the company
or have not signed specific non-disclosure agreements.


Strict adherence to sound ethical principles is not just a regulatory requirement or a
bureaucratic formality, but is the foundation of valid, respectful and socially
responsible scientific research in the field of game design. It builds trust with the
gaming community, protects the well-being of participants, and ensures that the
knowledge generated through research contributes positively to both the
development of better and more inclusive games, and a deeper scientific
understanding of the complex and fascinating phenomenon of the human gaming


143


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


experience.


**8.6. Conclusions: The Player as an Active and Informed Partner**
**in the Design and Gaming Research Process**


This chapter has outlined the crucial and irreplaceable role of playtesting and user
research within a scientific approach to game design. We've seen how iterative
playtesting, integrated into every stage of the development cycle, transforms design
from an activity based on mere assumptions to an empirically grounded process,
where hypotheses are constantly tested and refined through direct observation of the
player's experience. Best practices for conducting effective playtests, from clear goal
setting to targeted participant recruitment, from creating a non-judgmental
environment to active listening and rapid iteration, were highlighted as critical to
maximizing the value of this research method.


The exploration of advanced qualitative research methodologies, such as
phenomenological interviews to grasp the essence of lived experience, thematic
analysis and grounded theory to extract meaningful patterns from player accounts,
and virtual ethnographic studies or netnography to understand the complex cultural
and social dynamics of online gaming communities, has revealed the wealth of
insights that can emerge from an in-depth and contextualized investigation of the
"why" and "how" players interact and give meaning to their gaming experiences.


In parallel, quantitative methods analysis, with a focus on game analytics and
advanced telemetry, illustrated how collecting and analyzing large volumes of
behavioral data can provide a large-scale understanding of the "what" and "how
much" players do within the game. Defining meaningful Key Performance Indicators
(KPIs), behavioral player segmentation, predictive modeling, and systematic A/B
testing emerge as powerful tools for optimizing design, balancing systems, and
personalizing the experience, although they require careful privacy management and
critical reflection on data interpretation. Finally, the design and conduct of
experimental and quasi-experimental studies represent the pinnacle of scientific rigor
to establish cause-and-effect relationships between design variables and outcomes
on the player experience, providing a solid evidence base for design decisions.


The importance of a mixed-methods approach, strategically integrating the depth of
qualitative research with the breadth and generalizability of quantitative research,
was emphasized as the main route to a holistic and robust understanding.


However, beyond methodological sophistication, this chapter wanted to strongly
reiterate that any form of research involving players must be guided by mandatory
ethical principles. Detailed informed consent, protection of privacy and anonymity of
data (especially the most sensitive ones such as biometric or telemetry data),
minimization of risks and maximization of benefits, justice in the selection of
participants and protection of vulnerable individuals, together with the crucial role of
ethics committees and appropriate debriefing practices, they are not simple
obligations, but the ethical foundation of a responsible and respectful research
practice.


In conclusion, a scientific approach to game design does not see the player as a
simple "subject" of study or a passive "tester", but as an **active and informed**


144


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


**partner in the process of design and playful research** . Its experiences, feedback,
behaviors and even its physiological data, when collected and analyzed with
methodological rigor and ethical sensitivity, become an irreplaceable source of
knowledge that can drive innovation, improve the quality of games and deepen our
understanding of the complex and fascinating relationship between humans and
interactive systems. The scientifically oriented game designer is, therefore, also an
attentive researcher, capable of listening, observing, measuring and interpreting,
transforming the art of game creation into an increasingly informed, reflective and,
ultimately, human-centered discipline.


145


GAME DESIGN MANUAL

# **Chapter 9**

### **Adaptive Game Design,** **Artificial Intelligence and** **Technological Frontiers**


_In games, we learn by doing. We learn through experience, not through reading a_

_manual_


_**-Will Wright-**_


146


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


The game design landscape is characterized by a dynamic of continuous and
sometimes disruptive evolution, driven incessantly by the emergence of new
hardware platforms, by increasingly sophisticated and natural interaction
technologies, and by exponential advances in the field of artificial intelligence (AI)
and machine learning. These developments are not mere incremental upgrades, but
pave the way for radically new gaming paradigms, offering unprecedented
opportunities to create more adaptive gaming experiences, capable of dynamically
changing in response to the individual player's behavior, abilities, preferences, and
even physiological and emotional states; more **personalized**, tailored to the
individual; and more **immersive**, capable of engaging the senses and cognition in
ways previously unimaginable. However, these same innovations also pose new and
complex challenges for game designers, who must not only master the technical
specificities of each new context, but also deeply understand the psychological,
social and ethical implications of their use, in order to exploit technological potential
in a scientifically informed, creatively bold and ethically conscious way. This chapter
aims to critically analyze some of the most significant areas of this changing scenario.
We will start with a comparative analysis of the specific design for different
contemporary gaming platforms, considering the challenges posed by technological
convergence and market fragmentation. We will then explore the design for
innovative and natural interfaces, such as Virtual Reality (VR), Augmented Reality
(AR), Mixed Reality (MR), and voice, gesture, haptic and even olfactory control
systems. We will then delve into the complex social and behavioral dynamics that
characterize the design of advanced multiplayer systems, with a focus on promoting
cooperation and managing toxicity. Next, the crucial and multifaceted role of Artificial
Intelligence and Machine Learning in modern game design will be examined, from
the creation of more believable and adaptive behavioral AI, to dynamic difficulty
balancing systems based on sophisticated player models, to dynamic narrative
customization and AI-assisted or autonomous content generation. Finally, we will
push the frontiers of research, discussing emerging fields such as neurogaming, bioadaptive systems and affective computing, and reflecting on the profound ethical
implications of a design that aspires to become increasingly "empathetic" and
intimately connected with the human experience. The goal is to provide a concept
map to navigate this rapidly evolving territory, promoting game design that is both
cutting-edge technology and deeply human-centered.


**9.1. Platform-Specific and Cross-Platform Design: Technical, UX**
**and Market Challenges in the Era of Convergence and**
**Technological Fragmentation (Chandler, Montola)**


The hardware and software platform on which a video game is developed and, above
all, enjoyed by the end player, exerts a profound and pervasive influence on every
aspect of its design, from fundamental game mechanics to the user interface, from
art direction to business and distribution models. A thorough understanding of the
**affordances** (interaction possibilities), constraints (technical, ergonomic, economic)
and **user expectations** specific to each platform is a fundamental prerequisite for
effective and scientifically informed game design. **Heather Maxwell Chandler**
**(2009** ), in her "The Game Production Handbook", and more recently authors such as
**Olli Tapio Leino (formerly Montola)** (e.g. **Montola, Stenros, & Waern, 2009**,
"Pervasive Games: Theory and Design"), discussed the implications of designing for
different platforms, a topic made even more complex in the current era by
**technological convergence** (multifunction devices, cloud gaming that abstracts
from local hardware) and, paradoxically, by a continuous **fragmentation of the**


147


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


**market** (a myriad of mobile devices with different specifications, new consoles with
unique architectures, emerging VR/AR platforms).


The **Personal Computer (PC),** historically one of the most open and flexible
platforms, continues to offer designers considerable **computing power** (especially
with modern multi-core GPUs and CPUs) and extensive **hardware configurability** .
This allows the creation of games with extremely complex and detailed graphics (e.g.
_Alan Wake 2_, Remedy Entertainment, 2023, with its intensive use of path tracing),
deep physical or systemic simulations (e.g. _Kerbal Space Program_ ), and extensive
possibilities for customization of the graphics settings and control by the user. The
**keyboard and mouse** combination provides a **precise and versatile input** system,
ideal for genres such as high-APM (Actions Per Minute) real-time strategy (RTS)
such as _StarCraft II,_ competitive first-person shooters (FPS) that require pinpoint aim,
complex simulators (e.g. _Microsoft Flight Simulator_, Asobo Studio, 2020) and games
that require intensive management of menus, inventories or textual information
(many RPGs and management games). In addition, the relatively open nature of the
**PC platform** facilitates independent distribution (through stores such as Steam,
GOG, Epic Games Store, itch.io), access to a wide range of game engines and
development tools (many of which are open source or with accessible licenses), and
most importantly, a vibrant **modding culture**, where players themselves create and
share modifications, new content, or entire total conversions for their favorite games
(e.g. the longevity of _The Elder Scrolls V: Skyrim_ is largely due to its huge community
of modders). **PC users' expectations** are often high: they expect depth of gameplay,
advanced and customizable graphics options, support for high resolutions and
unlocked frame rates, modding possibilities, and a long product life, often with a
premium ("pay once, play forever") purchase model. Implications **for PC design**
include the ability to design complex mechanics and information-dense interfaces
(although clarity remains key), the need to ensure performance scalability across a
wide range of hardware configurations (a technically challenging task), and the
opportunity to embrace and support community creativity through modding tools.


Dedicated **game consoles** (such as Sony's PlayStation series, Microsoft's Xbox, and
Nintendo Switch) represent a different ecosystem. Their main feature, at least within
a single generation, is the **relatively standardized hardware** . This simplifies the
optimization process for developers, allowing them to "squeeze" the machine's
capabilities to the maximum and ensure a more consistent and predictable gaming
experience for all users of that platform. The **primary input is the gamepad**
**(controller),** an ergonomic device designed for two-handed use, with a limited
number of buttons, analog triggers, and analog sticks. This input constraint has a
significant impact on the design of the mechanics (favoring direct and accessible
actions) and the user interface (which must be comfortably navigable with a D-pad or
stick and readable from a distance, since the **typical environment** of use is the living
room, with the game displayed on a large TV screen). Consoles are often associated
with **"plug-and-play"** gaming experiences and a strong focus on visual and
cinematic impact (especially for first-party AAA titles) or **local social play ("couch**
**co-op" or multiplayer),** a distinctive feature of the Nintendo Switch with its
detachable Joy-Cons (e.g. _Mario Kart 8 Deluxe_ ). Console ecosystems **are typically**
**more closed** than PCs, with controlled distribution of games through proprietary
digital stores and, to a lesser extent, physical media, and with submission processes
that games must pass before they can be published. Console **users' expectations**
include high graphics quality and stable performance (smooth frame rates, reduced
loading times), ease of use, and a library of games that ranges from large AAA
productions with strong narrative and spectacular emphasis (e.g. _Horizon Forbidden_


148


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


_West_ on PlayStation 5) to innovative and creative indie titles. **Implications for**
**console design** include the need to optimize the UI for controller navigation and
remote viewing (larger text, more airy layouts), to design mechanics that adapt well
to the limitations and peculiarities of the gamepad (e.g. the use of aim assist systems
or "aim assist" in shooters), and to take advantage of console-specific features, such
as the advanced haptic feedback of the PS5 DualSense, motion sensors, or the social
features integrated into the platform.


Mobile **devices (smartphones and tablets)** have revolutionized the video game
market, introducing **portability** and **instant access** to the game anytime, anywhere.
The **primary input is the touchscreen**, which supports tap, double tap, drag, swipe,
pinch-to-zoom interactions, and is often combined with sensors such as the
accelerometer and gyroscope for motion-based controls of the device. **Mobile**
**gaming sessions are typically shorter and more intermittent** ("burst play") than
on PC and consoles, as gamers often engage in gaming during downtime,
commuting, or breaks. This has profound implications for the design of rhythm,
progression and save systems (which must be automatic and frequent). Mobile
**device hardware is extremely heterogeneous**, with a wide range of models with
different computing capabilities (CPU, GPU), amount of RAM, screen sizes and
resolutions, and operating system versions. This fragmentation presents a significant
challenge for developers in terms of optimization and testing. Mobile **monetization**
**models** are predominantly dominated by **free-to-play (F2P),** which offers the game
for free but includes **in-app purchases (IAPs)** for additional content, virtual currency,
progression accelerators, or cosmetics. Other models include **in-game advertising**
**(IGA),** premium **games** (one-time purchase), or **subscription services** (such as
Apple Arcade or Google Play Pass). Near-constant mobile connectivity facilitates
social gaming, push notifications, and frequent content updates ("live ops").
Implications **for** mobile design include the need for extremely simple, clear, and
touch-optimized user interfaces, with appropriately sized target elements to avoid
accidental touches; game mechanics suitable for short sessions and touch controls
(e.g., puzzle games such as _Monument Valley_, Ustwo Games, 2014; endless runners
such as _Subway Surfers_, Kiloo & SYBO Games, 2012; hypercasual games); a design
that considers frequent interruptions; careful management of device resources
(battery, mobile data consumption, memory); and, especially for F2P games, an
ethical and not overly intrusive integration of monetization models, balancing the
need to generate revenues with the offer of a fair and rewarding gaming experience
for all players, paying and non-paying (see Chapters 1.6 and 4.1).


**Cloud Gaming** (or game streaming), with services such as NVIDIA GeForce NOW,
Xbox Cloud Gaming (part of Game Pass Ultimate), and PlayStation Plus Premium
(for streaming PlayStation games), is an emerging trend that aims to decouple the
gaming experience from the user's on-premises hardware. In this model, the game
runs on powerful remote servers, and the video and audio streaming of the game
output is sent in real time to the user's device (which can be a low-powered PC,
smartphone, tablet, or smart TV), which in turn sends control inputs to the server.
The main advantage is the ability to access graphically intensive and computationally
demanding titles on a wide range of devices, provided you have a **fast, stable, and**
**low-latency internet connection** . Business models are typically subscriptionbased. The **implications for design** in cloud gaming are still being explored, but
some key challenges have already emerged. Input **lag (the** delay between the
player's action and on-screen display) is the primary concern. Even with optimized
connections, some latency is inevitable and can negatively impact the
responsiveness of the controls, especially for genres that require fast precision and


149


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


timing, such as fighting games, competitive shooters, or rhythm games. Designers
may need to consider **latency compensation techniques** (client or server side) or
design mechanics that are less sensitive to small delays. The **user interface** may
need to be **adaptive**, scaling or changing based on the type of device and input
scheme used for streaming. The **management of network bandwidth** by the game
and the streaming service is crucial to maintain an acceptable quality of experience
even in less than optimal network conditions. On the other hand, cloud gaming also
opens up **new theoretical possibilities**, such as creating games with extremely
complex persistent virtual worlds, large-scale physics simulations, or a number of
advanced AI NPCs that would be impossible to run on local consumer hardware, as
the main processing takes place on powerful cloud servers.


When designing a game that targets **multiple platforms (cross-platform**
**development)** or when **porting** an existing game to a new platform, it is essential to
take a scientific and methodical approach. This includes an **in-depth analysis of the**
**technical, ergonomic and cultural specificities of each target platform** ; an
**adaptive design of the user interface and control systems** that is not limited to a
simple 1:1 transposition (which often leads to suboptimal experiences), but that
redesigns the interaction to optimize it for each context (e.g. an interface designed
for mouse and keyboard on PC must be radically rethought for the needs of the user).
touch controls on mobile or for a gamepad on consoles); specific **and rigorous**
**testing on each target platform**, involving players who are regular users of that
platform and who have established expectations; and meticulous **performance**
**optimization** to ensure that the game runs smoothly and stably on all supported
hardware configurations within that platform. A "platform-aware" approach, which
recognizes, respects and exploits the uniqueness of each context of use, is essential
to maximize the quality of the player experience and the potential for success of the
game in the current technological landscape, characterized by a growing
convergence of services but also by a persistent and sometimes increasing
fragmentation of devices.


**9.2. Immersive and Natural Interfaces: Scientific Design for**
**Virtual (VR), Augmented (AR) and Mixed Reality (MR), Voice,**
**Gesture, Haptic and Olfactory Controls (Sherman & Craig,**
**Azuma, Billinghurst)**


Technological evolution is pushing game design beyond the boundaries of traditional
two-dimensional screens and established input methods (mouse, keyboard,
gamepad), paving the way for **immersive, natural interfaces** that aim to engage the
player in more direct, intuitive, and sensory-rich ways. Technologies such as Virtual
Reality (VR), Augmented Reality (AR), Mixed Reality (MR), together with voice,
gesture, haptic and even, experimentally, olfactory control systems, offer enormous
potential to transform the gaming experience. However, designing for these
innovative interfaces requires a deep scientific understanding of their technical
specificities, their implications for human perception and cognition, and the new
design challenges they entail.


150


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


**Virtual Reality (VR),** as defined by **Sherman and Craig (2003)** in "Understanding
Virtual Reality: Interface, Application, and Design", aims to fully immerse the user in
a three-dimensional computer-generated environment, sensorially isolating him from
the real world. This is typically achieved through the use of Head Mounted Display
HMDs that present independent stereoscopic images for each eye, creating a sense
of depth and three-dimensionality, and that track the movements of the player's head
(typically with 6 Degrees of Freedom, 6DoF: translation along the x, y, z axes and
rotation around the same axes, pitch, yaw, roll), allowing a 360-degree view of the
virtual environment. To this are often added manual controllers also tracked in 3D
space, which allow the player to interact with virtual objects in a more direct and
natural way (grabbing, throwing, manipulating). The main potential of VR for game
design lies in its ability to generate a very strong sense of **presence**, or the
psychological feeling of "being there", within the virtual environment (cf. **Slater, 2009** ;
**Witmer & Singer, 1998** ). This deep immersion can massively amplify the emotional
impact of an experience (e.g., the tension in a VR horror game like _Resident Evil 7:_
_Biohazard VR Mode_, Capcom, 2017, or the wonder of exploration in _Half Life: Alyx_,
Valve, 2020), the feeling of scale, and the possibility of more intuitive physical
interactions. However, designing for VR presents unique and significant challenges.
The best known is **motion sickness (motion sickness or Virtual Reality Sickness**
**VRS),** a motion sickness-like malaise that can be caused by discrepancies between
the visually perceived movement and the movement (or absence of it) of the physical
body, especially with regard to the vestibular system (cf. **LaViola, 2000** ; **Reason &**
**Brand, 1975** ). To mitigate VRS, designers must adopt specific strategies, such as
avoiding abrupt accelerations and decelerations of the virtual camera that are not
initiated by the player's actual physical movement; privilege, where possible, the real
physical movement of the player in the tracked space ("VR room scale"); o implement
carefully designed artificial locomotion mechanics, such as **teleportation** (the player
points and clicks to instantly move to another point, used in many VR games to avoid
continuous movement), **snap turning or "blink rotation"** to change the direction of
the camera without continuous rotational movement, or **gaze directed movement.**
It's crucial to maintain a **consistently high frame rate** (typically 90Hz or higher for
most modern HMDs) and **low end-to-end latency** (from player movement to image
refresh) to reduce sensory misalignment. Other techniques include providing a **static**
**visual reference** in the visual field (such as a virtual cockpit in a flight simulator, or a
stylized "virtual nose") or **dynamic visual field reduction (FOV) during artificial**
**movement** ("tunneling" or "vignetting" techniques, where the periphery of the image
is obscured or narrowed during movement to reduce conflicting vestibulo-ocular
stimulation). Research in this field is continuous (e.g. **Langbehn et al., 2018**, on the
optimization of locomotion techniques). The **user interface (UI) in VR** presents
another challenge: traditional 2D menus and HUDs (Heads Up Displays) projected
onto a virtual screen can break immersion or be difficult to read and interact with due
to depth and eye convergence. More effective solutions include **diegetic UIs**, i.e.
organically integrated into the game world (e.g. a virtual wristwatch that displays
information, a control panel on a spaceship, or physical objects in the world that act
as menus, as in _Job Simulator_, Owlchemy Labs, 2016), or **3D** spatial UIs (elements
that float in three-dimensional space and with which you interact with the hand
controllers). The readability of text in VR, the depth at which UI elements are
presented and the ease of interaction with them are crucial considerations (cf.
**Bowman & Kruijff, 2004**, "3D User Interfaces: Theory and Practice"). The **physical**
**interaction and affordances** of virtual objects must be carefully designed,
considering the absence (or limitation) of real haptic feedback. **Ergonomic comfort**
(HMD weight, cable management, session duration) and the **physical space**
**required** for room-scale experiences are other important practical factors. Finally,
**storytelling and directing in VR** require a rethinking of traditional techniques:


151


GAME DESIGN MANUAL


sudden camera cuts or forced camera movements can be extremely disorienting.
Designers must find new ways to guide the player's attention and tell stories in a 360degree medium, often relying on spatialized sound cues, ambient composition, or
attention-grabbing NPCs. **Mateer, 2017**, "Directing for Cinematic VR"). The scientific
evaluation of UX in VR requires specific methodologies to measure constructs such
as **presence** (e.g. through questionnaires such as the IPQ - Igroup Presence
Questionnaire, **Schubert, Friedmann, & Regenbrecht, 2001** ), **motion sickness**
(e.g. using the Simulator Sickness Questionnaire - SSQ, **Kennedy et al., 1993** ),
usability of 3D interactions and general comfort.


**Augmented Reality (AR),** unlike VR, does not aim to replace the real world, but to
**superimpose computer-generated information or virtual objects on the real**
**environment perceived by the user**, enriching their perception of reality. **Ronald**
**Azuma (1997),** in a classical definition, emphasized three key characteristics of AR:
the combination of real and virtual elements, real-time interactivity, and the threedimensional recording (alignment) of virtual objects in the real world. The **potential**
